# Overnight CTF writeup intake, 2026-10-10

The user supplied **114 PDFs** for sequential overnight processing. The fixed queue and resumable state are in ignored `work/overnight-ingest-2026-10-10/queue.json`; originals remain in ignored `ingest/`. Files are processed one at a time in filename order. The overnight cutoff for starting another PDF is **08:00 Europe/Copenhagen on 2026-10-10**. This is a progress record, not a claim that every queued document has been reviewed.

Each completed entry below identifies physical PDF page coverage, accepted changes, deliberately deferred material, primary sources, source fingerprint and actual verification. Review dates and source history are maintained here, outside worker skill instructions. Invented result examples are labelled illustrative. No target activity or Linux execution is implied by documentation or syntax checks.

## Completed documents

### 1. Bighead.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **35 physical PDF pages** were text-extracted and read. Source: Hack The Box, *Bighead*, prepared by MinatoTW, 5 May 2019, document D19.100.16. SHA-256: `3dc2cce84c00c737f4119402d9c892a2c49ceba3ee7cad96bc507faac07400fb`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `iconv` skill | 24â€“25 motivate encoding-aware conversion instead of deleting zeros from registry hex. Adds explicit encoding/BOM handling, non-ASCII interpretation, preserved terminators and partial-output failure handling. This procedure is a documented correction to the writeup, which does not invoke iconv itself. |
| Enriched `xxd` | 24â€“25 distinguish hex reconstruction from text decoding and hash identification, with a non-ASCII worked byte example. |
| Enriched `john` | 30â€“34 add KeePass conversion with a key file, byte-identical transfer, configuration-association limits, parser/build compatibility and independent confirmation of recovered candidates. |

The catalogue now contains **103 skills**. Historical footers for the two revised skills are retained below; the workers receive useful operational conditions and selected primary-documentation links.

**Corrections and interpretation.** Page 16 confuses a decimal address difference with hex notation: `0x0131FB40 - 0x0131FB28` is decimal 24, not hex 24. Its assertion that a character occupies two bits is also incorrect. No universal offset or exploit recipe was adopted. Page 22 labels default `zlib.compress` output as gzip and bases Content-Length on the uncompressed payload; these are not interchangeable representations or lengths. The page's proxy explanation was not generalized. Page 24's missing process-owner value does not establish SYSTEM identity; the later page 28 response is separate evidence. Page 26's printed Chisel command reverses the port roles described in its prose; its screenshot uses the intended 2222-to-2020 mapping. Existing Chisel instructions already teach these roles correctly. Page 31's screenshot explicitly removes the certutil envelope before decoding, a necessary step omitted from the short prose command. Existing base64 guidance already covers this. The archive password shown by John and the prose spelling on page 9 differ; retain tool evidence instead of copying the prose value.

**Reviewed without additional skills.** Existing Nmap, Gobuster, curl, Git, archive, base64, file, Chisel, SSH and SCP coverage handles the repeated portable tasks. Ghidra/Immunity/mona are demonstrated as GUI/Windows workflows; a headless Linux debugging skill would require separate source and fixture work. The application-specific memory-corruption, heap-spray and PHP inclusion chains, payload generation, firewall/DEP changes, Windows enumeration and final GUI KeePass entry remain source context. No such commands were executed or packaged as default utility workflows. Deliberately deferring these adaptations does not mean their PDF pages were unread.

**Primary-source review.** The [xxd manual](https://github.com/vim/vim/blob/master/runtime/doc/xxd.1) confirms permissive reverse parsing; the [Linux man-pages iconv manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man1/iconv.1.html) and [Unicode UTF/BOM FAQ](https://www.unicode.org/faq/utf_bom.html) support the conversion and byte-order distinctions. The [KeePass converter source](https://github.com/openwall/john/blob/bleeding-jumbo/src/keepass2john.c), [Jumbo format implementation](https://github.com/openwall/john/blob/bleeding-jumbo/src/keepass_fmt_plug.c) and [KeePass master-key documentation](https://keepass.info/help/base/keys.html) support key-file handling and compatibility conditions. The [Chisel remote syntax](https://github.com/jpillora/chisel#usage) resolves the port reversal. [Python zlib documentation](https://docs.python.org/3/library/zlib.html) distinguishes compression wrappers. Some GNU/Sourceware/Open Group manual URLs failed retrieval; the Linux man-pages project's published manual resolved iconv options.

**Checks actually run.** Three changed/new skill frontmatters passed the skill validator. Repository validation passed for 103 skill packages, 138 Markdown files, 185 local links and 195 Bash blocks (syntax only). Five synthetic local conversion cases passed with Windows Git's GNU libiconv 1.19 and xxd: non-ASCII UTF-16LE, preserved NUL terminator, BOM-aware UTF-16, rejected truncated input with partial output, and plain-hex reconstruction. An initial sandboxed version probe failed before fixture execution; the explicit local-fixture retry succeeded. John/KeePass recovery, Linux execution and worker outcomes were not tested. No target interaction occurred.

### 2. Bitlab.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **16 physical PDF pages** were read: text extraction covered the document and pages 3â€“16 were rendered and visually inspected, including the screenshot-only pages 12 and 16. Source: Hack The Box, *Bitlab*, prepared by MinatoTW, document D19.100.55; cover dated 8 January 2019. SHA-256: `8b1cc4cc656c9b18315a6ddbe1fc39de8b9d943a8e6669b1e9fb77021cbc4320`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| Enriched `git` | 7â€“9 and 14â€“16 distinguish hosting webhooks from local hooks, resolve configured hook paths, and interpret fast-forward, already-current, conflict and rebase outcomes before diagnosing deployment. |
| Enriched `sudo` | 14â€“16 add working-directory/repository context to assessment of a permitted `git pull`, including local configuration, hook execution identity and ownership checks. |
| Enriched `php` | 7 and 11â€“12 motivate a PostgreSQL diagnostic through an available CLI extension. The adapted example queries only database/role identity, uses existing libpq service/password configuration and distinguishes missing extension, connection failure and query failure. |

No new skill was needed; the catalogue remains **103**. The PHP example is a documented adaptation, not the writeup's credential-table dump and not an executed database test. Its output and the Git decision table are labelled illustrative.

**Corrections and interpretation.** The writeup overstates that `post-merge` runs whenever `git pull` is issued. Its successful example is a fast-forward; upstream source separates the already-current return path, and the hook manual excludes conflicted merges. Rebase is a different integration mode. The screenshots include making the local hook executable, which matters on Linux. Modern repository-ownership checks and effective configuration must be assessed before transferring the historical sudo result to another host. A GitLab Developer label alone does not establish the effective rights on every project/branch. Deployment of an HTML comment confirms content propagation, while the later PHP callback is separate execution evidence. A database running in Docker does not by itself prove that the caller lacks client binaries or establish the caller's network namespace. The screenshot's password happens to resemble base64; the observed SSH command uses the original value, so no decoding rule was imported. Replacing `jne` with `je` reverses a condition rather than universally bypassing a username check; the screenshot shows local `su` success, not proof that root SSH login is enabled.

**Reviewed without additional skills.** Existing Nmap, Gobuster, SSH/SCP, listener and binary-inspection coverage handles the repeated tool uses. The JavaScript bookmark and GitLab browser actions are application context, not a reason to execute copied code or add a generic Linux tool skill. Ghidra and x32dbg are demonstrated through GUI/Windows actions; no Linux command-line equivalent was asserted. The PHP reverse shell, privilege-escalation hook and credential replay were reviewed as source context but neither executed nor packaged as default workflows.

**Primary sources.** Reviewed [Git hook events](https://git-scm.com/docs/githooks), [pull modes](https://git-scm.com/docs/git-pull), [hook-path configuration](https://git-scm.com/docs/git-config#Documentation/git-config.txt-corehooksPath), [path resolution](https://git-scm.com/docs/git-rev-parse) and [merge implementation](https://github.com/git/git/blob/master/builtin/merge.c). The [PHP connection](https://www.php.net/manual/en/function.pg-connect.php), [query](https://www.php.net/manual/en/function.pg-query.php), [row-fetch](https://www.php.net/manual/en/function.pg-fetch-assoc.php) and [parameter-binding](https://www.php.net/manual/en/function.pg-query-params.php) manuals were checked alongside [libpq service configuration](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/libpq-pgservice.html) and [password files](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/libpq-pgpass.html).

**Checks actually run.** All three skill validators passed. Repository validation passed for 103 packages, 138 Markdown files, 186 local links and 197 Bash blocks (syntax only); `git diff --check` passed. A read-only Git 2.55.0.windows.5 invocation with a temporary command-line `core.hooksPath=example-hooks` override resolved `example-hooks/post-merge`; no persistent config, hooks, fetch, merge or commit was performed. PHP was not found on PATH, so the PHP example was documentation-reviewed but not linted or executed. There was no PostgreSQL connection, target interaction, Linux execution or worker evaluation. The scheduled environment had a different writable root; repository writes/checks succeeded using the authorized sandbox escalation.

### 3. Bizness.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **14 physical PDF pages** were read. Pages 2â€“4, 8â€“9 and 11â€“14 were additionally rendered and visually inspected for commands, screenshots, source excerpts and hash conversion. Source: Hack The Box, *Bizness*, prepared and authored by C4rm3l0, dated 27 February 2024, document D24.100.272. SHA-256: `e619ec04b67c53cdaccbf4bdddcfbfd4b48fbc7fab91d870cf05c1f9c03ded1f`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `feroxbuster` | 3â€“4 motivate path discovery and checking a parent route despite 404 child paths. Primary documentation supports a bounded, nonrecursive first pass, separate link-extraction control, per-directory rate limits, JSON evidence and resume/TLS conditions. |
| New `derby-ij` | 7â€“9 motivate inspection of an embedded application database with ij. The portable adaptation uses a consistent working copy, read-only SQL, schema-qualified metadata and bounded column selection, with display/lock/driver recovery decisions. |
| Enriched `hashcat` | 10â€“13 support verifying legacy OFBiz salt-before-password SHA-1 and converting unpadded base64url. The converter preserves UTF-8 salt bytes as hex and requires `--hex-salt` for mode 120, rejects malformed/noncanonical input and refuses to overwrite an existing output. |

The catalogue now contains **105 independently installable skills**. The new decision examples are labelled illustrative. The Hashcat helper remains an inline conversion in the skill; no runtime dependency on another skill or maintenance file was added. The earlier Hashcat source footer is retained below.

**Corrections and interpretation.** The many 404 child paths on page 3 do not themselves prove exposed content; the later parent/login response is separate evidence. The source's use of `-k` disables certificate validation and does not justify making it a default. Modern Feroxbuster link extraction and recursion are separate controls; `--depth 0` is unlimited, and rate limits apply per directory. The documentation's older threads/connection wording was reconciled against the current CLI/parser rather than generalized into an overall rate guarantee. Derby can run embedded or as a network server; the case's absence of a listening database port is not a product-wide restriction. Page 8's `tar cvf` makes an archive without compression, and an ordinary copy of a running database is not a guaranteed consistent backup. `READONLY ON` governs the SQL connection and does not promise zero filesystem writes while booting/recovering a working copy. Page 7's configured default hash algorithm alone does not identify all stored records. The legacy source hashes salt bytes before password bytes; base64url spelling, padding and digest length matter. The published digest converts to `b8fd3f41a541a435857a8f3e751cc3a91c174362`, with literal salt `d` represented as hex `64` by the new procedure. General SHA-1 collision weakness does not by itself explain password recovery; candidate coverage and verification cost still matter. A recovered database value and successful local `su` in the historical case do not establish other accounts' current authentication behavior.

**Reviewed without additional skills.** Nmap, curl, tar, base64, tcpdump, ysoserial and existing configuration/source inspection guidance already cover the repeated portable decisions. The OFBiz exploit delivery, reverse callback, global Java alternative changes, raw netcat transfer and root credential replay were read as case context; no such commands were executed or added as default tool workflows. A product-specific exploitation skill and a general Derby administration/backup procedure were deliberately left outside this bounded intake; all related pages were nevertheless read.

**Primary sources.** Feroxbuster's [CLI reference](https://epi052.github.io/feroxbuster-docs/configuration/command-line/), [option parser](https://github.com/epi052/feroxbuster/blob/main/src/parser.rs) and [thread-limit explanation](https://epi052.github.io/feroxbuster-docs/configuration/threads-and-limits/) were checked for expansion, rate, output, TLS and resume semantics. Derby's [connection command](https://db.apache.org/derby/docs/10.13/tools/rtoolsijcomref22318.html), [driver selection](https://db.apache.org/derby/docs/10.16/tools/ttoolsij97656.html), [metadata listing](https://db.apache.org/derby/docs/10.4/tools/rtoolsijcomrefshow.html), [Describe](https://db.apache.org/derby/docs/10.4/tools/rtoolsijcomrefdescribe.html), [Readonly](https://db.apache.org/derby/docs/10.4/tools/rtoolsijcomref61092.html), [MaximumDisplayWidth](https://db.apache.org/derby/docs/10.4/tools/rtoolsijcomref12281.html), [FETCH FIRST](https://db.apache.org/derby/docs/10.16/ref/rrefsqljoffsetfetch.html), [backup overview](https://db.apache.org/derby/docs/10.9/adminguide/cadminbckupdb.html) and [offline-copy requirements](https://db.apache.org/derby/docs/10.9/adminguide/cadminhubbkup12677.html) support the inspection procedure. The historical [OFBiz HashCrypt source](https://github.com/apache/ofbiz-framework/blob/release18.12/framework/base/src/main/java/org/apache/ofbiz/base/crypto/HashCrypt.java), [Hashcat mode 120 implementation](https://github.com/hashcat/hashcat/blob/master/src/modules/module_00120.c), [Hashcat CLI reference](https://hashcat.net/wiki/doku.php?id=hashcat) and [Python base64 API](https://docs.python.org/3/library/base64.html) support the conversion and explicit hex-salt handling. These are documentation/source reviews, not installed-version observations.

**Checks actually run.** All three changed/new skill validators passed. Repository validation passed for **105 packages, 140 Markdown files, 188 local links and 199 Bash blocks**, with Bash syntax parsed but commands not executed. Eight Python 3.12 fixtures passed on Windows using the converter extracted from the actual skill: the published digest encoding, synthetic UTF-8/delimiter salt, rejection of wrong algorithm, short digest, wrong alphabet, noncanonical padding bits and multiple records, plus preservation of an existing output. These are local representation checks without password guessing. The first whitespace check found an extra blank line at the Hashcat file's end; it was removed and the final `git diff --check` passed. Feroxbuster discovery, Java/Derby queries, Hashcat backend execution, Linux behavior and agent performance remain untested. No target interaction, authentication, installation, configuration changes, commits or pushes occurred.

### 4. Blackfield.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **16 physical PDF pages** were read. Pages **3â€“16** were also rendered and visually inspected, including page 12's backup/version screenshots and page 16's distinct cached-logon/LSA-secret results. Source: Hack The Box, *Blackfield*, prepared by cube0x0, machine author aas, dated 28 September 2020, document D20.100.89. SHA-256: `17b0e54f81b17bce0c4efc9c17c0c2a76245080677aac431b26eb59cbd2dad94`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `pypykatz` | 7â€“9 motivate offline inspection of an already acquired LSASS process dump. The skill scopes MSV parsing, retains structured session/account associations, distinguishes missing material from parser failures and avoids independently sorting usernames and hashes. |
| Enriched `ldapsearch` | 8â€“9 motivate a targeted policy query rather than grepping a directory-wide transcript. The adaptation reads domain defaults, explicitly requests the selected user's resultant PSO and inspects its settings before interpreting a zero threshold. |
| Enriched `impacket-secretsdump` | 13â€“16 distinguish the existing NTDS/SYSTEM procedure from SECURITY/SYSTEM parsing. Added the latter's cached-domain-logon and LSA-secret workflow, output-file conditions and artifact-type interpretation. |

The catalogue now contains **106 skills**. Earlier source footers from the two revised entrypoints are retained below. The existing offline-NTDS reference already handles the writeup's NTDS task and was left intact. All newly invented result examples are labelled illustrative.

**Corrections and interpretation.** The synopsis skips the password-reset stage between the initial support identity and access to the forensic share; pages 6â€“7 supply that missing step. Profile-directory names are candidate usernames, and a whitespace-split SMB listing can include headers, dot entries or truncated names; existing LDAP/GetNPUsers guidance already distinguishes candidates from verified principals. The no-preauthentication requests shown on page 4 are not password guesses. Its grep filter discards error context, which should be preserved. Page 5 mentions a rockyou run, while the displayed John command has no explicit wordlist; no candidate-list coverage was inferred from the prose. The BloodHound screenshot identifies a ForceChangePassword relationship; this is a reset operation, not evidence of merely knowing the old password. Existing collector guidance already handles legacy/CE differences and limits of the alternate DNS option.

Page 8's parser output contains NT/SHA values, not a demonstrated plaintext password. Separately sorting names and NT values on page 9 loses their associations and creates unnecessary ambiguity. The observed default `lockoutThreshold: 0` does not establish every user's effective fine-grained policy or unlimited application retries. Modern OpenLDAP guidance uses an explicit URI, authenticated transport and valid presence filter rather than importing the historical `-h`/`-p`, command-line password and standalone `*` filter. A recovered value remains snapshot evidence until a separate authorised validation establishes its current use. The denied file copy and the note on page 10 suggest encryption in that case; access denial alone does not prove EFS. The backup versions shown in prose and screenshots differ, so a recovery procedure must select the actual acquired version rather than copy the date. Page 16 demonstrates that SAM account records, cached domain verifiers and LSA secrets are distinct results. A machine's present role alone also does not prove which historical database artifacts may exist in its acquired files.

**Reviewed without additional skills.** Existing Nmap, Samba smbclient, GetNPUsers, John, BloodHound.py, Evil-WinRM and offline-NTDS instructions cover the repeated useful tasks. The rpcclient reset, CrackMapExec credential trials, Samba service/account configuration, WBAdmin/DiskShadow/SeBackupPrivilege acquisition and remote WMI/PSExec execution were read as case context. No such actions were performed. A standalone rpcclient skill for controlled account changes and Windows backup/EFS administration would need a separately scoped procedure and validation; they were deliberately deferred instead of turning a destructive historical chain into a default Linux utility workflow. This is a material-scope decision, not partial page coverage.

**Primary sources.** Reviewed Pypykatz's [project scope](https://github.com/skelsec/pypykatz), [LSA CLI and output handling](https://github.com/skelsec/pypykatz/blob/main/pypykatz/lsadecryptor/cmdhelper.py), [MSV/session structure](https://github.com/skelsec/pypykatz/blob/main/pypykatz/lsadecryptor/packages/msv/decryptor.py) and [core parser/result serialization](https://github.com/skelsec/pypykatz/blob/main/pypykatz/pypykatz.py). Impacket's [secretsdump CLI](https://github.com/fortra/impacket/blob/master/examples/secretsdump.py) and [LSA/cached-logon implementation](https://github.com/fortra/impacket/blob/master/impacket/examples/secretsdump.py) confirm LOCAL behavior, SECURITY parsing and conditional `.cached`/`.secrets` exports. Microsoft's [resultant password policy](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/powershell/module/activedirectory/get-aduserresultantpasswordpolicy), [resultant-PSO attribute](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/adschema/a-msds-resultantpso), [fine-grained policy guidance](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-server/identity/ad-ds/get-started/adac/fine-grained-password-policies) and [lockout-threshold semantics](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/previous-versions/windows/it-pro/windows-10/security/threat-protection/security-policy-settings/account-lockout-threshold) support the policy interpretation. Source copies and extracted text/renders remain in ignored work storage.

**Checks actually run.** Three affected skill validators passed. Repository checks passed for **106 packages, 141 Markdown files, 189 local links and 203 Bash blocks** (syntax only), and `git diff --check` passed. No LSASS dump, registry hive or directory query was executed, and no Linux or agent evaluation occurred. This host's malware protection blocked reading the downloaded secretsdump CLI source copy; the same official source was reviewed through the web reader without changing protection, exclusions or host configuration. The other saved sources were inspected as text only. No target scans, authentication, tool installation, credential replay, system changes, input deletion, commits or pushes occurred.

This adaptation teaches chain evidence, build diagnostics and package inspection rather than replaying the writeup's credential capture or privilege-escalation chain. No historical source footer was removed from these files in this pass; sudo's earlier trail was already retained with Bitlab.

**Corrections and interpretation.** Page 2's prose calls the web service Apache, while its scan reports Werkzeug. Its `10.10.11.435` RPC address is malformed. A latest block number of 12 is a height: the range 0â€“12 includes genesis and the writeup's 0â€“11 loop omits the observed tip. It also appends transaction hashes and greps surrounding display lines, so the generated list is not a reliable complete inventory. The new workflow selects a bounded block, extracts structured transaction data and preserves block hashes; no all-chain completeness is asserted. A successful block-number method does not establish permission for another RPC method. The case's application cookie and custom token header are distinct from an Ethereum Engine JWT secret.

The callback pages establish a request associated with the submitted payload; role/identity claims require the separate response and application observations. Decoding JWT fields does not itself verify a signature. The writeup's network-facing Flask debug proxy is not a portable default adapter. Contract creation input contains deployment code and constructor data; readable strings alone do not establish the ABI split, successful execution or present application state. The synopsis names Users.sol, while later pages discuss Chat.sol and Database.sol, so names were not normalised into a universal contract layout. Page 14 calls `/home/keira/user.txt` a root flag; it is the user path shown in the session.

`RUST_LOG=trace` exposes instrumented application events, not every system call. A log showing an unqualified Git helper is not sufficient by itself to prove exploitable elevated PATH resolution, and a newly downloaded local binary does not establish the restricted target binary's version or behavior. For packages, makepkg consumes PKGBUILD and builds the archive; pacman installs it. Package `post_install` scriptlets and ALPM post-transaction hooks are separate mechanisms. The dummy tar archive is not referenced by the displayed empty `source=()` recipe and is not established as necessary. `--skipinteg` bypasses checks and was not imported. The final screenshot shows both set-user-ID and set-group-ID effects; these were treated as historical evidence rather than packaged as a system-modification procedure.

**Reviewed without additional skills.** Existing Nmap, Python HTTP server, curl, base64/JWT handling, jq, xxd, SSH, Bash and tar coverage handles the repeated portable tasks. The XSS/token capture, application-specific forwarding proxy, credential replay, PATH replacement, callback payload, custom privilege-escalation package and system permission changes were deliberately not executed or adopted as default utility workflows. A full makepkg authoring skill, generic smart-contract ABI reversal and production RPC adapter implementation require their own supported tasks and fixtures; this intake does not claim them.

**Primary sources.** Checked Foundry's [block CLI](https://getfoundry.sh/reference/cast/block/), [block-number CLI](https://getfoundry.sh/reference/cast/block-number/), [transaction CLI](https://getfoundry.sh/cast/reference/cast-tx/), [receipt CLI](https://getfoundry.sh/reference/cast/receipt/), [ABI decoding CLI](https://getfoundry.sh/reference/cast/decode-abi/), [Forge build CLI](https://getfoundry.sh/reference/forge/build/), [compiler configuration](https://getfoundry.sh/reference/config/solidity-compiler) and [logging guide](https://github.com/foundry-rs/foundry/blob/master/docs/dev/debugging.md). Several Foundry pages failed the web reader's content-type handling or redirected to generic/older pages; current generated CLI help was retrieved directly and extracted from its HTML into ignored source files. [Ethereum JSON-RPC](https://ethereum.org/en/developers/docs/apis/json-rpc/) and the [Solidity ABI specification](https://docs.soliditylang.org/en/latest/abi-spec.html) establish block/transaction/receipt and encoding semantics. Arch's [pacman manual](https://man.archlinux.org/man/pacman.8.en), [PKGBUILD manual](https://man.archlinux.org/man/PKGBUILD.5.en), [ALPM hook manual](https://man.archlinux.org/man/alpm-hooks.5.en) and [libarchive bsdtar manual](https://github.com/libarchive/libarchive/blob/master/tar/bsdtar.1) support package-query and archive-inspection behavior. Sudo's existing policy source covers effective environment conditions.

**Checks actually run.** All four affected skill validators passed. Repository checks passed for **109 packages, 144 Markdown files, 192 local links and 211 Bash blocks** (syntax only); final `git diff --check` passed. A synthetic archive fixture using Windows **bsdtar/libarchive 3.8.8** verified `.INSTALL` listing, exact member bytes to stdout without filesystem extraction, and a failing missing-member request. The inert fixture's script text was never sourced or executed. jq was not available on PATH, so its extraction pipeline was reviewed but not run. Foundry RPC/builds, pacman queries/installations, Linux execution and agent outcomes were not tested. No target interaction, authentication, tool installation, system configuration changes, input deletion, commits or pushes occurred.

### 7. Blurry.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **22 physical PDF pages** were read. Pages **2â€“8, 10â€“11, 13, 17 and 19â€“21** were rendered and visually inspected, including the ClearML project/settings screenshots and the severity/output evidence. Source: Hack The Box, *Blurry*, prepared by and authored by C4rm3l0, dated 17 February 2024, document D24.100.306. SHA-256: `19ba2f6b4e7d08218ffc7ed681cd196fcea4a047f56fea0d33d24784af3eb6d9`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `fickling` | 18â€“21 motivate static pickle inspection, selecting raw metadata from the actual model format, retaining scanner failures and rejecting conclusions based on a single severity comparison. Added a separate symbolic-decompilation workflow and limits of a clean classification. |
| New `httpie` | 14â€“17 motivate a precise JSON task lookup and interpretation of the API envelope. Added offline construction, typed request items, unattended stdin behavior, explicit response output and HTTP/application status separation. |

The catalogue now contains **112 independent skills**. Existing skills were inspected before adding these distinct tools. Both new skills label invented result examples as illustrative. Neither is a replay of the writeup's payload chain.

**Corrections and interpretation.** Pages 2â€“3 vary an HTTP Host header; the result is virtual-host comparison, not evidence that public DNS contains those names. A 400 response from one candidate is still a response distinction worth examining. Existing Wfuzz guidance already teaches baseline controls, word-count filters and individual confirmation. Pages 4â€“8 supply the review-tag/project context and show a server version; that server version alone does not establish the SDK or model-loader version. The document's motivation says the disclosures lack CVEs, while its synopsis names them; these are historical source statements, not a current vulnerability inventory or patch assessment.

Pages 9â€“17 distinguish task metadata, artifact bytes and deserialization. The historical ClearML SDK's `get()` selects behavior using the artifact type and can fall back to a local Path. The first Path result from a `custom` artifact does not itself demonstrate double serialization; the later explicit pickle-type/Path serialization example is different. A dictionary check after deserialization cannot prevent side effects that occurred while loading. The upload transformation shown in the source is SDK logic, so attributing it solely to the remote backend is misleading. The shown artifact-metadata update is an authenticated API request for the created task; by itself it does not demonstrate an unauthenticated or cross-user fileserver write. The response also distinguishes requested API version 2.27 from actual endpoint version 2.10. An update count of one is distinct from the later worker/log/session evidence.

Pages 18â€“21 show a wrapper that rejects only `OVERTLY_MALICIOUS`, yet the accepted candidate is explicitly `LIKELY_UNSAFE`. This is an acceptance-policy gap, not evidence that the scanner declared it safe. Scanner exit status, report validity and all relevant records must survive the wrapper. Modern Fickling CLI source rejects findings above `LIKELY_SAFE`, and JSON output appends a result for each stacked pickle; a stale file or single-object parser could distort coverage. The ordinary CLI decompiles symbolically, whereas the similarly named library loading functions can actually deserialize. Model accuracy and an apparently successful evaluation do not prove that loading lacked side effects.

The writeup overgeneralises old `.pt` files as tar and newer `.pth` files as ZIP. Inspect actual formats: PyTorch has multiple historical representations, and extensions do not select them. Current PyTorch documentation also changes the context of the historical `torch.load` call: since 2.6 the default is weights-only when no pickle module is supplied, with documented remaining risks. No assertion is made that current tools reproduce the historical bypass. The failed web redirects for the rendered serialization page were resolved by reading the project's documentation source.

**Reviewed without additional skills.** Existing Nmap, Wfuzz, SSH, netcat, jq, Bash, sudo and archive guidance covers the repeated surrounding tasks. ClearML initialization, credentials, task creation, artifact type mutation, custom Python exploit scripts, model injection, privileged evaluation and callback/credential replay were read but not executed or imported as default utility instructions. A ClearML CLI skill needs a separately supported task beyond initializing a historical SDK and running a case-specific Python program. The installed-tool/version and live model-fixture questions remain for later Linux evaluation. No source page was skipped or partially counted as complete.

**Primary sources.** Reviewed Fickling's [project guide](https://github.com/trailofbits/fickling), [CLI](https://github.com/trailofbits/fickling/blob/master/fickling/cli.py), [analysis and report writer](https://github.com/trailofbits/fickling/blob/master/fickling/analysis.py), [loader/scan distinction](https://github.com/trailofbits/fickling/blob/master/fickling/loader.py) and [exit constants](https://github.com/trailofbits/fickling/blob/master/fickling/constants.py). The [PyTorch serialization documentation source](https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/blob/main/docs/source/notes/serialization.md) supports format and weights-only conditions. HTTPie's [CLI reference](https://httpie.io/docs/cli), [argument parser](https://github.com/httpie/cli/blob/master/httpie/cli/argparser.py) and [response/status handling](https://github.com/httpie/cli/blob/master/httpie/core.py) support request construction, stdin, netrc and error handling. ClearML's versioned [1.13.1 artifact implementation](https://github.com/clearml/clearml/blob/v1.13.1/clearml/binding/artifacts.py) supports the distinction between custom-file Paths, serialization and type-based loading. Two GNU Bash web pages timed out during an optional pipeline cross-check; no new Bash semantics were accepted from those failed retrievals.

**Checks actually run.** Both new skills passed the frontmatter validator. Repository checks passed for **112 packages, 147 Markdown files, 195 local links and 216 Bash blocks**, with command examples parsed only. Final `git diff --check` passed. Fickling and HTTPie were not found on PATH; no installation, model parsing/loading, request generation with HTTPie, target requests, authentication, Linux fixture or agent evaluation occurred. Original PDFs were retained, and no configuration changes, commits or pushes were made. Extracted text and fourteen page renders remain in ignored work storage.

|---|---|
| New `dive` | 7â€“11 motivate offline archive inspection, layer/file view controls, selecting an earlier file version and recovering only observed members. Added headless JSON interpretation, whiteout semantics and limits of efficiency checks. |

**Corrections and interpretation.** Page 5's ffuf screenshot includes a word-count filter absent from the prose command; the observed negative response must establish that threshold, and varying Host is not DNS discovery. Existing ffuf coverage already handles these controls. A registration HTTP 500 alone does not explain the cause or prove rollback.

On pages 7â€“8, loading the image imports it into a daemon; running it executes its configured workload/entrypoint behavior. Neither is required to inspect the acquired archive. Dive's archive source reads the file directly. `Ctrl+U` toggles unmodified entries rather than specifically highlighting modified ones, and the active pane matters for shortcuts. The preceding layer may not hold a removed file's bytes: locate the most recent layer that actually added or modified that path. Layers are changesets, and plain extraction of all layers is not whiteout-aware reconstruction. Modern Dive JSON export provides structured metadata/file lists, but not recovered content; current source returns from JSON export before the CI-rule branch. No generic claim is made about every old Dive release's JSON schema or newer archive compatibility.

The SQLite screenshot's entire display row is not the password input; preserve the complete `$1$` field separately. Historical database/source contents identify leads, not current online credential validity. On pages 12â€“13, displaying a submitted name in email alone would not prove SSTI; the later arithmetic result and separate command-result screenshot provide the case's additional evidence. Those application-specific payloads were not transferred into utility instructions.

The browser log excerpt on pages 15â€“16 is escaped data with contextual content and an armored secret-key field. Searching printable strings locates a candidate record but does not establish completeness, freshness or correct unescaping. Global replacement of escape sequences is not a general decoding method. The key passphrase and online account-recovery token serve different purposes. Pages 17â€“20 show database access, recovery flow and a stored root credential followed by an OS identity check; this does not by itself establish a current Passbolt product vulnerability or a universally reusable recovery URL. No live credentials, tokens or key contents were copied into skills.

**Reviewed without additional skills.** Existing Nmap, ffuf, SQLite, MySQL, strings, grep, curl, SSH and Python HTTP-server guidance covers the repeated surrounding tasks. Broad credential spraying, service registration, email-triggered SSTI, callback execution, browser extension installation, database token retrieval and online account takeover were read as context and deliberately not executed or packaged. A separate browser-storage/LevelDB parser workflow and Passbolt recovery administration need their own supported tasks and fixtures; `strings | grep` alone does not justify those claims. All source pages were covered.

**Primary sources.** Checked Dive's [project controls/source selection](https://github.com/wagoodman/dive), [archive reader](https://github.com/wagoodman/dive/blob/main/dive/image/docker/image_archive.go), [root control flow](https://github.com/wagoodman/dive/blob/main/cmd/dive/cli/internal/command/root.go), [JSON option](https://github.com/wagoodman/dive/blob/main/cmd/dive/cli/internal/options/export.go) and [export schema](https://github.com/wagoodman/dive/blob/main/cmd/dive/cli/internal/command/export/export.go). [OCI layer rules](https://github.com/opencontainers/image-spec/blob/main/layer.md) establish changesets and explicit/opaque whiteouts. Docker's [save](https://docs.docker.com/reference/cli/docker/image/save/), [load](https://docs.docker.com/reference/cli/docker/image/load/) and [export](https://docs.docker.com/reference/cli/docker/container/export/) references support archive boundaries. Hashcat's [mode 500 parser](https://github.com/hashcat/hashcat/blob/master/src/modules/module_00500.c), Jumbo's [OpenPGP converter](https://github.com/openwall/john/blob/bleeding-jumbo/src/gpg2john.c) and [gpg format](https://github.com/openwall/john/blob/bleeding-jumbo/src/gpg_fmt_plug.c) support record and format handling. Guessed Dive configuration/CLI paths were unavailable; the actual paths were resolved through the upstream repository tree before review.

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **17 physical PDF pages** were read. Source: Hack The Box, *Book*, prepared by MinatoTW, machine author MrR3boot, cover dated 7 July 2020, document D20.100.80. | Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `logrotate` | 15â€“17 motivate identifying the actual privileged invocation and distinguishing a writable file from pathname replacement. Added a debug-only diagnostic workflow, state/configuration comparison, skip interpretation and rotation-versus-hook identity. |
| Enriched `mysql` | 8â€“9 motivate checking schema, SQL mode and collation before attributing truncation or an identity collision to the server. Added read-only SQL metadata inspection and session-specific warning interpretation. The PDF demonstrates this through a web application, not a mysql CLI session; the CLI adaptation was checked against primary documentation. |

The catalogue now contains **114 independent skills**. MySQL's earlier source/test footer is retained below instead of loading it into every worker session.

**Corrections and interpretation.** Pages 3â€“7 cover service and web discovery. The page 4 Gobuster screenshot shows directory redirects (301), while the narrative reports a forbidden `/docs` resource; these are different observations, and a redirect alone does not establish the eventual resource status. An account whose username is `admin` is not thereby an administrator. A failed login does not prove the application's account-selection query. An acknowledged upload without a retrievable location does not prove code execution.

Pages 8â€“9 show the application accepting a long value and displaying a shortened one. Frontend constraints are not a database schema measurement. The blanket claim that MySQL ignores trailing spaces in all database operations is too broad: storage/retrieval, comparison and input conversion require separate evidence. No database insertion, account collision or mode change was reproduced. The skill uses the observed table's metadata and matching server documentation to resolve these questions instead of assuming the historical application's behavior applies elsewhere.

Pages 10â€“13 progress from a fetched image to a JavaScript-generated PDF result and then local-file content. A resource request alone does not prove script execution; JavaScript running inside a PDF renderer does not by itself establish an OS shell. The observed PhantomJS user agent is a case-specific lead, not proof every PDF generator has the same engine or permissions. Pages 13â€“14 show base64 transport of a private key and a later SSH session. Decode completion and key parsing matter; the source's `tee` would also expose the key on stdout. Existing base64 and SSH-key guidance already covers these distinctions.

The process screenshot on page 16 identifies a root invocation of `/usr/sbin/logrotate -f /root/log.cfg`. Neither the default system configuration nor a daily schedule can be inferred from that command. Page 17's race changes a directory pathname and shows a privileged shell result. This is not evidence that every writable log, installed version or configuration is exploitable. The instruction that completion files run on every sign-in also overstates a behavior dependent on Bash startup and completion configuration. The new skill supports inspection of the concrete conditions without adopting the race or callback procedure.

**Reviewed without additional skills.** Existing Nmap, Gobuster, base64, SSH/ssh-keygen, pspy, linpeas and Bash coverage handles the repeated utility tasks. Burp-driven account manipulation, stored PDF-renderer JavaScript/local-file access, private-key extraction, downloaded enumeration-script execution, Logrotten compilation/race invocation and callback execution were read as context and deliberately not executed or packaged. A separate PhantomJS or Logrotten skill would need an independently supported task and fixture; this case alone was not treated as that validation. No source pages were left unread.

**Primary sources.** Logrotate's [manual](https://github.com/logrotate/logrotate/blob/main/logrotate.8.in) and [implementation](https://github.com/logrotate/logrotate/blob/main/logrotate.c) were checked for debug behavior, state handling, script execution and permission/link checks. In particular, `runScript()` returns before execution in debug mode. MySQL's [string types](https://dev.mysql.com/doc/refman/8.4/en/char.html), [SQL modes](https://dev.mysql.com/doc/refman/8.4/en/sql-mode.html), [SHOW COLUMNS](https://dev.mysql.com/doc/refman/8.4/en/show-columns.html) and [SHOW WARNINGS](https://dev.mysql.com/doc/refman/8.4/en/show-warnings.html) support the metadata and interpretation changes. The [bash-completion project](https://github.com/scop/bash-completion) documents startup integration and conditional loading. These current sources qualify the historical case; they do not establish Book's exact server package or patches.

**Checks actually run.** Both affected skill validators passed. Repository checks passed for **114 packages, 149 Markdown files, 197 local links and 221 Bash blocks**, syntax only; `git diff --check` passed. Logrotate and mysql were not found on PATH. The catalogue now contains **116 independent skills**. Find's previous source/test footer is retained below. Commands in the two new skills are portable adaptations supported by upstream references; they were not executed against the machine or a Linux fixture.

**Corrections and interpretation.** On page 2, the scan output identifies nginx 1.18.0, whereas the prose says Apache 2.4.41. The shown `sudo echo ... >> /etc/hosts` does not elevate the invoking shell's redirection. Pages 3â€“9 distinguish an injected HTML result, a blocked inline script and the later same-origin uploaded-script alert. A 304 response is not the upload's content; the separate curl result confirms the retrieved bytes. The supplied Content-Type is not a file-integrity check.

Pages 10â€“17 describe application-specific authenticated script requests, basket identifiers and download handling. The initial textual decoding of a PDF/ZIP response is not a faithful binary capture; the later blob approach avoids that text conversion. The shared FormData object in the loop accumulates fields and should not be treated as a clean reusable uploader. The source's filename-parsing pipeline ending in `xargs rm` can mis-handle filenames and deletes evidence; it was neither executed nor adopted. An archive member named `Unknown.pdf` can contain non-PDF bytes. Existing file/strings/curl guidance already separates classification, extracted strings and complete artifacts. The disclosed application configuration is followed by a case-specific SSH success, not a general rule that database credentials authorize an OS account.

Pages 18â€“22 correlate a loopback listener, forwarding, process identity and application source. The find command selects directories with an other-read mode bit, not all files readable by everyone. The displayed Node.js `spawn` uses an argument array without enabling a shell; inadequate path validation does not by itself establish shell-command injection. Output extension handling explains why an extensionless destination can become an OEB directory. Current calibre HTML-input code constrains resource paths by default, so the historical inclusion of an unrelated local file must not be promised for every current build.

On pages 23â€“26, a password prompt after offering a key does not uniquely diagnose a missing authorized_keys file. Page 24's screenshot says `Cannot POST /convert`; that HTTP 404 does not prove a particular output-file rejection. The later file listing and successful response are separate evidence. A denied `sysctl fs.protected_symlinks` read leaves its value unknown. Kernel documentation describes the conditions when enabled, including the sticky world-writable directory and ownership checks; it does not establish the target's value or attribute every HTTP 500 to that protection. No symlink, permission or authorized-key change was reproduced.

Pages 27â€“33 show a privileged Python wrapper interpolating an SQL argument, inserting returned text into a PostScript template and invoking ps2pdf with NOSAFER. An unchanged generated template on page 29 does not establish that the intended row was returned. Page 30's rendered result identifies MariaDB 10.3.38; this is more specific evidence than the application connector's name. Page 32 shows the changed rendering, followed by a separate file-write/SSH sequence in the source. The wrapper's test of stdout alone is not a conversion-status check: the child return status, diagnostics and final artifact have distinct meanings. No SQL injection, PostScript file-write payload or privileged generator execution was adopted into the skills.

**Reviewed without additional skills.** Existing Nmap, curl, file, strings, SSH, ssh-keygen, process/socket inspection, Python/PHP serving, MySQL and sudo guidance covers the repeated surrounding tasks. The browser/Burp exploit chain, unbounded basket polling, callback upload servers, credential reuse, arbitrary resource extraction, symlink overwrite and privileged label manipulation were read for context and deliberately not executed or packaged. A dedicated sysctl or archive skill was not created from a single failed read or routine unzip transcript; the useful interpretation is preserved here and conversion-output handling is covered in the new tool skill. All pages were covered.

**Primary sources.** Checked calibre's [CLI reference](https://manual.calibre-ebook.com/generated/en/ebook-convert.html), [conversion stages](https://manual.calibre-ebook.com/conversion.html) and [HTML input implementation](https://github.com/kovidgoyal/calibre/blob/master/src/calibre/ebooks/conversion/plugins/html_input.py). Checked Ghostscript's [ps2pdf entry script](https://github.com/ArtifexSoftware/ghostpdl/blob/master/lib/ps2pdf), [ps2pdf14](https://github.com/ArtifexSoftware/ghostpdl/blob/master/lib/ps2pdf14), [wrapper implementation](https://github.com/ArtifexSoftware/ghostpdl/blob/master/lib/ps2pdfwr), [SAFER and path access](https://ghostscript.readthedocs.io/en/latest/Use.html) and [PDF device behavior](https://ghostscript.readthedocs.io/en/latest/VectorDevices.html). [Node.js child-process documentation](https://nodejs.org/api/child_process.html#child_processspawncommand-args-options) supports the no-shell distinction; the [kernel sysctl reference](https://docs.kernel.org/admin-guide/sysctl/fs.html#protected-symlinks) supports the limited symlink interpretation. Permission predicates were verified in the [GNU find manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man1/find.1.html), reproduced with its upstream authorship. The attempted GNU website manual paths and Ghostscript Ps2pdf.html page were unavailable through the web reader; the references above supplied the actual verification.

**Checks actually run.** All three affected skills passed the skill validator. Repository checks passed for **116 packages, 151 Markdown files, 199 local links and 224 Bash blocks**, syntax only; `git diff --check` passed. ebook-convert, ps2pdf and gs were not found on PATH. No converter, permission-predicate fixture, Linux execution or worker evaluation ran. No target request, authentication, installation, system configuration change, original-file deletion/movement, commit or push occurred. Extracted text, fourteen rendered pages, retained source trail and check outputs remain in ignored work storage.

### 11. Bounty.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **13 physical PDF pages** were read. All nine screenshot-bearing content pages, **3â€“11**, were rendered and visually inspected in contact sheets; pages **4, 5 and 8** were also inspected individually at full rendered size. Pages 12â€“13 contain the readable web.config appendix. Source: Hack The Box, *Bounty*, prepared by egre55, machine author mrb3n, cover dated 2 November 2018, document D18.100.25. SHA-256: `cdddec1da05f6f220ac14f152e768fb656876ee74731f5e9369e130fed339cd4`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `masscan` | 3 shows raw discovery followed by Nmap and a human-output port extraction pipeline. Added effective-configuration preview, explicit list output, host/protocol-preserving deduplication, an empty-result branch and service follow-up. |
| New `iis-shortname-scanner` | 4â€“5 show differing OPTIONS responses, the Java scanner and partial aliases. Added detection before serial enumeration, explicit matching configuration, method/delay/version controls and uncertainty-aware full-name inference. |
| Enriched `dirsearch` | 5 narrows a wordlist using short-name hints. Added a small ASP.NET candidate workflow, current extension-expansion semantics and distinction between a guessed alias and a confirmed URL. |

The catalogue now contains **118 independent skills**. The old dirsearch maintenance footer is retained below. Invented examples are labelled illustrative; the adapted commands are not presented as an executed target transcript.

**Corrections and interpretation.** Pages 1â€“2 establish the historical case and uploader/patching theme. Page 3 shows a selected-interface Masscan run and Nmap 7.70 identifying port 80 as IIS 7.5. The source parses human output by positional fields, losing host/protocol context. The new list parser retains both and avoids a merged multi-host port list. The old rate and tunnel name are not portable defaults. No-response discovery is not complete evidence of service absence.

On page 4, the illustrated OPTIONS probes return 200 for one nonexistent pattern and 404 for a matching pattern. That is this server's distinction, not a universal status-code rule. The screenshot uses Java scanner 2.3.9, whereas current upstream source identifies 2023.4 and different runtime requirements. The final output contains directory/file aliases and an uncertain extension with question marks plus a possible network/server warning; the latter was not treated as a confirmed name. Current source also trims an unsuffixed URL path component by default, so the skill requires a trailing slash and checking the reported target. The two-argument detection mode, progress mode 2 and serial thread setting 0 were checked in the argument parser. `maxRetryTimes` is a count despite a misleading nearby configuration comment; request delay is milliseconds. Unattended prompt suppression is separate from choosing delay, proxy, headers and methods.

Page 5 combines local wordlist matches into 36 entries and uses historical dirsearch v0.3.8. Its extension behavior is not assumed for the current tool: modern `-e` substitution and `-f` expansion were checked in the option parser. The displayed directory redirects and the browser's separate transfer.aspx result support different observations; the visible screenshot was not claimed to show every filename in the scan. A truncated ASP extension does not uniquely identify ASP versus ASPX or the full basename.

Page 6's brittle curl/grep/awk extraction of a legacy Microsoft HTML page is not a reliable general extension catalogue. The upload tests all return HTTP 200; length differences are triage hints and the inspected success body is stronger evidence of acceptance. Acceptance, subsequent retrieval and execution remain separate claims. Page 7 and the readable appendix on pages 12â€“13 show the case-specific web.config handler change and ASP/PowerShell command chain; none was executed or installed.

Page 8 shows Server 2008 R2, build 7600, x64, and `Hotfix(s): N/A`. That field alone is insufficient to certify all patches absent or prove a particular local exploit applicable. Microsoft's QFE documentation describes a limited update inventory; it does not prove the exact backend of this screenshot's systeminfo command. Page 9 shows the case's x64 Meterpreter payload/session and sysinfo output. The prose's absence-of-antivirus assumption was not adopted as a guarantee of undetected execution. Pages 10â€“11 distinguish module suggestions from the subsequent demonstrated SYSTEM sessions: a module without a check is not a confirmed vulnerability, and the scheduled-task workflow changes host state.

**Reviewed without additional skills.** Existing Nmap/curl guidance covers service follow-up and response-body interpretation. The Burp upload fuzzing, web.config payload, download-and-execute chain, msfvenom payload generation, session handlers, local exploit suggester and two privilege-escalation modules were read for context but not packaged into an additional general CLI skill here. A future Metasploit skill needs a dedicated, bounded session/module workflow and fixture; these screenshots do not establish current module compatibility or general coverage. All pages were covered; no unsupported pages were silently skipped.

**Primary sources.** Reviewed Masscan's [project guide](https://github.com/robertdavidgraham/masscan), [manual](https://github.com/robertdavidgraham/masscan/blob/master/doc/masscan.8) and [list-output writer](https://github.com/robertdavidgraham/masscan/blob/master/src/out-text.c) for configuration preview, defaults, IP targets, adapter/rate controls, receive interval, own-stack behavior and record fields. Reviewed IIS ShortName Scanner's [usage](https://github.com/irsdl/IIS-ShortName-Scanner), [configuration](https://github.com/irsdl/IIS-ShortName-Scanner/blob/master/release/config.xml) and [Java implementation](https://github.com/irsdl/IIS-ShortName-Scanner/blob/master/src/main/java/iisShortNameScanner/IISShortNameScannerTool.java) for CLI modes, path normalization, prompt suppression, delays/retries and uncertain results. Reviewed dirsearch's [usage](https://github.com/maurosoria/dirsearch/blob/master/docs/usage.md) and [option parser](https://github.com/maurosoria/dirsearch/blob/master/lib/parse/cmdline.py) for extension handling. Microsoft's [Win32_QuickFixEngineering documentation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/cimwin32prov/win32-quickfixengineering) supports the limited inventory distinction, not a target patch finding.

**Checks actually run.** All three affected skills passed the skill validator. Repository checks passed for **118 packages, 153 Markdown files, 201 local links and 230 Bash blocks**, syntax only; `git diff --check` passed. Four inert **Windows Git Bash** fixtures ran the skill's exact port-selection pipeline: mixed hosts/protocols, duplicate ports plus closed/banner records, no matching host, and empty output. All returned the expected list. No Masscan or Nmap command was executed by these fixtures. Masscan and dirsearch were absent from PATH; Java was present, but neither its version nor an IIS scanner JAR was executed. No HTTP probe, authentication, payload, Linux fixture or agent evaluation ran. No installation, system configuration change, original-file deletion/movement, commit or push occurred. Extracted text, nine rendered pages, retained source trail, fixture inputs/results and check outputs remain in ignored work storage.

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **16 physical PDF pages** were read. All fourteen content pages, **3â€“16**, were rendered and visually inspected, including the otherwise text-empty page 6. Source: Hack The Box, *BountyHunter*, prepared by felamos, machine creator ejedev, cover dated 19 November 2021, document D21.100.142. SHA-256: `37cd0f597fe9ca68dec5737b6b675df0eb0bef0ef47db9e8ef40bbca4eeedb51`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `python3` | 14â€“16 motivate examining input flow into eval and separating effects from a later validation result. Added a non-importing AST call locator, startup/encoding/version conditions, bounded static-analysis interpretation and a harmless control-flow example. This is a source-inspection adaptation verified against Python documentation, not a claim that the PDF ran that helper. |
| Enriched `base64` | 8â€“12 show form-encoded base64 wrapping XML and returned source bytes. Added the order of form/base64 decoding, plus-sign corruption and double-encoding decisions, and an exact-file-to-form request workflow. |
| Enriched `sudo` | 14â€“16 show a root-owned script allowed through an exact interpreter/script policy while a prompted file remains caller-controlled. Added the distinction between command restrictions, runtime inputs and effects before rejection. |

The catalogue now contains **119 independent skills**. The previous base64 source/test footer is retained below. No new umbrella or shared runtime dependency was introduced. Sample output remains labelled illustrative even where the harmless authored example was also tested locally.

**Corrections and interpretation.** Pages 1â€“2 describe the historical XXE/credential/source-review chain. Page 3's displayed pipeline explicitly starts with all TCP ports, so its wording about ports found "by default" must not be read as proof of Nmap's normal default coverage. The screenshot identifies SSH/OpenSSH and Apache HTTP; it does not establish a complete patch inventory. Existing Nmap guidance already handles uncertain coverage and the risks of parsing human output into a port list.

Pages 4â€“6 show path discovery, repeated 403 responses, a zero-byte 200 for db.php, README tasks and a portal page. An empty PHP HTTP response does not expose the file's source or prove it is empty on disk. README tasks are leads about application state; the document's claimed test-account login is not a general authentication procedure. Page 6 contains the portal screenshot omitted by text extraction and was explicitly inspected. Page 7's reflected form fields and conditional database message do not establish a committed database record.

Pages 8â€“10 establish the outer form encoding and inner base64/XML representation. The `%2B`/`%3D` layer must be handled before decoding base64, while a second form decode can corrupt a recovered plus sign. GNU `--wrap=0` disables output wrapping; it does not URL-encode the result. The new curl adaptation lets `--data-urlencode name@file` perform that outer layer exactly once. The supplied XXE payload and response show a case-specific file disclosure; an XML parser's resource support, process permissions and character/content restrictions limit any general "read any file" claim.

Pages 11â€“13 show a PHP stream-filter adaptation, decoded configuration and a subsequent SSH session as development. Apache alone does not prove `/var/www/html` is the document root; it was a hypothesis followed by this case's evidence. A database credential does not establish an OS account identity or authorization. The password-spraying claim was read as source context; no credential was copied into a runtime skill, no login was attempted, and no spraying workflow was added. The successful session screenshot and displayed source bytes are separate observations.

Pages 14â€“16 show the actual directory `/opt/skytrain_inc/`; the prose spelling with a space differs. The displayed script is root-owned and readable, with an eval call shown separately; the PDF does not include the entire validator source, so unshown parsing steps were not invented. `int(ticketCode) % 7 == 4` means remainder four, not divisibility by seven. A later `validationNumber > 100` check applies to the evaluated result, and cannot prevent an effect that already occurred during evaluation. The first page-16 screenshot prints root identity followed by `Invalid ticket.`; the later screenshot shows an interactive root session. These support the execution-versus-acceptance distinction more precisely than the prose's blanket success label. The exact sudo grant names Python 3.8 and the fixed validator path; it does not permit arbitrary interpreter arguments solely by virtue of naming Python.

**Reviewed without additional changes.** Existing Nmap, dirsearch, curl, SSH and PHP skills cover repeated enumeration, request evidence and interpreter-versus-web configuration. No additional general shell/cat/ls skill was justified by routine commands. The Burp GUI workflow, XML file-disclosure payload, PHP-wrapper target reads, credential spraying and privileged Python payload were read for context and deliberately not executed or packaged as new exploitation recipes. A broader XML-tool skill would need evidence for a specific CLI and parser configuration; this document principally shows browser/Burp requests. All pages were covered.

**Primary sources.** Reviewed Python's [AST parsing, traversal and literal-evaluation limits](https://docs.python.org/3.12/library/ast.html), [isolated/site startup switches](https://docs.python.org/3.12/using/cmdline.html), [eval/exec/compile semantics](https://docs.python.org/3.12/library/functions.html), [Boolean short-circuit and numeric behavior](https://docs.python.org/3.12/library/stdtypes.html), and [source-encoding reader](https://docs.python.org/3.12/library/tokenize.html). Reviewed [form decoding](https://docs.python.org/3/library/urllib.parse.html), curl's [form-file option implementation documentation](https://github.com/curl/curl/blob/master/docs/cmdline-opts/data-urlencode.md), GNU Coreutils' [base64/basenc option handling](https://github.com/coreutils/coreutils/blob/master/src/basenc.c) and sudo's [argument-matching manual](https://github.com/sudo-project/sudo/blob/main/docs/sudoers.man.in). The GNU HTML manual returned an internal error through the web reader; the upstream source supplied the actual wrap/decode verification. These references support portable semantics, not a reproduction of the target's full application.

**Checks actually run.** All three affected skills passed the skill validator. Repository checks passed for **119 packages, 154 Markdown files, 202 local links and 232 Bash blocks**, syntax only; `git diff --check` passed. Six inert **Windows Python 3.12.10** fixtures exercised the exact authored helper/example: direct eval call without executing a module body, comments/string text, the documented alias-detection limitation, syntax failure, Latin-1 source encoding, and a harmless print effect preceding rejection. All matched the expected observations. The target's Python 3.8 runtime and complete validator were not tested. No base64/curl request, XML parser, SSH/sudo command, supplied payload, Linux fixture or agent evaluation ran. No target authentication, installation, system configuration change, original-file movement/deletion, commit or push occurred. Extracted text, fourteen renders, retained source trail, fixture sources/results and check outputs remain in ignored work storage.

### 13. Breach.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **10 physical PDF pages** were read, rendered and visually inspected. Pages **7â€“9** were also inspected individually at full rendered size for key derivation, ticket creation and SQL/OS identity evidence. Source: Hack The Box, *Breach*, prepared by kavigihan, machine creator xct, cover dated 9 September 2025. SHA-256: `15bda79685d25254a29fa82ea6eb64ce2ad09010c408be08e37732a6d7dacc8d`. The extractor reported bad offsets for objects 10 and 19; text extraction completed and inspection of all ten renders covered the content rather than treating those warnings as a successful partial read.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `responder` | 4â€“5 motivate distinguishing direct SMB authentication from name poisoning, selecting modules, diagnosing bind/no-capture results and retaining complete NetNTLMv2 evidence. Current upstream code confirms that analyze mode does not disable independent authentication servers. |
| New `impacket-ticketer` | 6â€“8 motivate exact SPN, key type, domain SID/RID and cache checks. Added an explicitly scoped local service-ticket fixture using a keytab, explicit identity/groups and one-hour lifetime; this adaptation avoids silently inheriting privileged defaults or unrelated cache contents. Creation and cache inspection are separated from acceptance. |
| New `impacket-mssqlclient` | 8â€“9 motivate password/cache authentication, service-name versus connection-address handling, SQL identity/configuration inspection and the distinction between SQL login and xp_cmdshell process identity. |
| Enriched `pypykatz` | 7 shows `crypto nt` with a known password. Added exact NT derivation semantics, a public example string, positional-argument exposure and the distinction from AES keys and challenge-response records. |
| Enriched `hashcat` | 5 shows captured NTLMv2-SSP material. Added mode 5600, the complete input structure and why a truncated record or raw NT mode is inappropriate. |

The catalogue now contains **122 independent skills**. The new command forms are documentation-derived adaptations, not claims that the PDF used them or that they were executed locally. No review footer or shared runtime dependency was added.

**Corrections and interpretation.** Page 1 outlines the guest-SMB, captured-authentication, service-key, SQL and Windows escalation chain. Page 2 uses Nmap default TCP discovery before a version/script follow-up; that is not all-ports coverage. Human-output filtering and a SQL Server RTM banner do not establish complete service or patch coverage. Page 3's `tee /etc/hosts` command lacks append mode and would replace the file; it was not adopted or executed. The actual NetExec flag shown is `--shares`, despite singular prose. The later smbclient prompt names the client's default identity, so the earlier guest result does not establish that the two sessions used the same principal.

Pages 4â€“5 show an uploaded Internet Shortcut whose icon points to a literal UNC address, followed by SMB NTLMv2-SSP output. That route need not rely on name poisoning. Upload success alone does not establish that a particular person opened the file or requested authentication. The captured value is a challenge-response record, not a raw NT password hash; the PDF truncates the displayed record and therefore does not supply a usable complete fixture. Responder's unrestricted defaults were replaced with explicit mode/module decisions. Analyze mode only suppresses selected name-poisoning behavior; independently enabled SMB and other servers can still collect authentication. A running main process does not prove each requested listener bound successfully.

Pages 5â€“6 show the reported recovered credential, broad BloodHound collection and a GetUserSPNs request for `MSSQLSvc/breachdc.breach.vl:1433`. Existing BloodHound/GetUserSPNs guidance already covers bounded collection and TGS evidence. The displayed absence of a credential cache does not negate a subsequently returned TGS record. The graph's KRBTGT node and narrative about automatic rotation do not establish a password-change schedule; no unsupported rotation claim was transferred.

Page 7 uses a known service password with `pypykatz crypto nt`: this computes MD4 over the exact UTF-16LE password bytes, without a trailing null. The resulting NT key also serves RC4-HMAC, but it is not an AES key or a NetNTLMv2 response. The source ticket command omits the port displayed in the preceding SPN and changes the DC address; neither discrepancy was copied as a portable convention. The domain SID is distinct from an account RID. Current ticketer defaults include RID 500, multiple privileged groups and a ten-year lifetime; the authored fixture explicitly sets those fields. Current cache-saving code can import an inherited cache, so the scoped subshell clears that variable before creating a fresh fixture. Keytab use and current options were checked against upstream code; the source screenshot is an older Impacket version.

Pages 8â€“9 show a saved cache and SQL connection. A TLS-upgrade message is not successful SQL authentication, and a saved ticket does not prove its signature or target acceptance. The SQL prompt identifies a login/database context; it does not identify the OS token used by a command launched by the service. The source changes advanced options and xp_cmdshell with RECONFIGURE, then prints the service-account identity. The accompanying NULL row does not invalidate the preceding identity row. Current helpers also change advanced options, so disabling a helper is not proof that original configuration was restored.

Page 10 shows SeImpersonatePrivilege and a Windows-only GodPotato payload followed by a SYSTEM callback. A listed privilege alone is not proof that a particular exploit is compatible with every Windows version or service context. PowerShell's `curl` spelling is not evidence of GNU curl semantics. These observations were read for context; no payload, reverse connection, privileged service change or target authentication was executed.

**Reviewed without additional changes / deliberately deferred.** Existing Nmap, NetExec, smbclient, BloodHound, GetUserSPNs, Hashcat's Kerberoast workflow, netcat and basic HTTP-server guidance already cover the reusable portions of their appearances. The GUI graph workflow, client-side shortcut trigger, xp_cmdshell enablement/payload sequence, PowerShell callback and Windows-only GodPotato chain were not expanded into new Linux tool recipes. A separate Windows execution skill would require appropriate platform scope and fixtures. All PDF pages were covered; the truncated challenge-response and payload text were not reconstructed or claimed as runnable evidence.

**Primary sources.** Impacket verification used the upstream [mssqlclient CLI](https://github.com/fortra/impacket/blob/master/examples/mssqlclient.py), [SQL shell helpers](https://github.com/fortra/impacket/blob/master/impacket/examples/mssqlshell.py), [TDS authentication/cache lookup](https://github.com/fortra/impacket/blob/master/impacket/tds.py) and [ticketer CLI/key/cache implementation](https://github.com/fortra/impacket/blob/master/examples/ticketer.py). These support authentication selection, host/port/SPN handling, KDC fallback, local-versus-remote helpers, keytab support, explicit ticket fields, request-mode differences and cache behavior.

SQL interpretation was checked against Microsoft's [SPN formats](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/sql/database-engine/configure-windows/register-a-service-principal-name-for-kerberos-connections), [ORIGINAL_LOGIN](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/sql/t-sql/functions/original-login-transact-sql), [role membership results](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/sql/t-sql/functions/is-srvrolemember-transact-sql), [configuration values and visibility](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/sql/relational-databases/system-catalog-views/sys-configurations-transact-sql) and [xp_cmdshell execution context](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/sql/relational-databases/system-stored-procedures/xp-cmdshell-transact-sql). Cache inspection was checked against [MIT klist](https://web.mit.edu/kerberos/krb5-latest/doc/user/user_commands/klist.html).

Responder verification used the [project guide](https://github.com/lgandx/Responder), [startup and analyze-mode handling](https://github.com/lgandx/Responder/blob/master/Responder.py), [default configuration](https://github.com/lgandx/Responder/blob/master/Responder.conf), [configuration loader](https://github.com/lgandx/Responder/blob/master/settings.py), [logging and filters](https://github.com/lgandx/Responder/blob/master/utils.py) and [SMB server](https://github.com/lgandx/Responder/blob/master/servers/SMB.py). The runtime skill consequently does not claim that RespondTo is a universal authentication-server ACL. Password derivation and capture format were verified against pypykatz's [crypto CLI helper](https://github.com/skelsec/pypykatz/blob/main/pypykatz/utils/crypto/cmdhelper.py) and [NT derivation](https://github.com/skelsec/pypykatz/blob/main/pypykatz/utils/crypto/winhash.py), [RFC 4757](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4757.html) and Hashcat's [NetNTLMv2 module 05600](https://github.com/hashcat/hashcat/blob/master/src/modules/module_05600.c). An initially guessed pypykatz source path was unavailable; the actual paths were resolved through the upstream repository. None of these documentation checks implies Linux execution.

**Checks actually run.** All five affected skills passed the skill validator. Repository checks passed for **122 packages, 157 Markdown files, 205 local links and 238 Bash blocks**, syntax only; `git diff --check` passed. None of the five affected tool entrypoints (including the checked upstream aliases) was found on PATH. No affected CLI, password derivation/recovery, cache generation, SQL statement, Responder listener, supplied payload, Linux fixture or worker evaluation ran. No target authentication, tool installation, system configuration change, original-file movement/deletion, commit or push occurred. Extracted text, all ten renders, availability and check outputs remain in ignored work storage; the source review trail is recorded above.
