This includes the entirely screenshot-only page 20. Source: Hack The Box, *Breadcrumbs*, prepared by MinatoTW, machine creator helich0pper, cover dated 14 July 2021, document D21.100.123. SHA-256: `a0b93005a05b8c3328fad35234090bfbb0e15175abd1bb7887ff8f084b2dc81c`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| Enriched `openssl` | 25â€“26 motivate a known-format AES-CBC decryption adaptation. Added a skill-local conditional reference connecting raw key/IV bytes, outer encoding, default versus disabled padding, partial output on failure and the limits of apparent success. The PDF uses pyaes; the OpenSSL workflow is an explicitly verified adaptation. |
| Enriched `file` | 23 contains a direct conflict: prose calls the binary 32-bit while the screenshot reports ELF 64-bit x86-64. Added header follow-up, storage-host versus file-format separation and limits of the `not stripped`/ABI labels. |
| Enriched `jwt-tool` | 11â€“16 show a page/session gate and a separate JWT-plus-session upload gate. Added nested-claim and independent-session diagnosis, plus why non-302 status alone is insufficient evidence. |

No new tool package was justified; the catalogue remains **122 independent skills**. The AES reference stays within the OpenSSL folder. The three revised skills' earlier source/test footers were relocated below with their attribution retained.

**Page coverage and corrections.** Pages 1â€“2 identify the source and historical Windows web-application chain. Pages 3â€“8 cover Nmap, library pages, path discovery, registration, role listings and another directory scan. The Nmap command explicitly requests all TCP ports, but a service guess marked `mysql?` is not confirmation of an authenticated database. Gobuster's fifty-thread setting is source context, not a universally appropriate request rate. A zero-byte PHP response does not establish an empty source file, and a listed admin name is not authorization to act as that identity.

Pages 9â€“11 show a quoted-title result, intercepted book requests, traversal-based file disclosure and PHP source. A search returning a title with an apostrophe does not prove the implementation escaped input or that every SQL-injection path is absent. The disclosed win.ini and PHP bytes support this endpoint's file-read observation; they do not establish arbitrary code execution merely because a PHP file was read. Returned escaped source text must be decoded accurately before review.

Pages 12â€“14 show a session-ID generator selecting one username character and hashing fixed surrounding text, followed by candidate requests and an observed changed session. Its possible suffixes are bounded by distinct username characters, rather than a large random secret space. The script labels every non-302 response a valid session; that heuristic also admits errors or denials and was not adopted. PHP's Location header does not itself terminate execution. A candidate identifier only retrieves authenticated state if the application has corresponding saved session data; constructing the identifier does not populate that state.

Pages 15â€“16 show why access to the file-management page did not establish upload permission: the handler separately checks a nested JWT username and the server session. The hardcoded hex-looking secret is used as literal text by the displayed application. Existing jwt-tool guidance already covers exact key-file bytes and signature-versus-acceptance limits; this intake adds the independent session gate. Browser cookie edits, secret values and forged source-case tokens were not copied into runtime examples.

Pages 17â€“18 show multipart upload handling, an executed PHP payload and a PowerShell callback. A client-supplied MIME label or filename is not server validation. The prose's claim that renaming a function evades AMSI was not generalized into guidance: the provided session output establishes only the historical observed session, not a reliable detection guarantee. No bypass, payload download, upload or callback was performed. Pages 19â€“20 show a credential-containing JSON-like excerpt and a later SSH identity result; the excerpt includes trailing commas and is not a strict JSON fixture. Stored password-looking text and actual authentication evidence remain separate observations. Page 20's screenshot was read explicitly despite having no extracted text.

Pages 21â€“22 show a task note about password storage, a Sticky Notes directory and SQLite results. The directory contains a 4 KiB main database, a much larger WAL and a shared-memory sidecar. The existing SQLite/scp skills already require a consistent acquisition and warn that copying changing files independently is not a backup. Successful inspection here does not prove the wildcard transfer was atomic or that a modern app uses the same schema/path. The broad `SELECT *` was not repeated where bounded, schema-led queries are already documented.

Pages 23â€“24 show the downloaded binary, its ELF classification, decompiler output and Windows netstat listing. The screenshot is unambiguously ELF 64-bit x86-64, contradicting the 32-bit sentence. A Linux binary stored on Windows is not a Windows executable. The hardcoded hostname described as a cloud service ultimately leads to an observed loopback listener; the phrase alone is not evidence of public hosting. The libcurl form body is a clue to application inputs, not proof of the full server query. Existing SSH guidance already preserves listener scope, remote-loopback meaning and application hostname when forwarding.

Pages 25â€“27 show a form response, UNION-based data retrieval, a pyaes block-decryption loop, padded output and a subsequent administrator login. The first table-enumeration command as printed lacks its closing shell quote; it was not copied. AES alone does not establish CBC, an IV or a KDF. The reviewed pyaes constructor uses a zero IV when omitted and its low-level decrypt method returns whole blocks without removing padding. The source's displayed repeated trailing byte is consistent with PKCS#7; the new procedure checks the actual padding rule rather than simply ignoring arbitrary suffix bytes. No source-case ciphertext or credential was decrypted or authenticated locally.

**Reviewed without additional changes / deferred material.** Existing Nmap, Gobuster, curl, PHP, SQLite, scp, SSH, readelf and JWT signing instructions cover the repeated workflows. Ghidra appears as a GUI analysis step without a supported headless task, so no CLI skill was invented from it. PyJWT and pyaes are used as Python libraries; neither justified a separate command-line package here. Session enumeration, traversal reads, web-shell upload, AMSI evasion, UNION extraction and Windows-specific remote execution were read as source context and deliberately not executed or expanded into additional recipes. All pages were covered.

**Primary verification.** OpenSSL behavior was checked against its [enc manual](https://docs.openssl.org/3.5/man1/openssl-enc/) and [3.5 implementation](https://github.com/openssl/openssl/blob/openssl-3.5/apps/enc.c); the producer's default IV and block behavior against [pyaes CBC source](https://github.com/ricmoo/pyaes/blob/master/pyaes/aes.py). The public known-answer fixture uses [NIST SP 800-38A Appendix F.2.2](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication800-38a.pdf). ELF interpretation was checked against the file project's [ELF magic](https://github.com/file/file/blob/master/magic/Magdir/elf), [ELF reader](https://github.com/file/file/blob/master/src/readelf.c) and [GNU readelf manual](https://sourceware.org/binutils/docs/binutils/readelf.html). Session and redirect distinctions were checked against PHP's [session_start](https://www.php.net/manual/en/function.session-start.php) and [header](https://www.php.net/manual/en/function.header.php) documentation, alongside the [JWT specification](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7519.html). SQLite's [WAL documentation](https://sqlite.org/wal.html) corroborated existing coverage without requiring repeated instructions.

**Checks actually run.** All three revised skills passed the skill validator. Repository checks passed for **122 packages, 158 Markdown files, 206 local links and 240 Bash blocks**, syntax only; `git diff --check` passed. Five local fixtures passed using **Git for Windows OpenSSL 3.5.7**: the public NIST 64-byte CBC known answer with no padding; rejection of that non-PKCS7 input with 48 partial output bytes; a padded public-text round trip; a changed IV that still exits successfully while corrupting only the first block; and rejection of truncated ciphertext, also leaving 48 partial bytes. These test the authored decryption and failure interpretations, not the CTF credentials or source application. `file --version` reported 5.48, but no source ELF was available and no ELF parser fixture ran. Initial MSYS version calls were blocked by the sandbox; the authorized execution context succeeded without installation or configuration changes. No PHP/JWT/SQLite/source-case execution, HTTP/SSH request, target authentication, attack command, Linux fixture or agent evaluation occurred. Text, 26 renders, retained trails, fixture source/results and check artifacts remain in ignored work storage. Original PDFs were not changed, moved or deleted; no commit or push occurred.

The catalogue now contains **123 independent skills**. The prior LinPEAS source/test footer is retained below. Neither accepted workflow depends on another skill folder.

**Coverage and corrections.** Pages 1â€“2 show the source identity and all-TCP Nmap discovery followed by service/script scanning. SSH and Apache banners describe observed services; they do not inventory all patch state. The filtered human-output pipeline and high minimum scan rate remain source context; existing Nmap guidance covers explicit scope and incomplete results.

Pages 3â€“4 show hostname clues, an HTTP Host-header ffuf comparison, the selected crm response and a Dolibarr login. The reported 15,949-byte filter is a case-specific baseline. Existing ffuf guidance already distinguishes vhost routing from DNS existence and requires measured negatives before filters. The hosts-file commands do use append mode here, but no local configuration was changed. The historical successful admin/admin login was not turned into a credential-guessing default. A displayed application version is a lead whose affected feature, permissions and patch state still matter.

Pages 4â€“7 show the Website module, creation/editing of a page and case-manipulated PHP tags. The original research advisory confirms an authenticated Website-plugin issue in Dolibarr 17.0.0, fixed in 17.0.1; it is not evidence that an arbitrary unauthenticated Dolibarr deployment is exploitable. The doubled `www-data` in the screenshot is consistent with `system()` displaying output and the surrounding echo displaying its returned last line, rather than proof of two command executions. The named-pipe callback and Netcat listener were read as historical execution evidence and not reproduced.

Page 8 shows `script /dev/null -c /bin/bash`, a configuration file, a local account entry and subsequent SSH identity. `/dev/null` discards the typescript log, while terminal output remains interactive; it does not discard all shell output or make the transport equivalent to SSH. Existing script guidance covers that distinction. The database login in configuration and the OS account are different identities; the later successful SSH transcript, rather than the database password field alone, establishes the source case's reported reuse. No credential or target login was copied into examples or tested.

Page 9 downloads a current LinPEAS build, serves the current directory, pipes the retrieved script to Bash and highlights several SUID helpers. Existing instructions already require a reviewed saved build, explicit serving scope and avoiding unreviewed download-to-shell execution; repeating the broad collector procedure would add no value. The helper of interest is `enlightenment_sys`; the desktop program, `enlightenment_ckpasswd`, `enlightenment_backlight` and a cpufreq helper are separate files. The collector's label does not itself establish a package identity or CVE. Current upstream code emits the unknown label after classification tests, and other specific checks explicitly distinguish mount/NoNewPrivs conditions.

Page 10 reports version 0.23.1 from the desktop executable, names CVE-2022-37706, then shows a supplied exploit and a later `uid=0` result. The upstream 0.25.4 release notes confirm the CVE fix, but that upstream threshold cannot be applied mechanically to distribution packages. Debian's tracker lists the bullseye source-package fix as `0.24.2-8+deb11u1`, illustrating a backport below the upstream threshold. Ubuntu's reviewed tracker instead showed e17 entries including focal as needing evaluation; no Ubuntu fixed version was invented or borrowed from Debian. The PDF supplies neither dpkg ownership nor a complete package revision, so the new skill is a way to close that evidence gap, not a claim that it was closed on this target. The mount error near the end does not, by itself, negate the separately displayed root identity; conversely the exploit's own success message would not establish that identity without further evidence.

**Reviewed without changes / deliberately deferred.** Existing Nmap, ffuf, curl, Netcat, script, PHP, SSH and Python HTTP server guidance covers the recurring CLI portions. Enlightenment appears as a GUI application's version query and privileged helper target; this did not justify a general desktop CLI skill. Dolibarr's browser workflow, injected PHP/reverse connection, credential reuse and the supplied local exploit were studied as context and deliberately not executed or packaged as new payload procedures. No general wget/cat/id/hosts tutorial was added merely to mirror routine commands. Every page was covered.

**Primary sources.** Package query fields, patterns, installed status and exit codes were verified with the [dpkg-query manual](https://manpages.debian.org/bookworm/dpkg/dpkg-query.1.en.html); comparison behavior with the [dpkg manual](https://manpages.debian.org/bookworm/dpkg/dpkg.1.en.html) and [Debian version format](https://manpages.debian.org/bookworm/dpkg-dev/deb-version.7.en.html). The concrete scope/backport distinction was checked against the [Debian tracker](https://security-tracker.debian.org/tracker/CVE-2022-37706), [Ubuntu tracker](https://ubuntu.com/security/CVE-2022-37706) and [Enlightenment 0.25.4 release](https://www.enlightenment.org/news/2022-09-15-enlightenment-0.25.4).

SUID classification was reviewed in the actual current [LinPEAS SUID module](https://github.com/peass-ng/PEASS-ng/blob/master/linPEAS/builder/linpeas_parts/8_interesting_perms_files/1_SUID.sh). An initially guessed older monolithic path was unavailable; the upstream GitHub directory listing resolved the current module, whose relevant source was read as text only. Credential-transition and inspection semantics were checked against [execve](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/execve.2.html), [GNU stat](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man1/stat.1.html) and [util-linux findmnt](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man8/findmnt.8.html). Contextual source corrections used the original researcher's [Dolibarr advisory](https://www.tinextacyber.com/security-advisory-dolibarr-17-0-0-php-code-injection-cve-2023-30253/), PHP's [system output/return semantics](https://www.php.net/manual/en/function.system.php) and the [util-linux script manual](https://github.com/util-linux/util-linux/blob/master/term-utils/script.1.adoc).

**Checks actually run.** Both affected skills passed the skill validator. Repository checks passed for **123 packages, 159 Markdown files, 207 local links and 244 Bash blocks**, syntax only; `git diff --check` passed. dpkg-query, dpkg, findmnt, LinPEAS and Enlightenment were not found on PATH. No affected CLI or version-comparison fixture ran, and no SUID, application or target behavior was reproduced. No attack command, target authentication, tool installation, system configuration change, Linux fixture or agent evaluation occurred. Text, all ten renders, retained source trail, availability and check outputs remain in ignored work storage. Original PDFs were not changed, moved or deleted; no commit or push occurred.

### 16. Broker.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **7 physical PDF pages** were read, rendered and visually inspected; screenshot pages **2, 4 and 5** were additionally inspected individually at full rendered size. Source: Hack The Box, prepared by and machine creator TheCyberGeek, cover dated 5 November 2023, document D23.101.39. SHA-256: `a80668e89b68bd17debad9d1d3caa9e9edd58f7aed23f884d5e24a7248a37df7`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `nginx` | 5â€“6 motivate selecting the actual binary/configuration, understanding worker identity and URI-to-file mapping, checking DAV prerequisites, and separating a listener from active configuration and write evidence. Added bounded configuration inspection/testing with explicit test side effects and failure branches. |
| Enriched `sudo` | 5 supplies the screenshot-only path grant. Added the crucial difference between no argument specification and an explicit empty argument string, plus configuration/module authority in a permitted daemon. |
| Enriched `curl` | 7 motivates a file PUT workflow that preserves bytes, uses an explicit destination and checks status/state. Added it to the existing API reference; relocated the two historical source/test footers without discarding their attribution. |

The catalogue now contains **124 independent skills**. Nginx inspection/testing is an administrative adaptation from the source's daemon/configuration behavior, not a claim that the PDF performed `-V` or `-T`. All required resources remain within each accepted skill folder.

**Coverage and corrections.** Pages 1â€“2 identify the case and show all-TCP discovery followed by service/script scanning. The screenshot reports Nginx 1.18.0 with HTTP 401 and ActiveMQ OpenWire 5.15.15 on TCP 61616. These are separate services; the HTTP response does not establish OpenWire authentication. The search phrase on page 2 incorrectly says CVE-2023-46606; the surrounding explanation, linked repository and Apache advisory identify **CVE-2023-46604**. Apache describes the Java OpenWire marshaller and affected clients as well as brokers, with branch fixes including 5.15.16. The version banner remains evidence to corroborate against the deployed build, not a reason to execute a proof of concept.

Pages 3â€“4 show generation of an ELF callback, a Spring XML payload, a Python HTTP server, Netcat and execution of a Go proof of concept. The embedded download uses `curl -s` without HTTP failure handling, and semicolons do not gate later commands on download success. The sender's packet message alone does not prove target execution. The separate listener screenshot includes a PTY command and `uid=1000(activemq)`, which support the historical case's reported session. These screenshots were read directly because extraction omitted the results. Existing Go, Python server, Ncat and script guidance already covers the reusable mechanics; no payload was executed or retained as a new recipe.

Page 5's screenshot shows `(ALL : ALL) NOPASSWD: /usr/sbin/nginx` without an argument restriction. The important authority is selecting configuration and its included/native resources, not merely skipping a password prompt. The source then combines root workers, a filesystem-wide HTTP root and DAV PUT; these are distinct directives and prerequisites. Nginx `user` controls workers, while `root` maps request paths. Neither a parent master running as root nor a root-looking path alone establishes the request's actual permissions. The general skill teaches those distinctions without providing the privileged replacement configuration.

Pages 5â€“6 start an additional instance through `-c`; that is not a test or reload of an existing master. The `ss` output shows an IPv4 wildcard listener on 1337 with no process details. It does not prove ownership, which configuration is loaded, remote reachability or successful writes. The source leaves parent-directory creation unspecified; standard DAV defaults require existing parents unless `create_full_put_path` is enabled. Included-module availability, request-body temporary storage and destination permissions remain consequential conditions. Existing ss guidance already covers socket visibility and namespace limits.

Pages 6â€“7 create an SSH key pair, issue a PUT and show a later root SSH identity. The source uses shell command substitution to construct the body, which removes trailing newlines, and does not capture an HTTP status. Curl's quiet completion alone would be insufficient write evidence; the later displayed `uid=0` is separate historical evidence. PUT can replace the destination rather than append a line. The accepted upload example uses a user-selected ordinary file/URL and explains byte preservation and uncertain outcomes, without reproducing an authorized_keys write or attempting a login.

**Reviewed without changes / deliberately deferred.** Existing Nmap, Go, Python HTTP server, Ncat, script, ss, SSH and ssh-keygen cover recurring selection, scope, session and transfer decisions. ActiveMQ appears as a remote service and proof-of-concept target, not an administrative CLI workflow; no generic ActiveMQ skill was invented. The source's exploit download/build, msfvenom callback, Spring process launch, privileged Nginx startup, credential-file replacement and root login were studied as context and deliberately not executed or packaged as new payload instructions. No pages were left unread.

**Primary sources.** Nginx behavior was checked against its [command-line parameters](https://nginx.org/en/docs/switches.html), [core directives](https://nginx.org/en/docs/ngx_core_module.html), [HTTP root/alias mapping](https://nginx.org/en/docs/http/ngx_http_core_module.html#root), [DAV module](https://nginx.org/en/docs/http/ngx_http_dav_module.html), [process control](https://nginx.org/en/docs/control.html) and [beginner guide](https://nginx.org/en/docs/beginners_guide.html). Upstream [nginx.c](https://github.com/nginx/nginx/blob/master/src/core/nginx.c) confirmed the configuration-test path, dynamic module loader and ignored `user` directive with a non-root master. Source was read as text only. Sudo argument matching was checked in the upstream [sudoers manual](https://github.com/sudo-project/sudo/blob/main/docs/sudoers.man.in).

Curl's upload, data and method distinctions were checked against the [curl manual](https://curl.se/docs/manpage.html), and newline removal against the Bash maintainer's [reference manual](https://tiswww.case.edu/php/chet/bash/bashref.html#Command-Substitution). The GNU web-reader URL and an attempted source mirror were unavailable; the maintainer-hosted manual supplied the relevant section. The vulnerability identifier, affected scope and historical branch fixes were checked against the [Apache ActiveMQ advisory](https://activemq.apache.org/security-advisories.data/CVE-2023-46604). Its text was also retrieved directly because the web excerpt omitted the description.

**Checks actually run.** All three affected skill packages passed validation. Repository checks passed for **124 packages, 160 Markdown files, 208 local links and 248 Bash blocks**, syntax only; `git diff --check` passed. Four inert Windows **curl 8.21.0** requests to a temporary Python HTTP fixture bound only to 127.0.0.1 passed: upload-file preserved all 32 fixture bytes; data-binary preserved the same bytes; data-at-file removed CR/LF/NUL, leaving 27 bytes; an HTTP 403 with an empty body still returned curl exit zero without a fail option. Requests disabled user curl configuration and proxies to isolate the fixture. The server was stopped and joined afterward. These establish client request/status behavior against that fixture, not Nginx persistence or target behavior.

Nginx was not found on PATH. No Nginx test/start/reload, DAV destination write, sudo execution, ActiveMQ operation, attack command, target authentication, installation or system configuration change ran. No Linux fixture or worker evaluation occurred. Text, all seven renders, retained source trails, fixture bytes/headers/results and check outputs remain in ignored work storage. Original PDFs were preserved; no commit or push occurred.

The catalogue now contains **125 independent skills**. The earlier Bash and pspy footers are retained below, keeping source/test history out of routine worker instructions.

**Coverage and corrections.** Pages 1â€“3 introduce the machine and a TCP service scan showing SSH, Apache HTTP/HTTPS and a hostname. The hosts-file command appends, but no host configuration was changed. Service banners and certificate names were treated as observations, not patch or identity proof.

Pages 4â€“5 show registration, an image retrieval parameter, rejection of an initial traversal and returned account-file content after double URL encoding. That result establishes the demonstrated file-read path, not arbitrary code inclusion or universal filter bypass. The `/bin/bash` grep is a filtered view, not a complete account inventory; `curl -k` disables certificate verification and was not made a routine default. Existing curl, grep and ffuf guidance already covers the reusable request/filter boundaries.

Pages 6â€“8 show PHP's time-seeded activation generator, an HTTP Date observation, an eleven-seed window and a filtered ffuf response. There is a material timestamp inconsistency: the full page-7 screenshot and prose show **2023-01-09 12:48:18 GMT**, which is **1673268498**, while the supplied **1673269358** is **13:02:38 UTC**, 860 seconds later. Both conversions were checked with timezone-aware Python. A plus/minus-five-second window cannot reconcile those timestamps. The reported later hit remains historical evidence, not validation that the written derivation is reproducible. PHP's official rand changelog also documents version-dependent generator/sequence changes; a local CLI sequence needs a matching runtime and exact code. No tokens were generated or submitted.

Pages 9â€“12 show the authenticated theme-cookie path and the AvatarInterface wakeup method copying bytes through application-controlled paths. The prose's abbreviated serialized fragment is not a complete serialized object to reuse. Source review establishes why loading the object can invoke behavior; it does not justify safely unserializing the captured object locally. The PHP session path/handler and web execution configuration are deployment-specific. The unquoted array-key expression in the historical payload is also version-dependent; it was not promoted as a current portable PHP example. The screenshot-only response/identity evidence was read, but no cookie injection, session-file copying or web payload ran.

Pages 13â€“15 show a reverse session, PTY use, application database configuration, a psql **13.9** client banner, TLS connection, selected user records, Hashcat results and a subsequent SSH identity. The configured database role is distinct from the web process's OS identity. A TLS cipher line alone does not establish hostname/CA verification. The source's fixed salt, colon-delimited hash input and successful mode-20 results support its case; the actual password-producing expression is not shown in the extracted source, so general guidance requires producer evidence or a known pair. No source-case password was checked, recovered, reused or copied into skill examples. Existing SSH and PHP pgsql coverage was preserved.

Pages 15â€“16 show pspy v1.2.0 observing UID 0 commands, including a timeout-wrapped Bash renewal job. UID metadata does not itself prove script-file ownership, writability or exact argv boundaries. The source's Ctrl-Z-and-restart advice can leave a collector suspended; the accepted correction directs inspection of the original instance first. The displayed `bash -c` command line lacks preserved argument boundaries, so the caller's source is needed before inferring which value reached the script as `$1`.

Pages 16â€“18 show the renewal excerpt, a one-day generated certificate, a command-bearing subject and later root identity. The script treats any nonzero checkend result as a path toward renewal, even though malformed input also produces nonzero. Its own `exit 1` for 'no need to renew' is the wrapper's convention, not OpenSSL's success convention. The injection arises when certificate text is reparsed by a child Bash; OpenSSL does not execute the CN. The source's `-keyout /dev/null` discards the private key and does not produce a reusable certificate/key pair; current OpenSSL documents `-nodes` as a deprecated synonym for `-noenc`. The later file ownership and root callback are historical observations only, not tests run here.

**Reviewed without changes / deliberately deferred.** Nmap, curl, ffuf, PHP, base64, SSH and PTY-related skills already cover the recurring mechanics. No payload for activation bypass, deserialization, web execution, reverse connection or privileged certificate injection was packaged. The timestamp/PRNG discrepancy would need a coherent source transcript and matching PHP fixture for a reproducibility claim; PHP was unavailable here. No generic tutorial was added merely because a command appeared. Every page was covered.

**Primary sources.** Psql flags, quoting, startup behavior, metadata, CSV and exit statuses were checked against the [current manual](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/app-psql.html) and [version-13 manual](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/13/app-psql.html); CSV introduction against [PostgreSQL 12 release notes](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/release/12.0/). Connection and transaction constraints used [libpq connection parameters](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/libpq-connect.html), [service entries](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/libpq-pgservice.html), [password files](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/libpq-pgpass.html), [read-only transactions](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-set-transaction.html) and [statement timeouts](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/runtime-config-client.html#GUC-STATEMENT-TIMEOUT).

Hashcat ordering and public self-tests came from upstream [module 20](https://github.com/hashcat/hashcat/blob/master/src/modules/module_00020.c) and [module 10](https://github.com/hashcat/hashcat/blob/master/src/modules/module_00010.c). OpenSSL semantics used [x509](https://docs.openssl.org/3.5/man1/openssl-x509/), [req](https://docs.openssl.org/3.5/man1/openssl-req/) and the reviewed [3.5 x509 source](https://github.com/openssl/openssl/blob/openssl-3.5/apps/x509.c). Bash invocation/expansion/job control was checked in the [Bash manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man1/bash.1.html); GNU and maintainer web-reader endpoints were intermittently unavailable, so this published manual supplied the relevant sections. Pspy debug behavior was checked against [upstream documentation](https://github.com/DominicBreuker/pspy). Contextual PHP findings used [rand](https://www.php.net/manual/en/function.rand.php), [unserialize](https://www.php.net/manual/en/function.unserialize.php) and [PHP 8 migration notes](https://www.php.net/manual/en/migration80.incompatible.php).

Repository checks passed for **125 packages, 161 Markdown files, 209 local links and 253 Bash blocks**, syntax only; `git diff --check` passed. Nine inert Windows checks passed: four with **OpenSSL 3.5.7** (a valid two-day fixture is beyond one hour, within three days, untrusted despite non-expiry, and malformed input also returns nonzero); three with **Bash 5.3.15(2)** (fixed positional data stays literal, interpolated source reparses a harmless printf, and the first extra argument is `$0`); two Python hashlib checks matched public Hashcat mode-20/10 self-test pairs. The OpenSSL fixture's temporary key/certificate were created only under ignored work and never installed or used for authentication. No file-moving or privileged-injection example was executed.

Psql, Hashcat, pspy and PHP were not found on PATH. No database connection/query, hash recovery, collector/job-control run, target operation, Linux fixture or worker evaluation occurred. ### 18. Bruno.pdf

| New `unzip` | 7â€“8 show a stored Windows-looking member name extracted on Linux before a separate Windows test. Added metadata-first inspection, CRC versus destination evidence, selected recovery, internal wildcard matching, collisions/skips and partial-output handling. The read-only inspection and bounded recovery controls are portable adaptations, not a claim that the PDF used them. |
| Enriched `ilspycmd` | 5â€“8 motivate tracing a managed assembly's exact archive-write API, destination construction, method return values and enumeration order. Adapted the source's desktop dnSpy workflow to the existing Linux decompiler procedure, without executing the captured application. |

The catalogue now contains **126 independent skills**. The previous ILSpyCmd and NetExec source/review footers are retained below and in ignored work; their historical mappings were not discarded.

**Page coverage and corrections.** Pages 1â€“3 give the synopsis and TCP scan, including anonymous FTP, LDAP, SMB, WinRM, the DC/domain names and SMB signing required. The reported clock skew, differing historical addresses and dates are case observations, not current environment facts. The human-output port pipeline and rate settings did not justify another Nmap workflow.

Pages 4â€“6 show FTP directories and application artifacts. `test.exe` is reported as ASCII text despite its extension. `SampleScanner.dll` is identified as managed while the native executable is a loader candidate, with later Windows observations showing the DLL loaded. The `.NETCoreApp,Version=v3.1` dependency target is framework information, not an exact compiler/SDK build record. A developer probing path naming a user is not proof of a live domain account. Existing file/dotnet skills already teach the useful identity/runtime distinctions; no obsolete runtime was installed and neither binary was executed.

The page-7 decompiler screenshot adds two details missing from the prose. The string contains `EYCAR`, and the subsequent `Replace` return value is discarded; the shown code therefore does not replace those characters in the original string. Also, `Directory.GetFiles` runs before ZIP extraction, and the ZIP branch advances to the next element of that original array. Newly created entries are not automatically added to that invocation's scan list. These are static observations from the displayed code, not a tested behavioral claim about an independently obtained assembly. The illustrative antivirus-test string was not created or executed locally.

Pages 7â€“8 trace `FullName` through `Path.Combine` into the two-argument `ExtractToFile` extension call. The shown code lacks a destination containment check, but writing still depends on the process, filesystem and extraction overload. That overload refuses an existing destination; the source's broad arbitrary-write language does not establish unrestricted replacement. The Linux `unzip` listing shows a filename containing a colon and backslashes; it does not show a Windows drive write on Linux. The later Windows screenshot is separate evidence about the custom application. Python's archive-writing name normalization also depends on the writer's OS, so running the same `ZipFile.write(arcname=...)` text on native Windows need not store the same name. No traversal archive was constructed or extracted here.

Pages 9â€“11 show Procmon path probes and a `LoadLibraryExW` stack, followed by a reported local callback. A `NAME NOT FOUND` event identifies an attempted lookup, not a successful load or execution by itself. Page 10 names a `windows/x64` payload but the displayed output selects `x86`; this is an unresolved command/output inconsistency. The page-11 local Windows desktop identity is distinct from the later domain-controller service identity. No payload generation, DLL substitution, loader execution or callback was reproduced.

Pages 11â€“13 show username probing, GetNPUsers AS-REP output, Hashcat mode 18200, credential validation and SMB shares. AS-REP recovery for an account without preauthentication is distinct from an SPN/TGS kerberoasting workflow. The prose's two named candidates differ from the displayed valid-user pair, so the full input list cannot be reconstructed from those assertions. Existing GetNPUsers, Hashcat and Kerbrute skills cover the supported distinctions. The source's application directory inference from FTP siblings and a known queue path still needs actual filesystem/configuration evidence. Its SMB-client command uses `svc_net` where the surrounding narrative uses `svc_scan`; that identity discrepancy was not silently normalized into a working transcript.

Pages 13â€“14 show a queue upload and later `svc_scan` identity, process-token privileges and a NetExec quota result of ten. A share-level write result does not prove permission for every nested file. The quota module reads a domain attribute rather than measuring remaining per-user capacity or trying a computer creation. A disabled token privilege does not by itself establish the outcome of a directory operation; domain rights, existing use and delegated container permissions are separate evidence.

Pages 15â€“16 show machine-account creation with a native Windows executable, port discovery, SID lookup, a combined relay/delegation/password-reset command and a subsequent administrator WinRM session. Possessing a service principal does not itself establish permission to modify the target delegation attribute. The writeup's 'Silver Ticket' wording was not accepted as evidence that this displayed sequence forged a ticket; its shown final operation includes a password reset, and the excerpt supplies no complete S4U exchange to validate. The resulting historical login was read as a case claim only.

**Reviewed without changes / deliberately deferred.** Existing Nmap, FTP, file, dotnet, Kerbrute, GetNPUsers, Hashcat, SMB-client and WinRM coverage was retained without repeating generic examples. Desktop Procmon/dnSpy observations informed interpretation, but do not justify Linux CLI skills for those Windows interfaces. Native Sharpmad, CheckPort, KrbRelay and CLSID enumeration, DLL payload creation, account creation, relay and password reset were not packaged or executed. The architecture, account-name and candidate-list discrepancies would require consistent original artifacts/transcripts for reproducibility claims. All pages were covered; no unread material was called completed.

**Primary verification.** UnZip behavior was checked against the installed Info-ZIP `-v`/`-hh` output, saved in work, and [Info-ZIP's project documentation](https://infozip.sourceforge.net/UnZip.html). An attempted project-hosted manual URL was unavailable; the installed extended help established option order, ZipInfo flags, environment defaults, wildcard matching, byte conversion, skips and traversal override behavior. Six local fixtures below verified selected consequences. A mirrored man page was encountered but was not needed as the final authority.

ILSpy CLI selection/IL inspection used the [upstream README](https://github.com/icsharpcode/ILSpy/blob/master/ICSharpCode.ILSpyCmd/README.md). Path and overwrite constraints used Microsoft's [Path.Combine](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.io.path.combine), [ExtractToFile documentation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.io.compression.zipfileextensions.extracttofile) and the [CoreFX 3.1 implementation](https://github.com/dotnet/corefx/blob/release/3.1/src/System.IO.Compression.ZipFile/src/System/IO/Compression/ZipFileExtensions.ZipArchiveEntry.Extract.cs). Return/collection observations were checked against [String.Replace](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.string.replace), [Directory.GetFiles](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.io.directory.getfiles) and [the .NET 8 Directory implementation](https://github.com/dotnet/runtime/blob/v8.0.0/src/libraries/System.Private.CoreLib/src/System/IO/Directory.cs). Cross-platform archive creation was reviewed in [CPython's ZipInfo.from_file implementation](https://github.com/python/cpython/blob/3.12/Lib/zipfile/__init__.py); no cross-platform execution claim is made.

NetExec's [maq module](https://github.com/Pennyw0rth/NetExec/blob/main/nxc/modules/maq.py) and [LDAP arguments](https://github.com/Pennyw0rth/NetExec/blob/main/nxc/protocols/ldap/proto_args.py) establish the query/CLI. Microsoft documents the [quota attribute](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/adschema/a-ms-ds-machineaccountquota), [computer-creation permission conditions](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-server/identity/ad-ds/manage/active-directory-domain-join-permissions) and [separate delegation security descriptor](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/adschema/a-msds-allowedtoactonbehalfofotheridentity). These sources support the bounded metadata interpretation; no directory mutation was attempted to test it.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `aws` | 7â€“9, 12â€“17 and 20â€“24 motivate explicit emulator/service endpoints, scoped credential sources, selected S3 objects, bounded DynamoDB reads, typed values and composite-key interpretation. Replaced the source's broad sync/scan default with inspectable bounded read procedures; no authentication, upload or database write ran. |
| New `getfacl` | 11 and 19 show extended ACLs and an access failure. Added numeric identity/mask interpretation, named-user precedence, parent traversal and inheritance distinctions. The source's claim that only two users have access overlooks the owning-group entry. |
| New `pd4cmd` | 20â€“24 show the Java converter and embedded PDF attachments. Added current supported CLI packaging/arguments, input/output association, resource context and stale-output diagnosis, with an explicit legacy-entrypoint distinction. |
| Enriched `curl` | 6 shows `x-amz-version-id` inside CORS policy headers rather than an actual version-ID field. Added the distinction between advertised header/method names and observed response evidence. |

The catalogue now contains **129 independent skills**. These additions have documentation and structural validation; this PDF did not receive runtime or agent evaluation.

**Coverage and corrections.** Pages 1â€“3 introduce LocalStack and show SSH/Apache service discovery, an HTTP redirect and hostnames. Pages 4â€“5 show image URLs under an S3-like endpoint and the later loaded page. Those observations motivated following the observed object path, not assuming every path segment represents a filesystem directory. No hosts-file edit or service scan was made.

Page 6 displays a JSON health-like response and, in a different screenshot, HTTP 404 headers. The latter command also differs from the preceding printed curl pipeline. `x-amz-version-id` appears in `Access-Control-Allow-Headers` and `Access-Control-Expose-Headers`; the screenshot does not show an actual `x-amz-version-id` response field. CORS policy alone therefore does not establish an object's version or authenticated method permissions. Existing curl status/body capture and jq pipeline guidance remained applicable.

Pages 7â€“9 show an initial missing-credentials error, dummy configuration, bucket listing, object listing, sync and later uploads. Dummy keys worked in the historical emulator; that is neither proof of real AWS authentication nor a universal rule for custom endpoints. Current LocalStack documentation distinguishes account namespacing and optional signature validation. The accepted skill uses existing scoped configuration and keeps the endpoint on each call, rather than changing HOME or running global configuration. S3's static object storage does not itself execute PHP; the later Apache/PHP result requires a distinct consumer/runtime relationship. The PDF's delay before uploaded content appears is an observation, not evidence of a particular replication schedule or script.

Pages 9â€“10 show the PHP information page and a reverse session as the web account. The original commands were read but no web code, listener or shell stabilization was executed. Pages 11â€“12 show denied access to the application directory, its full ACL and a readable development project. The ACL grants the root owner `rwx`, named user roy `r-x`, and the owning root group `r-x`, with a matching mask and no other rights. Membership of that group and other access mechanisms were not enumerated, so 'only roy and root' overstates the evidence. The application configuration points to a local DynamoDB endpoint and region; it does not itself establish authentication success.

Pages 13â€“14 show credentials configuration through a repurposed HOME, table enumeration, a full scan and a later SSH identity. The record values are typed DynamoDB attributes; their presence does not prove they are current system credentials. The accepted workflow reads selected fields and preserves pagination/status instead of routinely collecting passwords. No case credential was copied into examples or tested. Pages 15â€“18 show an alternative web-shell interface, redirects/health output, repeated database inspection, failed broad SSH guessing and a later hit. A failed attempt list does not prove users are absent, and the broadened username list was not promoted into a default recovery step.

Page 19 shows a loopback virtual host with `AssignUserId root root` inside an `IfModule mpm_itk_module` condition, followed by an SSH forward. That configuration is conditional and is not independent evidence of the currently loaded module, active config or every Apache process's identity. The existing SSH skill already explains client versus server loopback, explicit bind addresses and destination failures, so it was left unchanged.

Pages 20â€“21 show the PHP handler's DynamoDB scan, exact title filter, filename generation, writes inside a loop and a converter invocation after the loop. The filter uses equality, despite the prose's 'contains' wording. With multiple matches, the converter receives the last filename assigned; scan ordering is not established, and zero matches leave no assignment shown. The create-table command defines a composite primary key with title as partition key and data as sort key. Subsequent puts with changed data therefore have a different full key; they need not replace the earlier row. This changes the interpretation of later conversion attempts and prevents treating them as a deterministic latest-payload pipeline. No table or record was created here.

Pages 22â€“25 show a generated test PDF, attachment icons, displayed local-file/directory content, a private-key screenshot and the reported final SSH root identity. Embedded attachment content is distinct from rendered page text. The source's Java wrapper can read resources in the converter's process context; the source excerpts do not establish the behavior of every PD4ML version or a general directory-listing guarantee. The accepted skill teaches a reviewed local conversion and evidence checks without packaging the file-disclosure requests, key capture or privileged login.

**Reviewed without changes / deliberately deferred.** Nmap, jq, PHP, SSH and terminal-related skills already cover the recurring mechanics. No Hydra spraying workflow, S3 web-shell upload, DynamoDB payload insertion, sensitive-file attachment request, key recovery or privileged login was executed or added. Native browser attachment clicks were not turned into a speculative pdfdetach skill: that binary was unavailable in the bundled location checked, and it was not a source CLI. Exact legacy PD4ML behavior would require the original versioned JAR and a controlled fixture; the new skill explicitly targets the documented current interface after build verification. The source's account and root-session results remain historical claims. All pages were covered.

**Primary verification.** AWS endpoint and credential handling used [endpoint configuration](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/cli-configure-endpoints.html), [configuration files/providers](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/cli-configure-files.html) and [LocalStack credential rules](https://docs.localstack.cloud/aws/connecting/credentials/). S3 behavior used [ListObjectsV2](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/reference/s3api/list-objects-v2.html), [GetObject](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/reference/s3api/get-object.html), [sync](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/reference/s3/sync.html) and [static website hosting](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/WebsiteHosting.html).

DynamoDB commands/types/key semantics used [list-tables](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/reference/dynamodb/list-tables.html), [describe-table](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/reference/dynamodb/describe-table.html), [scan](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/reference/dynamodb/scan.html), [scan costs and consistency](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/Scan.html), [create-table](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/reference/dynamodb/create-table.html) and [put-item](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/reference/dynamodb/put-item.html). The CLI's [pagination source](https://github.com/aws/aws-cli/blob/v2/awscli/customizations/paginate.py) confirms that service-specific controls may be hidden from generated help and that API-shaped JSON parameters bypass the unified paginator; the skill uses `--no-paginate` with `MaxKeys`/`Limit` JSON rather than incorrectly combining it with `--page-size`. [CLI pagination documentation](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/cli-usage-pagination.html) distinguishes CLI tokens from service continuations.

ACL semantics were checked in the upstream-project manuals published at [getfacl(1)](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man1/getfacl.1.html), [acl(5)](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man5/acl.5.html) and [path_resolution(7)](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man7/path_resolution.7.html). The curl correction uses the [Fetch Standard's CORS header definitions](https://fetch.spec.whatwg.org/#http-access-control-expose-headers). Pd4Cmd invocation/width/output options and attachment behavior used the vendor's [current command reference](https://pd4ml.com/docs/pd4cmd-command-line-reference.html), [usage examples](https://pd4ml.com/docs/usage-examples.html) and [PHP wrapper documentation](https://pd4ml.com/support-topics/php-wrapper/). Older guessed vendor/manual URLs failed; the successfully read official pages above were used instead.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| Enriched `python3` | 5â€“6 motivate inspecting actual binary bytes instead of trusting a string variable named magic bytes. Added a bounded local binary-prefix check and interpretation of Unicode/UTF-8 expansion, incomplete signatures and prefix-only evidence. |
| Enriched `php` | 5â€“6 motivate tracing the upload MIME field, exact destination variables and actual move result. Added the distinction between HTTP upload provenance and content validity, including why populating `$_FILES` in CLI does not reproduce an HTTP upload. |

No new package was necessary; the catalogue remains **129 independently installable skills**. README descriptions were updated for PHP and Python. The existing Chisel review footer was moved out of runtime instructions; its preserved history is: documentation reviewed 2026-10-09, Linux execution untested, Antique p. 8, Anubis p. 8 and Appsanity p. 15, upstream Chisel usage as source. That older record is attribution/history, not new execution evidence.

**Coverage and corrections.** Pages 1â€“2 give the overview and reported chain. Pages 3â€“4 show service discovery on 8080, Apache/PHP banners and a Gym Management Software 1.0 contact-page statement. These identify investigation leads rather than proving the installed code's exact revision. The source's human-output Nmap extraction and generic unzip invocation did not warrant duplicate skills.

Page 5 shows a partial PHP upload handler and a Python Requests multipart client. The handler checks a client-supplied MIME field. It assigns `$extension` but later constructs the saved filename with `$ext`; the excerpt omits surrounding code. The relation between those values cannot be reconstructed from the shown lines. Consequently, the claimed double-extension behavior is not a general conclusion from this excerpt, and an extension allow-list declaration does not establish where or how it is enforced.

Pages 5â€“6 build the purported PNG prefix as a Python 3 Unicode string containing seven characters followed by a newline. That final newline makes eight characters, but UTF-8 encoding expands U+0089 to two bytes. Current Requests passes string-valued file content into urllib3's multipart encoder, whose string writer uses UTF-8; under that path the prefix becomes nine bytes starting `c2 89`. The W3C signature is eight bytes starting `89 50`, and even its exact presence does not make a complete PNG. The historical library versions and transmitted body were not supplied, so this is a source-supported current behavior and a reproduction caveat, not a recovered packet capture. The source's unconditional `Uploaded!` print does not check the HTTP or application result. Later screenshots report command execution, which is separate evidence from the claimed image-validation explanation.

Pages 6â€“7 show curl requests, a temporary HTTP server, a downloaded Windows Netcat executable and the reported session as `buff\shaun`. They were read without sending requests, transferring executables or starting listeners. No web-shell body or case credential was added to the skills.

Page 8 shows `CloudMe_1112.exe` in Downloads and a Windows netstat line with local address `127.0.0.1:8888`; the `0.0.0.0:0` foreign-address column is not the bind address. The installer filename is a version lead, while that netstat output alone does not identify the owning process or running build. The PDF describes a local installation in a VM but supplies no debugger derivation of the later offset/address.

Page 9 calls the connection a SOCKS proxy, but its remote syntax specifies a fixed reverse TCP forward to client loopback port 8888. It also says port 8080 in prose while the command uses 8888; the Chisel transport uses 9999. The displayed connection message establishes transport progress, not an application response. The accepted skill already used explicit loopback binding, authentication and a trusted fingerprint; those conditions were preserved.

Pages 9â€“10 display a generated reverse payload with callback port 4444, then a listener on 2222 and a reported administrator session. The callback host also differs from the earlier Chisel server host. Different hosts can be intentional, but the PDF does not reconcile this sequence, and the differing ports cannot be silently treated as one verified transcript. A fixed forward does not automatically rewrite a separate outbound connection's endpoint.

Page 11 supplies the complete appendix code: fixed padding, a hard-coded return address, payload bytes, a remaining-length calculation and a socket send. It was read as source evidence without execution. These build-specific constants and the source's Python 2 invocation do not demonstrate a portable exploit or a validated debugging workflow.

**Reviewed without changes / deliberately deferred.** Existing `xxd`, `searchsploit` and `msf-pattern` coverage already supplies exact-byte interpretation, applicability review and independently established offsets. No new debugger, payload generator or Windows netstat skill was inferred from mere mentions. Full application upload behavior, the exact CloudMe build, offset/address derivation and the inconsistent callback transcript would require controlled fixtures and original versions. They remain explicitly unverified; page coverage is complete. No exploitation, payload construction, authentication or remote operation ran.

**Primary verification.** PHP's [POST upload fields](https://www.php.net/manual/en/features.file-upload.post-method.php) and [move_uploaded_file](https://www.php.net/manual/en/function.move-uploaded-file.php) establish client control of the MIME field, HTTP upload provenance, result semantics and destination behavior. Chisel's [upstream usage](https://github.com/jpillora/chisel#usage) establishes remote syntax, reverse direction, default bind behavior and the explicit SOCKS variant. Python's [binary I/O](https://docs.python.org/3.12/library/functions.html#open) and [bytes/encoding documentation](https://docs.python.org/3.12/library/stdtypes.html#bytes-objects), plus the [PNG signature specification](https://www.w3.org/TR/png-3/#5PNG-file-signature), support the byte-inspection procedure. The current [Requests multipart preparation source](https://requests.readthedocs.io/en/latest/_modules/requests/models/) and [urllib3 multipart encoder](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/urllib3/urllib3/main/src/urllib3/filepost.py) support the encoding caveat. The urllib3 rendered-module URL failed; upstream source was successfully read instead.

**Checks actually run.** The three affected skills passed focused validation. Repository checks passed for **129 packages, 165 Markdown files, 213 local links and 265 Bash blocks**, syntax only; `git diff --check` passed. Four inert local byte fixtures ran with **Windows Python 3.12.10**, invoking the exact Python helper embedded in the new skill under `-I -S`: signature-only returned True without establishing a complete image, UTF-8-expanded and seven-byte prefixes returned False, and a 26-byte fixture reported only its first 16 bytes. All checks passed and fixture bytes remained unchanged. These tests did not decode a complete image. Requests was absent from both inspected Python runtimes; no Requests preparation/execution was claimed. PHP and Chisel behavior was documentation/source-reviewed, not executed. No Linux fixture or worker evaluation was available.

Text, all eleven renders, fixture results and structural checks remain in ignored work. No tool installation, machine configuration change, original-PDF movement/deletion, commit or push occurred.

**Coverage and corrections.** Pages 1â€“3 introduce the chain and display service discovery, filtered ports and the repository service. The synopsis/skills text says GitLab, while the HTTP title, cookie and screenshots identify Gitea. Page 3 labels port 512 as RSH despite the displayed scan distinguishing 512/exec, 513/login and 514/shell candidates. These names/probes are leads, not interchangeable protocol verification.

Pages 3â€“4 show account registration, a public repository, its Jenkinsfile and a daemon module listing. No registration or repository access was performed here. Module enumeration alone is not the same as reading a listed file; the accepted rsync workflow separates those steps. Page 5 shows the archive size and completed source download, then an encrypted XML field and a third-party decryption invocation using a configuration file and two Jenkins secret files. The source prose capitalizes `Hudson.util.Secret`, while the command uses `hudson.util.Secret`; portable Linux paths must retain their actual case. The path prefixes in the displayed ls commands/results also differ. A complete, matching backup is needed before treating those as a reproducible decryption invocation. No case password or secret key was copied into skill examples.

Page 6 shows a Gitea push-event webhook to a private address and a changed Jenkinsfile. A configured URL alone does not prove delivery, job execution or its OS identity. Page 7 reports the resulting container session. These are separate observations; neither repository edits nor Jenkins code execution were reproduced. Jenkins' primary documentation confirms that the filesystem master key protects other stored keys and is not itself encrypted, explaining why a backup containing the keys and ciphertext is sensitive without validating the supplied third-party decryptor.

Pages 7â€“8 show an interface address, several mounts using the same logical-volume device, a readable `.rhosts` and two hostname trust entries. The source's `.docker` marker is asserted without a displayed listing of that marker. An address or marker alone does not establish the complete namespace/host relationship. Crucially, the displayed device name and container target `/root` do not establish which directory on the host is the bind source. The report preserves that uncertainty instead of promoting the source's assumption that the host root user's trust file is necessarily the same file. A read-write mount also does not bypass ownership, ACL or identity constraints.

Pages 8â€“9 show a reverse SOCKS connection and a ProxyChains-wrapped database client. The transcript identifies a MariaDB 11.3.2 server; a command named mysql does not establish Oracle MySQL options or behavior. A command without a visible password argument does not alone establish that no credential/configuration provider supplied authentication. Existing mysql guidance already establishes connection context and actual matched account and avoids routine TLS disabling. The external filtered port and later reachable private endpoint were observed from different routes; neither observation invalidates the other.

Pages 9â€“10 show database/table enumeration, broad row reads, application records and a bcrypt recovery result. The accepted guidance keeps reads bounded and preserves the complete hash format. Cost 12 corresponds to 4096 internal iterations, not twelve password candidates or a portable timing guarantee. No database query, record/hash modification, password recovery or login was run here.

Pages 10â€“11 show the DNS administration UI, an A-record edit, a subsequent dig answer and a reported root session. A response from the explicitly queried DNS server does not show every cache, NSS source or the target daemon's hostname-trust decision. The final `rsh` invocation has no remote command and reports a connection to port **513**. GNU Inetutils documents falling back to rlogin when no command is supplied, making that a plausible explanation rather than proof that ordinary RSH command execution uses port 513. The exact source client build and daemon trust policy remain unidentified. No DNS record was changed or authentication attempted.

**Reviewed without changes / deliberately deferred.** Existing mysql, chisel, proxychains and general John session guidance already cover the recurring mechanics. No duplicate tunnel or database skill was added. A standalone Jenkins decryptor skill was deferred because the PDF provides an invocation but not enough versioned implementation detail to support its formats and failure handling. A standalone rsh skill was deferred pending clearer client/daemon scope; the final invocation and policy assumptions were not converted into a generic root-login recipe. The Jenkins reverse-session payload, database/DNS mutations and trust-based login were read but neither executed nor packaged. All pages were covered; these deferrals concern unsupported workflows, not skipped extraction.

**Primary verification.** Rsync behavior uses the upstream [command manual](https://download.samba.org/pub/rsync/rsync.1) and [daemon module configuration](https://download.samba.org/pub/rsync/rsyncd.conf.5). Filesystem evidence uses [findmnt](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man8/findmnt.8.html), [mountinfo field definitions](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man5/proc_pid_mountinfo.5.html) and upstream [FSROOT implementation](https://github.com/util-linux/util-linux/blob/master/misc-utils/findmnt.c). Docker uses [container inspect](https://docs.docker.com/reference/cli/docker/container/inspect/), [inspection output](https://docs.docker.com/reference/cli/docker/inspect/) and [bind-mount metadata](https://docs.docker.com/engine/storage/bind-mounts/); the JSON selection uses [jq's documented filtering/exit behavior](https://jqlang.org/manual/#invoking-jq).

DNS semantics use [BIND's dig manual](https://bind9.readthedocs.io/en/latest/manpages.html#dig-dns-lookup-utility), [RFC 2308](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2308) and [getent/NSS](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man1/getent.1.html). John format/cost handling uses [BF_fmt.c](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/openwall/john/bleeding-jumbo/src/BF_fmt.c) and [BF_common.c](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/openwall/john/bleeding-jumbo/src/BF_common.c), including the shift by the encoded cost. Jenkins context uses [storing secrets](https://www.jenkins.io/doc/developer/security/secrets/). The GNU manual's [rsh section](https://www.gnu.org/software/inetutils/manual/html_node/rsh-invocation.html) was available through indexed primary-source text after direct opens failed; its no-command rlogin behavior is used only to qualify the transcript, not to claim the historical client was identified.

**Checks actually run.** All five affected skills passed focused validation. The command was changed to ordinary inspect JSON with jq selection and Bash pipefail, and the subsequent checks passed; the checker itself was not changed. Rsync, dig, findmnt and Docker were not found on PATH. No runtime fixture, rsync transfer, DNS query, container inspection, database connection, decryptor, password recovery, target access, Linux execution or worker evaluation ran. Illustrative rows/records in the skills are labeled as such.

**Coverage and corrections.** Pages 1â€“2 introduce the chain, show service discovery and identify Jenkins 2.441 from the UI. A banner is a version lead; the advisory and actual build/configuration determine applicability. The displayed `-noCertificateCheck` is irrelevant to that plain HTTP URL and was not carried into the skill as a transport fix. Page 3 shows fragments from controller-side argument expansion, environment strings and local Docker setup. A random-looking hostname is a container lead, not proof of the complete host/namespace relationship. The selected mutable image tag does not establish reproduction of the remote controller version; the published port also lacks an explicit loopback bind. Pages 4â€“5 cover local setup screens and Docker exec, then inspect local Jenkins user directories and mappings. All setup screenshots were inspected. Pages 6â€“7 show users.xml, an application password field and a switch from help to connect-node for more output. The source's two-line example is not a universal disclosure limit: the advisory distinguishes permissions, commands and file encodings. More seriously, Jenkins 2.441's ConnectNodeCommand builds a HashSet from node arguments and may invoke node connection behavior. Its diagnostic lines can be reordered and duplicate lines removed; they are not a lossless copy of the input file. Neither command was run. Malformed or partial XML/hash evidence must remain incomplete instead of being repaired by speculation.

Page 7's anonymous-access explanation is interpreted as an effective Overall/Read permission condition rather than a universal boolean-to-permission rule. The displayed bcrypt value must be separated from the exact framework marker and visual wrapping. The reported password recovery was read but not reproduced, and no case credential was copied to runtime examples.

Pages 7â€“9 show an SSH credential entry, an sshagent pipeline, a build and a console containing private-key material. The plugin selects credentials by ID. The pipeline authenticates with that credential and then reads a file from the remote home directory; those two keys need not be identical. The supplied command disables SSH host-key checking; this was not promoted into reusable connection guidance. Existing SSH and fingerprint guidance cover the needed identity checks. The new CLI workflow reads an already existing build, without configuring or triggering one.

Pages 9â€“10 show a separate encrypted-credential field and Script Console decryption. The official Script Console documentation requires Administer permission and describes powerful controller execution; an ordinary successful login or ability to view a credential entry does not establish that permission. The application's encrypted field is distinct from a passphrase-protected OpenSSH file. The last screenshot contains only the beginning of a private key, not a complete delimited artifact, so that image alone cannot support a usable-key claim. The reported final login was not attempted.

The controller-side disclosure commands, pipeline credential use, remote-key retrieval, Groovy decryption and target login were read as source evidence but neither executed nor packaged as procedures. A broader Jenkins administration skill and runtime verification remain outside this narrow addition. All pages were covered; these decisions concern workflow scope and evidence limits, not skipped extraction.

**Primary verification.** Client transport, credential-file use, command discovery and console options were checked against the [Jenkins CLI handbook](https://www.jenkins.io/doc/book/managing/cli/) and upstream [CLI.java](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/jenkinsci/jenkins/master/cli/src/main/java/hudson/cli/CLI.java). The handbook's console prose says the omitted selector uses the last completed build, whereas the reviewed [ConsoleCommand.java](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/jenkinsci/jenkins/master/core/src/main/java/hudson/cli/ConsoleCommand.java) initializes it to `lastBuild`. The skill deliberately supplies a numeric build and claims no universal omitted-selector behavior. That source also confirms job read permission, output tailing and optional following; limiting returned lines is not a claim of bounded server-side log-scanning work.

Historical file-expansion behavior and fixes were checked against the [2024-01-24 advisory](https://www.jenkins.io/security/advisory/2024-01-24/), including the later listed 2.440.1 backport, and the version-tagged [2.441 ConnectNodeCommand](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/jenkinsci/jenkins/jenkins-2.441/core/src/main/java/hudson/cli/ConnectNodeCommand.java). Jenkins password-field handling uses [HudsonPrivateSecurityRealm.java](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/jenkinsci/jenkins/master/core/src/main/java/hudson/security/HudsonPrivateSecurityRealm.java). Credential/controller distinctions use the [SSH Agent plugin](https://www.jenkins.io/doc/pipeline/steps/ssh-agent/) and [Script Console documentation](https://www.jenkins.io/doc/book/managing/script-console/). Public-key derivation and fingerprint interpretation use the [OpenSSH ssh-keygen manual](https://man.openbsd.org/ssh-keygen.1). **Coverage and corrections.** Pages 1â€“4 introduce the chain, discover SSH/HTTP, add a local hostname mapping and show the search UI. The initial port extraction relies on a text pipeline and an unquoted variable; it is not promoted as a universally reliable scanning pattern. Existing Nmap and HTTP guidance already covers explicit targets and interpretable results. No scan, hosts-file edit or web request to the case system occurred.

Pages 5â€“7 identify a reported Searchor 2.4.0 footer, examine a patch and download a versioned source archive before studying the search function. The tagged upstream source confirms that 2.4.0 assembles an expression from engine/query and calls eval; 2.4.2 invokes the selected Engine method directly. This is a source comparison, not proof that every deployed application with the displayed footer has exactly that code. Browser field restrictions, server validation and the library CLI's arguments are distinct input paths. Existing Python AST/data-flow guidance already covers inspection without importing and the need to follow the actual value into evaluation.

Pages 7â€“9 describe local and remote code execution and a reverse connection. Those payloads were read as evidence but not executed or packaged. A local test under one identity does not establish the remote identity; the source provides separate output for those claims. Encoded payloads and the generated search URL are not required to teach the accepted CLI inspection tasks.

Pages 9â€“12 show a Git config containing a remote credential, a subsequent SSH session under another username, Gitea login and repository browsing. The config identifies stored HTTP authentication material and a service hostname. Any validity or reuse on SSH/web login needs independent evidence; neither follows automatically from the URL. The accepted Git query reads one acquired file and deliberately excludes includes and broader credential sources, so an empty result has a narrow meaning. No credential was copied into runtime examples or used to authenticate.

Pages 12â€“13 show a sudo policy allowing a particular Python interpreter and script with argument matching, and a file mode of **rwx--x--x**, owned by root. Explicitly running an interpreter as root explains its ability to read the script; the caller's execute bit does not itself let unprivileged Python read the source. The walkthrough's bare `*` invocation is subject to shell filename expansion before sudo sees argv, separately from wildcard matching in sudoers. Its observed help output is therefore not a portable consequence of copying that star into an arbitrary directory.

Pages 13â€“15 show Docker-like wrapper commands, custom-format JSON and a configured Gitea environment. A restricted wrapper grant is not direct permission to use Docker, and wrapper output may have a different JSON top level from the direct CLI's array. Crucially, `GITEA__database__PASSWD` belongs to the database account configured alongside HOST/USER/NAME. The reported later administrator login is evidence of reuse in this case, not the semantic meaning of that setting. Container configuration also does not reveal every live process change, mounted configuration file or external credential source.

Pages 15â€“16 show a scripts repository and a branch passing `./full-checkup.sh` to `run_command`. The screenshot does not include that function's implementation. Its behavior must be resolved together with the actual deployed code, process directory and any directory change or subprocess cwd. The working directory is distinct from the Python file's containing directory, and a slash-containing relative executable path is distinct from PATH lookup. Sudo's default, explicit policy directory settings and login mode are relevant conditions. A sequence of subprocess arguments can avoid shell parsing while still naming an untrusted executable.

Pages 16â€“18 show a successful check from one directory, creation of a replacement helper elsewhere and a reported root connection. No helper replacement, chmod, listener, privileged wrapper or target login was run. The final transcript is case evidence, not a verified reproduction on this host. All pages were covered; deferral below concerns executable workflows rather than unread pages.

**Reviewed without changes / deliberately deferred.** Existing `python3`, `jq`, `unzip`, SSH and Nmap coverage handles the recurring syntax and source-inspection mechanics. No Searchor exploitation skill or application-specific system-checkup wrapper package was added. Searchor's ordinary search-URL generation alone does not justify another narrow tool package from this material, while the exploit and relative-helper replacement are unnecessary to the accepted diagnostic tasks. No source attack commands, password reuse attempts, package installation or settings changes were performed.

**Primary verification.** Git field selection, file scope, origin output and include behavior use the [git-config reference](https://git-scm.com/docs/git-config); stored/effective URL distinctions also use [git-remote](https://git-scm.com/docs/git-remote). The older query spelling remains documented, although current Git recommends the newer subcommand interface. Docker behavior uses [inspect](https://docs.docker.com/reference/cli/docker/inspect/), [container inspect](https://docs.docker.com/reference/cli/docker/container/inspect/), [container environment settings](https://docs.docker.com/engine/containers/run/#environment-variables) and the tagged [Moby configuration type](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/moby/moby/v27.5.1/api/types/container/config.go). Account interpretation uses [Gitea's environment/configuration mapping](https://docs.gitea.com/administration/config-cheat-sheet/#use-environment-variables-to-setup-gitea).

Directory and executable resolution use upstream [sudoers policy](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/sudo-project/sudo/main/docs/sudoers.man.in), Python's [subprocess documentation](https://docs.python.org/3/library/subprocess.html#popen-constructor), [Python command-line input](https://docs.python.org/3/using/cmdline.html#interface-options) and Linux [exec path semantics](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man3/exec.3.html). The Searchor comparison used [2.4.0 main.py](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ArjunSharda/Searchor/v2.4.0/src/searchor/main.py) and [2.4.2 main.py](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ArjunSharda/Searchor/v2.4.2/src/searchor/main.py). Direct GNU Bash manual retrieval failed; the limited filename-expansion claim was additionally checked with an inert installed Git Bash fixture, without claiming sudo-policy execution.
