| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `sqlmap` | 9â€“11 use a saved HTTP request to identify databases, tables and selected fields. Added explicit parameter scope, baseline/session fidelity, metadata discovery, limited data sampling and incomplete-result handling. |
| New `hashid` | 12 identifies a bcrypt record before John recovery. Added offline format hints, ambiguous candidate interpretation and recovery from unsupported prefixes or misleading normal exit status. |
| Enriched `ncat` | 18â€“19 query Memcached with nc. Adapted the protocol task to the existing Nmap Ncat package, with CRLF framing, slab counts, bounded legacy key listing and one selected value. |
| Enriched `docker` | 19â€“21 connect group membership to daemon-backed host access. Corrected the mechanism: rootful daemon authority and socket/API access, rather than a SUID Docker client; distinguished rootless mode and container versus host identity. |

The catalogue now contains **135 independent skills**. The existing Ncat skill already covers TCP transport, so no duplicate nc or Memcached helper package was created. The new runtime instructions contain no source-case passwords, addresses or document footers.

**Coverage and corrections.** Pages 1â€“4 introduce the case, show SSH/HTTP enumeration, reveal client-side JavaScript credentials and try login/discovery requests. Literal browser-side comparisons are visible source evidence; one rejected payload does not prove that every server-side path is safe. Existing Nmap/HTTP and credential-context guidance covers the recurring CLI mechanics. A failed SSH login alone does not explain which authentication or account condition failed.

Pages 5â€“8 infer a second virtual host, identify OpenEMR and compare PHP code. The printed hosts-file command has problematic formatting and elevates printf rather than the shell redirection; it was not reused or executed. A copyright year and release chronology do not reliably establish a deployed patch version. On page 7, the displayed replacement code uses placeholder queries with separate parameter arrays, despite prose describing it as escaping. Page 8 shows mysqli_real_escape_string, although the explanation names the older mysql function. Escaping depends on connection character set and SQL context; these excerpts do not establish that every input is protected in a particular release.

Pages 9â€“11 show an error-based database response, raw-request export and sqlmap enumeration. A MySQL version banner is evidence about the database response, not proof of an exact OpenEMR patch level. The prose's user_secure spelling differs from the displayed users_secure table. The accepted workflow selects observed names and columns instead of inheriting either spelling. Broad database enumeration and a credential-field dump in the case are not necessary defaults for establishing the finding. Hashes were interpreted as stored password verifiers, not encrypted plaintext.

Page 12 displays several HashID labels and a subsequent John bcrypt recovery. Pattern matches do not prove the originating application, and multiple labels can describe the same format family. Current upstream HashID omits the bcrypt 2b prefix from its recognition expression; the skill records that concrete compatibility issue without prescribing mutation of the acquired value. The case's password recovery was read but not reproduced. Existing John bcrypt guidance already covers full records, cost and bounded recovery.

Pages 13â€“16 describe five application-side execution routes, including file editing, uploads, document templates and legacy exploit scripts. Directory listing by itself does not prove server-side execution; the source provides separate execution output for one route. Those mechanisms were read as evidence, but no PHP payload, Python 2 exploit invocation, remote upload or reverse connection was executed or added as a reusable procedure.

Page 17 shows an interactive shell and a local account switch. Its success does not retroactively explain the earlier SSH failure because authentication paths and policy can differ. Pages 18â€“19 then show Memcached statistics, key listing and a selected value. The displayed STAT items:1:number 5 means five stored items in slab class 1, contrary to the prose's claim of a single item numbered 1. The zero in stats cachedump 1 0 removes the requested item-count limit; server implementation limits still apply. Current upstream walks the cold LRU and caps the response buffer, so this legacy listing is not a complete inventory. The VALUE header's zero is opaque flags and its nine is a byte count excluding framing. One quit closes the connection; sending it twice is not a protocol requirement. Cached authentication-looking data does not itself prove that any login is currently valid.

Pages 19â€“21 show docker group membership, an Ubuntu image, a host-root bind mount, chroot and a reported root password change/login. The explanation incorrectly relies on the Docker client being SUID. A rootful daemon's authority and access to its API/socket explain the relevant privilege boundary. Group membership alone still needs effective identity, context and actual daemon-access evidence, and rootless mode changes the authority model. No host-root mount, chroot, password change or authentication was attempted.

**Reviewed without changes / deliberately deferred.** Existing Nmap, HTTP, John, PHP and Python coverage handles the repeated tasks. The upstream memcached-tool helper was examined, but adding a separate package would duplicate the accepted protocol task without demonstrated need. OpenEMR-specific exploitation, browser administration, bulk credential extraction, password reuse, root-container creation and account mutation were not packaged or executed. No generic OpenEMR, su or chroot skill was justified by these narrow observations. All pages were covered; deferral concerns executable scope and verification limits, not skipped reading.

**Primary verification.** Request import, test selection, metadata/retrieval controls and cached-session distinctions use the [sqlmap usage guide](https://github.com/sqlmapproject/sqlmap/wiki/Usage) and upstream [CLI definitions](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/sqlmapproject/sqlmap/master/lib/parse/cmdline.py), including time-limit and disable-hashing. Hash-format hints and recognition/exit behavior use the [HashID project](https://github.com/psypanda/hashID) and its [implementation](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/psypanda/hashID/master/hashid.py).

Transport behavior uses the [Ncat manual](https://nmap.org/book/ncat-man.html); the [OpenBSD nc manual](https://man.openbsd.org/nc.1) was also reviewed to avoid assuming interchangeable switches. Memcached framing, counters and values use the [basic protocol](https://docs.memcached.org/protocols/basic/), upstream [protocol specification](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/memcached/memcached/master/doc/protocol.txt), [text-command handling](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/memcached/memcached/master/proto_text.c) and [cachedump implementation](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/memcached/memcached/master/items.c). The optional helper review used upstream [memcached-tool](https://github.com/memcached/memcached/blob/master/scripts/memcached-tool). Current implementation details are qualified by server version; they are not claims about an executed reproduction of the historical system.

Docker authority uses the official [Linux daemon/group guidance](https://docs.docker.com/engine/install/linux-postinstall/) and [rootless documentation](https://docs.docker.com/engine/security/rootless/). The PHP correction uses [mysqli_real_escape_string](https://www.php.net/manual/en/mysqli.real-escape-string.php). The prior Ncat runtime footer was relocated here: its earlier source was **Appsanity pp. 15, 27**, adapted from nc interaction, documentation reviewed **2026-10-09**, Linux execution untested, with the same Ncat manual as primary reference. That attribution and historical limitation remain preserved without burdening workers.

**Checks actually run.** All **four affected skills** passed focused validation. Repository checks passed for **135 packages, 171 Markdown files, 219 local links and 286 Bash blocks**, syntax only; `git diff --check` passed. Ncat/nc, HashID, sqlmap and Docker were not found on this host's PATH. No runtime fixture, scanner, cache connection, sqlmap request, hash-identification/recovery execution, container operation, target authentication, Linux execution or worker evaluation occurred. The workflows are source/documentation reviewed and structurally checked, not runtime validated.

All 21 renders, extracted text and validation output remain in ignored work. No installation, system configuration change, original-PDF movement/deletion, commit or push occurred.


### 25. Cap.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **six physical PDF pages** were read, rendered and visually inspected. Pages **5 and 6** were additionally inspected individually at full rendered size for FTP commands/replies, file capability flags and final identity output. Extraction produced 3,638 characters with no warnings; screenshots supplied substantial command/result evidence missing from the text layer. Source: Hack The Box, prepared by MinatoTW, machine author infosecjack, cover dated 20 September 2021, document D21.100.132. SHA-256: `4c316021f93596f5d8f92501362280bade8270d4341b30fe1c79413685e0f909`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `tshark` | 3â€“5 inspect downloaded captures in Wireshark and recover an FTP authentication conversation. Adapted this GUI task to the compatible CLI analyzer: selected fields, stream/frame correlation, reply interpretation, stream following and incomplete/undecoded evidence. |
| New `getcap` | 5â€“6 show LinPEAS file capabilities and a Python UID transition. Added direct file inspection, effective/permitted/inheritable distinctions, namespace/mount/exec conditions and separate observation of an existing process. |

The catalogue now contains **137 independent skills**. Existing tcpdump covers capture and packet-arrival evidence, while TShark adds application field dissection and reconstructed conversations. Existing LinPEAS covers collector scope and SUID leads; getcap adds independent capability metadata inspection. No existing skill needed duplicate instructions, and no shared runtime dependency was introduced.

**Coverage and corrections.** Page 1 introduces the case and uses a text pipeline to derive ports for a second scan. Existing Nmap coverage already handles explicit scan scope and interpretation; the source's pipeline and high-rate defaults were not copied. Page 2 shows FTP, SSH and HTTP services and an anonymous FTP attempt ending in 530. That demonstrates rejection of that attempt, not necessarily a complete diagnosis of anonymous-access configuration. A service fingerprint and banner also do not prove every deployment detail.

Page 3 shows dashboard pages resembling ifconfig and netstat output and a capture download interface. The UI suggests administrative operations but does not independently establish their exact commands, live execution or process identity. Page 4 compares a newly generated capture with an older numbered object. Predictable object IDs alone do not establish IDOR; the access policy and ownership/authorization mismatch matter. No object enumeration, snapshot creation, capture download or request to the case system occurred.

Pages 4â€“5 show FTP traffic in the earlier capture. The page-5 packet list contains USER, a 331 challenge, PASS and a 230 successful-login response, so the supplied screenshot supports more than merely the presence of submitted credentials. The accepted CLI guidance keeps these events within the correct TCP conversation and preserves endpoints, time and frame references. The screenshot's endpoints are in a different address range from the target scanned in the walkthrough; capture provenance and network context must be preserved rather than silently equating them. Subsequent SSH use is separately reported case evidence of reuse, not a conclusion derivable solely from FTP bytes. The original pcap was not supplied, so no packet reanalysis was claimed, and no password was copied into skill examples.

Page 5 recommends downloading the latest LinPEAS and piping a fetched script directly into Bash. That execution pattern was not adopted: the existing collector skill requires a reviewed saved build. The displayed Python 3.8 file entry contains cap_setuid and cap_net_bind_service with effective, inheritable and permitted flags; ping/traceroute show cap_net_raw. The prose's explanation that the developer must have granted the Python flags for packet capture is speculation. Privileged port binding, RAW/PACKET socket use and UID changes are distinct authorities. General-purpose interpreter capabilities warrant direct inspection, independent of a SUID bit.

Page 6 invokes the capability-bearing Python interpreter, changes UID and starts Bash. Its final identity line shows UID 0 with the original nonzero GID and group membership. The source demonstrates a reported UID change, not a simultaneous change of every identity component. The new skill examines file metadata and already-running process evidence without reproducing that transition. Namespace mapping, exec suppression conditions and actual effective capabilities remain necessary to assess a different Linux host.

**Reviewed without changes / deliberately deferred.** Existing Nmap, FTP/SSH, tcpdump, Python and LinPEAS skills were inspected for overlap. No generic ifconfig/netstat package was created from browser screenshots without a meaningful supported CLI task. IDOR enumeration, credential reuse, arbitrary script download/execution, interpreter UID changes and a privileged shell were read as source evidence but not executed or packaged. No setcap/remediation operation was added. All pages were covered; these decisions concern scope rather than partial extraction.

**Primary verification.** Offline read, display filters, exported fields, stream following, Decode As and packet-count behavior use the [TShark manual](https://www.wireshark.org/docs/man-pages/tshark.html). Field names use the [FTP display-filter reference](https://www.wireshark.org/docs/dfref/f/ftp.html), and missing/retransmitted-segment interpretation uses the [Wireshark TCP analysis guide](https://www.wireshark.org/docs/wsug_html_chunked/ChAdvTCPAnalysis.html). FTP response meanings use [RFC 959](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc959.html). The runtime transcript is explicitly illustrative, not extracted or executed evidence.

File inspection and empty-versus-absent attributes use the libcap [getcap manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man8/getcap.8.html). Capability flags, file versus thread state, bounding sets and namespaced capabilities use [capabilities(7)](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man7/capabilities.7.html); UID transition conditions use [setuid(2)](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/setuid.2.html). Suppression conditions use [execve(2)](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/execve.2.html). Current process fields use [proc_pid_status(5)](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man5/proc_pid_status.5.html). The capability text-conversion manual was also consulted, but did not contain the sought flag explanation; the capability/exec definitions support that explanation instead.

**Checks actually run.** Both new skills passed focused validation. Repository checks passed for **137 packages, 173 Markdown files, 221 local links and 290 Bash blocks**, syntax only; `git diff --check` passed. TShark, getcap and capsh were absent from PATH, and the standard Windows Wireshark TShark path was absent. No runtime fixture, packet analyzer, live capture, capability query/change, collector, target authentication, Linux execution or worker evaluation occurred. Windows document extraction and structural checks do not establish runtime behavior on Linux.

All six renders, extracted text and validation output remain in ignored work. No installation, system configuration change, original-PDF movement/deletion, commit or push occurred.


### 26. Caption.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **25 physical PDF pages** were read, rendered and visually inspected in five contact sheets. | Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `h2csmuggler` | 8â€“15 use an upgrade path and later application requests. Added bounded upgrade/probe comparison, per-stage response interpretation, Python compatibility and request-fidelity limits. A role-error redirect is not successful access to the requested logs. |
| New `thrift` | 17â€“24 describe an IDL, Go bindings and a custom RPC client. Added fresh-directory generation, includes/output layout, compiler/runtime compatibility and distinct construction/open/protocol/method outcomes. |
| Enriched `go` | 18â€“25 show generated imports, shell-command construction and a success return. Added project-local module resolution and tracing of actual launch methods, file truncation and unchecked errors. |
| Enriched `curl` | 3, 10â€“12 interpret cached responses. Added stable-request comparison and corrected Age, TTL and the two transaction numbers in X-Varnish. |

The catalogue now contains **139 independent skills**. Final catalogue reconciliation found and corrected a missing Thrift row and the stale displayed total; all 139 skill folders have exactly one catalogue entry. No shared runtime requirement was introduced.

**Page coverage and source corrections.** Page 1 summarizes the exploit chain. Its mention of default GitBucket credentials is not itself a demonstrated login: pages 3â€“4 show publicly accessible repositories. Page 2's Nmap pipeline/high-rate defaults add no supported task beyond existing scan coverage. Service banners identify observations, not the complete deployed build/configuration.

Pages 3â€“7 show the reverse-proxy/cache configuration, a historical credential and a custom service. HAProxy forwards to cache HTTP port 6081; Varnish management port 6082 is a different endpoint. The VCL backend literal is 8000, while adjacent prose initially calls it 8080; the GitBucket listener also uses 8080. These were preserved as conflicting source details, not merged into one port. A regex anchored at the beginning of a path does not mean every double slash anywhere in a path is denied, and a hostname-prefix predicate is not an exact hostname equality test. The shown ACL expression's validity on the actual installed HAProxy release was not tested; no new HAProxy configuration skill was created from that fragment. A password found in an old commit establishes stored historical data before any separate authentication evidence.

Pages 8â€“9 demonstrate why h2c output must be separated into request stages. An initial response is followed by the requested /logs response redirecting to a role-error page. The initial status is not proof that protected log content was returned. The accepted skill keeps upgrade recognition, usable HTTP/2 data and application authorization separate and compares equivalent request context. Upstream's test mode itself stops at upgrade recognition.

Pages 10â€“12 describe forwarded-header reflection, shared cache manipulation and browser script execution. The source says the first X-Varnish number is absent on a miss; ordinary Varnish response construction instead omits the second, cached-object number. Age zero alone is not a universal miss test, and the observed age is not the object's TTL or a refresh schedule. Page 11 visibly contains a double `http://` prefix when the supplied forwarded-host value already includes a scheme. Reflection is evidence of the returned content, not proof of every intermediate proxy's trust or rewrite behavior. The narrative describes cache poisoning of content; its use of a cache-deception label does not establish a separate mechanism. Browser script/cookie collection was read but neither executed nor packaged as a CLI workflow.

Pages 13â€“15 identify a Copyparty traversal candidate and display selected file responses. A search result title is not a version/applicability check. The case's published exploit request was not promoted into a portable command without deployed-version and configuration evidence. Page 15 also contains a material identity mismatch: its printed authorized_keys record and the public record embedded in its OpenSSH private-key envelope have **different public blobs**. A local, public-only comparison produced these fingerprints:

- Printed authorized_keys record: `SHA256:ui/tjroDv1J8dgCcGNIaAr3QIk9BBLFDJVwiH9emcSQ`.
- Public record in the printed key envelope: `SHA256:PIlo0I1/f6tMTMzYzfexwv9juripWTYAf44zA0Wdtg0`.

This comparison parsed only the envelope's public fields in memory; no private-field decryption, signing or authentication occurred. The supplied records do not establish a matching pair. The later SSH login is a separate reported outcome, not a repair of the printed mismatch. Existing ssh-keygen guidance already covers independent public-key identity comparison, so no duplicate instructions were added and no key material was copied into a runtime skill.

Pages 16â€“17 show a loopback 9090 listener and a root Go process listing. The listener output lacks a visible PID association; grepping a process list does not by itself prove ownership of that particular socket. The later source uses `:9090`, which does not encode the displayed loopback-only binding. A source copy, process command and observed deployed socket require reconciliation rather than assumed equivalence. Existing ss guidance handles observed listener/namespace evidence.

Pages 17â€“20 present the Thrift contract, runtime and handler. The `1` in its argument declaration is a field ID, not the prose's method number. The rendered Go handler visibly uses **`exec.Command{...}`**, which is invalid call syntax for the standard-library function. Even replacing braces with parentheses would merely construct a command: the displayed code has no Run, Start, Output or CombinedOutput call. It therefore does not establish the claimed shell execution as printed. The source also creates/truncates output before processing, omits the scanner error check and returns a success string without establishing subprocess completion. These gaps explain the accepted source-tracing guidance; they are not repaired silently into an exploit.

Pages 21â€“25 describe a later RPC client and claimed privileged result. Any injection is in the custom handler's use of shell input, not demonstrated as an Apache Thrift framework vulnerability. The page-22 tidy output fetches an HTTP/2 dependency and is not proof that the shown Thrift client's dependencies were resolved. Constructing a socket and opening its transport are separate client stages. Moving generated application code into `/usr/local/go/src` on page 24 is not a portable module repair. The final narrative refers to making /bin/bash SUID while its command works on a copied /tmp/bash; reported effects and the shown code remain distinct evidence. No RPC or privilege transition was reproduced.

**Reviewed without changes / deliberately deferred.** Existing Nmap, Git, SSH/key inspection, ss and Chisel skills cover the repeated scan, history, socket and forwarding tasks. The source's broad Chisel bind does not justify copying a new exposure default into existing loopback-oriented instructions. No new package was made for a browser-only GitBucket/Burp task, an incomplete HAProxy fragment or an unverified Copyparty exploit candidate. Cache poisoning, cookie collection, traversal, credential reuse, custom RPC command injection and SUID shell creation were fully read as case evidence but not executed or packaged. These are deliberate scope decisions after reading all pages, not partial extraction.

**Primary verification.** The [h2csmuggler README](https://github.com/BishopFox/h2csmuggler) and [implementation](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/BishopFox/h2csmuggler/master/h2csmuggler.py) establish flag placement, socket timeout, test-mode stopping point and separate response stages. Source inspection also establishes initial-query/header/port limitations and replacement decoding of printed bodies. [Python 3.12 SSL changes](https://docs.python.org/3.12/whatsnew/3.12.html#ssl) explain the removed legacy HTTPS wrapper. These are client compatibility limits, not evidence that a selected server rejects an upgrade.

Thrift generation and contract interpretation use the [IDL reference](https://thrift.apache.org/docs/idl), [compiler option implementation](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/apache/thrift/master/compiler/cpp/src/thrift/main.cc), [language tutorials](https://thrift.apache.org/tutorial/) and [Go tutorial](https://thrift.apache.org/tutorial/go). The generic usage page was inaccessible through the reader; compiler source supplied the output/include/recurse semantics instead. Go behavior uses [os/exec](https://pkg.go.dev/os/exec), [os.Create](https://pkg.go.dev/os#Create), [Scanner.Err](https://pkg.go.dev/bufio#Scanner.Err) and [module documentation](https://go.dev/ref/mod).

Cache interpretation uses [RFC 9111, Age](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9111.html#section-5.1) and [Varnish 6.6 response construction](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/varnishcache/varnish-cache/6.6/bin/varnishd/cache/cache_req_fsm.c); the tagged [cache lifetime guide](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/varnishcache/varnish-cache/6.6/doc/sphinx/users-guide/increasing-your-hitrate.rst) was also consulted after the HTML guide redirected without useful reader content. Supplemental prefix/path semantics use the [HAProxy 2.8 configuration manual](https://www.haproxy.org/download/2.8/doc/configuration.txt), without claiming that release was the case's deployed version. The public-envelope comparison follows OpenSSH's [key format description](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/openssh/openssh-portable/master/PROTOCOL.key).

**Relocated Go history.** The old worker footer recorded: documentation reviewed 2026-10-09; Linux execution untested; Bamboo pp. 2â€“3. Its sources were the [Go command reference](https://go.dev/cmd/go/), [modules](https://go.dev/ref/mod), [toolchain selection](https://go.dev/doc/toolchain) and [reviewed scanner source](https://gist.githubusercontent.com/xct/597d48456214b15108b2817660fdee00/raw/f1fff423d457655b0740699acf4a61126804d16f/squidscan.go). That source trail is retained here; it is prior review history, not a newly executed scanner test. The runtime skill now states the reusable hardcoded-scanner condition without requiring the Bamboo case.

**Checks actually run.** Four focused skill validators passed. Repository checks passed for **139 packages, 175 Markdown files, 223 local links and 293 Bash blocks**, syntax only; `git diff --check` passed. Catalogue names exactly match all 139 folders. One inert Windows Python 3.12.10 public-envelope comparison verified the key mismatch; its initial single-line extraction failed on PDF wrapping and was corrected before the successful result. Only public fingerprints and the boolean comparison were saved as test results. No private-key parser/tool was invoked. Go, Thrift, HAProxy and h2csmuggler were absent from PATH. No compiler, RPC, upgrade request, cache request, target connection/authentication, Linux execution or worker evaluation occurred. Structural checks and the public-data fixture do not establish runtime behavior of these Linux workflows.

Extraction, 25 renders, source-review artifacts and structural output remain in ignored work; public comparison results are in ignored runs. No installation, system configuration change, original-PDF movement/deletion, commit or push occurred.


### 27. Carpediem.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **23 physical PDF pages** were rendered and individually read at full rendered size, including the mostly screenshot-only final pages. The extractor reported 17,421 characters without PDF-parser warnings, but Windows malware protection blocked reading the extracted text file. That blocked artifact was left untouched: no exclusion, protection change, alternate text reader or executable recovery was attempted. The original PDF remained renderable, so the complete review used its inert page images. This is full visual coverage, not a claim that the blocked text was read. Source: Hack The Box, prepared by polarbearer, machine authors d4rkpayl0ad and TheCyberGeek, cover dated 23 June 2022, document D22.100.182. SHA-256: `96e0b561243cc9ea3f7462bf2d5bb032f7f75cf0dd9c9ac6dee52bfbbe56b50d`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| Enriched `tshark` | 14â€“16 capture TLS and decrypt it through the Wireshark GUI. Added a skill-local CLI reference for handshake inspection, matching session secrets versus RSA-key conditions, request/stream correlation and incomplete decryption evidence. |
| Enriched `tcpdump` | 14â€“15 show a host-published service and bridge-side capture. Added interface/namespace and translated-port selection, handshake timing, snapshot length and bounded observation guidance. |
| Enriched `curl` | 7â€“8 build an incomplete multipart upload by hand and show a UI error despite a success response. Added explicit multipart file/text construction to the existing API reference, with byte/field semantics and separate write-state interpretation. |
| Enriched `grep` | 18 passes captured PHP output as a filename to grep. Added literal stdin matching and a distinction between absent text, input errors and producer failures. |
| Enriched `php` | 18â€“19 check a launcher's checksum before including a different writable file. Added dependency/path/identity tracing and the limits of launcher-only hashing or linting. |

No new tool package was needed. The catalogue remains **139 independent skills**; its TShark and tcpdump descriptions now reflect the added tasks. The conditional TLS workflow stays inside the TShark folder and does not depend on another installed skill.

**Coverage and interpretation.** Pages 1â€“3 introduce the case and show TCP/UDP service scans, a coming-soon page and a virtual-host response. Existing Nmap and Wfuzz instructions already distinguish explicit scope, protocol-specific results and response filtering. A virtual-host response is not proof of DNS existence. The source's system-wide hosts-file additions and broad/high-rate enumeration were not executed or copied as defaults.

Pages 4â€“6 enumerate /admin, register an account and change an account field before observing administrative access. The hidden field alone is not proof of a broken authorization check; the subsequent changed access is the reported case evidence. The GUI and Burp steps were read without being expanded into a generic CLI attack package.

Pages 7â€“8 show a zero-length request with a multipart error, a hand-built file part, a success-looking stored path and later command output. The displayed image/jpeg part type does not validate or transform the uploaded PHP bytes. The UI error, HTTP status, response payload, stored object and later execution are separate observations. The accepted curl example sends a reviewed ordinary file and documented fields; it does not reproduce the web shell or reverse connection.

Pages 9â€“12 show application configuration, ticket queries and a GUI SIP client used to access voicemail. A source-defined initial ticket counter is not evidence of the maximum or currently valid ticket IDs. Likewise, the narrative's claim that no listing API exists is not established by the shown single-ticket endpoint. Existing ffuf already handles measured negatives, filters and request budgets; no broad numeric enumeration was added. Zoiper is demonstrated graphically, with no supported CLI procedure in the material, so no invented VoIP command recipe was created. API keys, phone credentials and the spoken password were not copied into runtime instructions or used.

Pages 13â€“15 show SSH identity, a loopback host entry, local listening ports, tcpdump capabilities, a bridge interface and a readable certificate/key pair. The page-13 login banner has a different host address from earlier/final screenshots, so those views should not be silently treated as one timestamped network state. Existing ss and getcap guidance already separates observed listeners, ownership, file metadata and effective process authority.

The browser-side service uses port 8002, while the bridge screenshot contains TLS on port 443. This supports choosing a capture filter at the actual observation point. An interface called docker0 does not make all container traffic visible, and the source's unfiltered capture can include unrelated sessions. The new guidance retains both directions and the handshake while accounting for capture duration, drop counts and truncation.

Page 15 shows TLS 1.2 and an RSA-encrypted premaster-secret exchange before decoded HTTP is displayed. That case does not establish that every TLS connection can be decrypted with a server private key. A matching RSA certificate/key is insufficient for ephemeral key exchange or TLS 1.3; session secrets and capture completeness change the answer. No original pcap or matching case key was supplied as a separately usable fixture, and no packet decryption was performed here. A captured POST establishes submitted bytes; successful authentication is a separate server/session outcome.

Pages 15â€“18 forward a local port, log into Backdrop, upload and enable a custom module, then request it. The later curl command uses an HTTPS loopback URL without the earlier forwarded port 8888. Its execution host/context and socket therefore need reconciliation; it is not a faithful copy of that forwarded URL. Installing a module, enabling it and executing its PHP endpoint are also different effects. The source's zip invocation adds no distinctive task beyond basic packaging, so a new zip skill was not manufactured from one command.

Pages 18â€“19 contain a more useful source-review lesson. The heartbeat comment says ten seconds, while the displayed process contains a 45-second sleep; neither alone proves the scheduler's actual cadence. Its grep command treats captured PHP output as a filename, so an error can select the restore branch even if the marker was present. The checksum covers the launcher, while the launcher's include resolves another file. That is a dependency-integrity gap, not a demonstrated MD5 collision. The appended PHP statement is also printed without a terminating semicolon; its complete surrounding source would be required to establish executable syntax. The reported root shell is retained as a separate case outcome, not proof that every printed fragment runs unchanged.

Pages 19â€“23 present a container-escape script, a returned SSH key and a final host-root login. All script and screenshot pages were visually reviewed, including the cgroup/user-namespace branch and a busy-unmount diagnostic preceding the script's success text. A script's success message is not independent proof of a clean mount state, complete cleanup or host privilege. Existing Docker guidance already distinguishes container UID 0 from host authority. Cgroup version, kernel/package patch state, namespace permissions and mount conditions cannot be inferred from that identity alone. No release-agent writes, namespace changes, mounts, key use or host login were performed or packaged.

**Primary verification.** TLS prerequisites and secret handling use the [Wireshark TLS guide](https://wiki.wireshark.org/TLS). CLI options, two-pass dissection and TLS stream following use the [TShark manual](https://www.wireshark.org/docs/man-pages/tshark.html); RSA-key table syntax and crypto-build conditions are corroborated by the [upstream decryption tests](https://github.com/wireshark/wireshark/blob/master/test/suite_decryption.py). Those tests were read, not executed. The current TLS implementation marks the older keys_list preference obsolete, so the guidance uses the modern RSA table rather than copying historical preferences.

Snapshot length and capture-output behavior use the [tcpdump manual](https://github.com/the-tcpdump-group/tcpdump/blob/master/tcpdump.1.in); network distinctions use the [Docker bridge documentation](https://docs.docker.com/engine/network/drivers/bridge/). Form construction uses [curl's form and form-string reference](https://curl.se/docs/manpage.html). PHP uses [include semantics](https://www.php.net/manual/en/function.include.php) and [CLI option behavior](https://www.php.net/manual/en/features.commandline.options.php). Grep input/status rules use the official [input documentation](https://www.gnu.org/software/grep/doc/html_node/grep-Programs.html) and [usage guidance](https://www.gnu.org/software/grep/manual/html_node/Usage.html), with local fixtures corroborating the behavior. Several GNU manual URLs returned reader errors; indexed official sections and the installed GNU tool supplied the relevant evidence instead.

The [kernel cgroup-v1 documentation](https://www.kernel.org/doc/html/latest/admin-guide/cgroup-v1/cgroups.html) was consulted to bound the deferred release-agent material. An attempted Ubuntu CVE-page read failed; no fixed-version or current-vulnerability claim is based on that unavailable page.

**Relocated history.** The prior worker footers are preserved here:

**tcpdump:** Documentation reviewed 2026-10-09; Linux execution untested. Case: AdmirerToo pp. 11â€“12. Source: [upstream tcpdump manual](https://github.com/the-tcpdump-group/tcpdump/blob/master/tcpdump.1.in).

**grep:** Documentation reviewed 2026-10-09; Linux execution untested. Cases: Baby pp. 3â€“5; BackendTwo pp. 17â€“19. Source: [GNU grep manual](https://www.gnu.org/software/grep/manual/grep.html).

**Checks actually run.** Five focused skill validators passed. Repository checks passed for **139 packages, 176 Markdown files, 224 local links and 299 Bash blocks**, syntax only; `git diff --check` passed. Five inert Windows fixtures passed with GNU grep 3.0 from Git and Git-bundled curl 8.21.0: stdin match, no match, missing filename, a failed producer with a successful grep, and one loopback multipart upload preserving all 15 binary bytes plus a literal form-string value. The HTTP fixture bound only 127.0.0.1 and ended after its single request. This was a fixture transport issue, not a Linux runtime result.

TShark, tcpdump and PHP were absent from PATH; the standard Windows Wireshark TShark path was also absent. No live capture, TLS decryption, PHP application, VoIP call, source exploit, target authentication, Linux execution or worker evaluation occurred. Runtime guidance for those unavailable tools remains documentation-reviewed. The successful local tests validate the specific grep/form behavior only.

All 23 page images and review/check outputs remain in ignored work; inert test artifacts remain in ignored runs. The blocked text artifact was not reread or modified. No tool installation, security/configuration change, original-PDF movement/deletion, commit or push occurred.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `vtysh` | 18â€“28 show Quagga daemons, configuration, BGP neighbors, per-prefix paths, route installation and communities. Added a portable diagnostic workflow with modern FRR commands and explicit legacy Quagga alternatives, separating session state, BGP selection, Zebra/kernel forwarding and observed advertisement. |

The catalogue now contains **141 independent skills**. Neither new package requires another skill folder; the VTY guidance uses ordinary installed iproute2 only for its kernel cross-check. The existing SNMP review/footer history has moved below.

**Page coverage and corrections.** Pages 1â€“3 give the case scope and initial service scan. A filtered TCP port with no response does not identify a listening service; the aggressive all-port scan was not copied into a default procedure. Pages 4â€“7 show login errors, a recursive web enumerator, PHP information and exposed documents. The enumerator is a separate recursive-gobuster.pyz wrapper; its switches and behavior should not be transplanted into stock Gobuster. No wrapper skill was created without upstream support for a distinct task. Existing Nmap, Gobuster and PHP coverage handles the portable observations.

Pages 8â€“12 describe a three-AS topology and product-specific error codes. Error 45009 links the chassis serial to this application's unset admin credentials; it is not a universal router convention or permission to try inventory strings as passwords. The source queries the physical-table subtree but calls it the serial OID. Its screenshot prints the serial column without an instance suffix. The accepted example distinguishes the table, column and indexed instance, and labels its complete row output illustrative rather than claiming the historical agent returned it. A module's serial need not be the chassis serial; stripping an SN# prefix is case-specific. SNMP walks are also bounded by the selected subtree and access view, not every possible MIB.

Pages 13â€“18 show the restricted dashboard, ticket hints, a diagnostic process listing, a base64 form parameter, command injection and a reset script. Existing base64/curl/ps/Ncat/shell guidance covers the transferable encoding, argument and process observations. The ticket text is scenario evidence, not an instruction or authorization. The source's ten-minute cron entry stops Quagga, kills vtysh and restores two configuration files; observations taken before and after that reset need separate timestamps. No scheduler disabling, payload generation, reverse connection or command injection was executed or added as a utility default.

Pages 19â€“22 show the daemon/kernel relationship and configuration. The screenshot identifies Quagga 0.99.24.1 later on page 25; modern FRR commands and configuration storage must not be silently treated as identical. The page-21 configuration explicitly contains connected-route redistribution and outbound maps. BGP path choice is policy-driven; its best marker does not establish minimum latency or successful forwarding. The routing daemon's view, Zebra installation and Linux lookup answer different questions.

Pages 23â€“26 show FTP control login followed by an illegal PORT result, prefix selection and interface-address changes. Existing FTP guidance already separates a successful login from a failed data channel. The narrative's better-metrics explanation does not explain selection between a /24 and /25: longest-prefix matching applies to those distinct installed prefixes. A received more-specific route can cover only part of the aggregate. The later /32 example likewise differs from the connected /24. Co-hosting alone does not make newly assigned addresses propagate via BGP; the earlier redistribution configuration and export policies are material conditions. A running-config diff cannot by itself account for kernel addresses changed externally with iproute2.

Pages 27â€“28 show a simulated FTP listener, captured credentials, an SSH login, a no-export route map and remote routing observations. The no-export community is not no-advertise: internal propagation remains possible, and the actual external boundary is the AS/confederation. The final screenshot also denies the selected prefix on the other neighbor's outbound map; tagging one peer does not automatically suppress the originator's separate advertisements to every other peer. The receiving router's selected route and the other router's absent-prefix result are stronger case evidence than configuration text alone, but still describe those observations at those times. No route origination, address assignment, route-map edit, BGP clear/save, interception, credential collection/use or target authentication was performed. The synthetic FTP response loop was not promoted as a complete standards-compliant FTP server.

**Primary verification.** [FRR VTY shell documentation](https://docs.frrouting.org/en/latest/vtysh.html) and the [upstream vtysh manual](https://github.com/FRRouting/frr/blob/master/doc/manpages/vtysh.rst) support daemon selection, batch commands, pager and error-status behavior, socket authority and configuration distinctions. [FRR BGP documentation](https://docs.frrouting.org/en/latest/bgp.html) supports explicit address-family commands, summary/policy interpretation, per-prefix/outbound views and origination/redistribution conditions. [Zebra documentation](https://docs.frrouting.org/en/latest/zebra.html) supports route legends, installation and kernel-versus-configuration state. Legacy syntax and policy selection were checked in [Quagga's BGP manual source](https://github.com/Quagga/quagga/blob/master/doc/bgpd.texi) and [VTY source documentation](https://github.com/Quagga/quagga/blob/master/doc/vtysh.texi). [RFC 1997](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1997.html) resolves no-export versus no-advertise scope.

The iproute2 project's manuals as published on man7 support [route get/fibmatch and filtering](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man8/ip-route.8.html), [rule priorities](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man8/ip-rule.8.html), [address-created routes and noprefixroute](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man8/ip-address.8.html), and [namespace/brief options](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man8/ip.8.html). Attempted raw GitHub iproute2 paths returned 404 and the kernel.org raw view was inaccessible to the web reader; those failures were not counted as reviewed sources. The nongnu Quagga HTML request timed out; its upstream source documentation was successfully read instead.

[RFC 6933](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6933.html) supplies ENTITY-MIB field/index and empty-value semantics; [Net-SNMP snmpwalk](https://www.net-snmp.org/docs/man/snmpwalk.html) and [snmpcmd](https://www.net-snmp.org/docs/man/snmpcmd.html) support subtree selection, numeric output and query options. No live agent inventory was queried.

**Earlier SNMP source trail retained.** Documentation reviewed 2026-10-09; Linux execution untested. Case: Antique pp. 3â€“5. Sources: [snmpwalk](https://www.net-snmp.org/docs/man/snmpwalk.html), [snmpcmd](https://www.net-snmp.org/docs/man/snmpcmd.html), [BITS formatter](https://github.com/net-snmp/net-snmp/blob/master/snmplib/mib.c).

An earlier sandboxed Git read reported that it was not in a work tree; it was not counted as a successful check. No checker or packaging script was changed.

Vtysh, ip, snmpwalk and snmpget were absent from this Windows PATH. Examples and interpretations are documentation-reviewed; illustrative output is identified in the skills. Added a bounded legacy-Python workflow, distinguishing it from the Go rewrite, custom attribute visibility from object scope, printer encoding from stored values and the upstream cleanup exit status from query failure. |

The catalogue now contains **142 independent skills**. Required instructions remain inside each folder; no umbrella or shared runtime resource was added. Existing base64, SMB, OpenSSL CBC and Evil-WinRM coverage was reviewed without repeating those workflows.

**Page coverage and corrections.** Pages 1â€“4 establish the case, scan, anonymous LDAP query and an application-specific legacy attribute. Successful anonymous bind or RootDSE discovery does not establish visibility of every user or attribute. The source's `--full` selects attributes, not expanded object scope. The legacy Python code connects using plaintext LDAP and has no TLS switch; its independent skill deliberately directs authenticated transport requirements to an appropriate client. Its printer may itself base64-encode binary values, and its common cleanup exits 1 after successful searches. Neither behavior should be inferred from generic LDIF or generic exit-status conventions.

Pages 5â€“9 show different SMB share permissions, an email, deletion logs, a VNC registry value and subsequent remoting. Page 7's screenshot lists an empty user directory and another containing VNCInstall.reg, which text extraction entirely missed. SMB authentication, share access and WinRM endpoint authorization are separate observations. `WinRMAuthorizationError` alone does not prove a particular missing group; the later successful session is separate evidence. Display names of custom domain groups are not universal rights assignments. The fixed-key VNC DES/Rex IRB recipe was read but deliberately not promoted into a general msfconsole skill: it is a narrow library invocation requiring product/version and encoding validation, not substantial supported console operation. No secret from the registry, LDAP or screenshots was used in testing.

Pages 10â€“12 connect a logon script to the Audit$ share and a batch file to a particular SQLite database plus .NET executable and DLLs. The executable's database argument and selected record provide identity/context; a field named Pwd is not a format specification. Unreadable base64-decoded bytes do not by themselves prove encryption. The GUI sqlitebrowser/Wine/dnSpy workflow was adapted to existing SQLite/ILSpy Linux CLI skills without installing or executing those applications.

Pages 13â€“15 show `SELECT * FROM LDAP`, one unchecked reader `Read()`, and a decrypt helper in CascCrypto.dll. With multiple rows, missing ORDER BY leaves selection unspecified; with no rows, the unchecked reader call is a separate failure. The helper decodes base64, converts literal key/IV strings to UTF-8 bytes and selects CBC. The displayed code omits a padding assignment; standard .NET symmetric-algorithm defaults use PKCS7. It allocates plaintext storage to ciphertext length, ignores the returned read count and decodes the whole buffer, which can append NULs. A corrected read-through-EOF format check must not be described as exact reproduction of the historical framework/application. The source's low-level pyaes decrypt-and-decode does not explicitly remove padding; the existing OpenSSL AES reference already covers raw CBC versus validated PKCS7 rather than adding a duplicate recipe. The narrative's ArcSvc spelling differs from the database/session identity ArkSvc.

Pages 16â€“17 show a custom domain group named AD Recycle Bin and readable deleted-object data. The group's name alone establishes neither feature state nor delegated rights. Recycle Bin data cannot be restored indefinitely: deletion state, retention, garbage collection, schema and ACLs matter. Show Deleted and Show Recycled expose different states. DisplayName is neither a unique key nor guaranteed retained; the accepted procedure preserves DN/GUID identity. A historical password-reuse email is a hypothesis about another account, with the later successful login providing separate source-case evidence. No restoration, permission change, credential reuse or authentication was attempted here.

**Primary verification.** The [Python windapsearch repository](https://github.com/ropnop/windapsearch) and [implementation](https://github.com/ropnop/windapsearch/blob/master/windapsearch.py) support the implementation distinction, bind/transport, attribute overrides, DN output, value formatting, paging and cleanup status. [OpenLDAP's ldapsearch manual source](https://github.com/openldap/openldap/blob/master/doc/man/man1/ldapsearch.1) supports arbitrary critical search controls, paging, scope and StartTLS options. An attempted OpenLDAP manual CGI URL was unavailable; the upstream manual source was successfully read instead.

Microsoft's [Show Deleted OID definition](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/previous-versions/windows/desktop/ldap/ldap-server-show-deleted-oid), [control comparison](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/openspecs/windows_protocols/ms-adts/4b8f2a0e-9ea6-4be3-b639-4b019948c568), [Show Recycled control](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/openspecs/windows_protocols/ms-adts/b96e43b0-2783-4597-b7a0-f3f3f458cbf6), [retrieval guidance](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/ad/retrieving-deleted-objects), [object lifecycle](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/openspecs/windows_protocols/ms-adts/538ec135-1465-4266-bb0a-777924f02846) and [container access documentation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/troubleshoot/windows-server/active-directory/non-administrators-view-deleted-object-container) distinguish search visibility, retained data and effective permissions. No AD directory was queried.

The .NET runtime sources for [CipherMode](https://github.com/dotnet/runtime/blob/main/src/libraries/System.Security.Cryptography/src/System/Security/Cryptography/CipherMode.cs), [CryptoStreamMode](https://github.com/dotnet/runtime/blob/main/src/libraries/System.Security.Cryptography/src/System/Security/Cryptography/CryptoStreamMode.cs) and [SymmetricAlgorithm defaults](https://github.com/dotnet/runtime/blob/main/src/libraries/System.Security.Cryptography/src/System/Security/Cryptography/SymmetricAlgorithm.cs), together with Microsoft's [.NET 6 stream-read compatibility note](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/core/compatibility/core-libraries/6.0/partial-byte-reads-in-streams), support the crypto interpretation. Direct API-page requests for CryptoStream.Read/Padding failed; those unavailable pages were not counted as reviewed. [SQLite SELECT ordering](https://www.sqlite.org/lang_select.html) and [scalar functions](https://www.sqlite.org/lang_corefunc.html) support the row-selection, type and byte-count guidance.

**Earlier SQLite source trail retained.** Documentation reviewed 2026-10-09; Linux execution untested. Case: Artificial pp. 6, 8. Sources: [SQLite CLI](https://sqlite.org/cli.html), [WAL behavior](https://sqlite.org/wal.html), [backup API](https://sqlite.org/backup.html).

**Checks actually run.** Four focused skill validators passed. Repository checks passed for **142 packages, 179 Markdown files, 227 local links and 312 Bash blocks**, syntax only; README catalogue membership matched all 142 folders and `git diff --check` passed. An initial sandboxed Bash run failed with MSYS `NtCreateDirectoryObject` access-denied errors; it was not counted as a syntax pass. The same checker succeeded in the authorized repository context, without changing system settings or checker code.

Five inert Windows fixture checks passed. PowerShell 7.6.0 on .NET 10.0.5 verified CBC/Read enum values and default PKCS7, a complete 12-byte public plaintext roundtrip, and four appended NULs when a 16-byte ciphertext-sized buffer was decoded in full. It did not force short reads or reproduce the historical target framework. Python 3.12.10 with SQLite library 3.49.1 verified the documented metadata query against text/BLOB/NULL rows and distinguished a missing row from a present NULL field. That was the Python library, not sqlite3 CLI execution. Only synthetic public inputs were used.

Windapsearch, ldapsearch, ILSpyCmd and sqlite3 were absent from PATH. No relevant CLI invocation against a target, source application/database/assembly execution, credential recovery/use, Linux fixture or worker evaluation occurred. Extraction, all page images, pre-edit snapshots, structural results and relocated history remain in ignored work; synthetic test artifacts remain in ignored runs. Original PDFs were preserved; no tool installation, system change, commit or push occurred.


### 30. Cat.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **19 physical PDF pages** were rendered and individually read visually, including every displayed command, code excerpt, browser image and result. Extraction reported 25,533 characters without warnings, but Windows antivirus blocked access to the extracted text. That file was left untouched; no protection setting was changed and no alternative text extraction was used to bypass the block. Full-page visual review supplied the reading coverage. Source: Hack The Box, *Cat*, prepared by ctrlzero, machine author FisMatHack, cover dated 10 June 2025, classified Official. SHA-256: `f2bd8f66a4199202b507eb23f39292bdcd8cb2dd321ad30f303e566dfb06ad4d`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `git-dumper` | 2â€“3 show HTTP recovery of exposed Git data despite a denied directory listing. Added bounded acquisition, isolation for automatic checkout, transport/config caveats and missing/dangling-object interpretation. |
| New `sendmail` | 18 submits a local message to request a repository review. Added the independently usable Sendmail 8 submission interface, explicit envelope recipients, message construction, dot handling and queue evidence. No message was sent here. |
| Enriched `php` | 7â€“11 compare output encoding on separate pages and concatenated versus bound SQL. Added a harmless CLI encoding check with explicit flags/charset and guidance to trace each field and statement to its actual use. |
| Enriched `john` | 13â€“14 export database identities/digests and use Raw-MD5. Added the format decision and record-association guidance to the existing bounded offline workflow. |

The catalogue now contains **144 independent skills**. New packages have no required files outside their folders. Existing Nmap, ffuf, grep, base64, SQLite, curl, Python, SSH, key inspection, permissions and listener guidance covers the remaining ordinary CLI uses; it was not duplicated solely to add examples.

**Page coverage and corrections.** Pages 1â€“3 give the attack narrative, a top-1000 TCP scan, exposed Git metadata, the downloader and web enumeration. The scan's two open ports describe that tested set, not all possible listeners. A denied `.git/` index does not make individual objects inaccessible. Current upstream git-dumper invokes Git checkout automatically, modifies potentially unsafe config lines and warns that attacker-controlled content can execute locally. Its normal HTTP sessions disable certificate verification, and its non-directory-listing path ignores checkout status. The accepted skill therefore separates endpoint identity, isolation, transfer status and recovered-object completeness; no downloader invocation was performed. Config sanitization also means its output cannot be described as an untouched copy of every source byte.

Pages 4â€“6 show registration, a GET request with credentials in its query string and an upload result. HTTP method alone does not establish the backend's account-lookup/create logic. A query string can be recorded by the configured log format; it is not proof that every service logs passwords. A success message and an uploaded filename are distinct from a complete image decode and successful review.

Pages 7â€“9 compare a blacklist and different HTML output paths, then build an event-handler payload. The prose sometimes calls this a filename injection, but the illustrated altered `alt` attribute and multipart `cat_name` field show the display-name flow; the image filename is separate. The accepted PHP guidance preserves that distinction and explains why encoding on the admin list does not establish encoding on the detail page. Browser entity decoding, event triggering, cookie collection and image corruption were read as source evidence and were not executed or packaged as a new CLI utility. Existing binary/upload guidance already separates GIF/PNG labels and prefixes from valid image content.

Pages 10â€“12 show the recovered browser session, a concatenated INSERT, separately bound DELETE, SQLite ATTACH statements and a web-shell/reverse-connection sequence. The later bound statement does not protect the earlier concatenation. ATTACH is a database/file operation, not intrinsic OS command execution: the historical outcome also depends on the application's SQL API, filesystem permissions, served path and PHP execution mapping. The initial generic file path and adapted application path differ. Binary-looking response prefixes should be captured to a file rather than forced to a terminal. No SQL injection, database write, web shell, server, reverse connection or payload execution was attempted.

Pages 13â€“15 export users, transform delimiters, run John, inspect groups and read Apache logs. Raw-MD5 was not selected from length alone in the new guidance; identical-length NT or other records require origin evidence. The displayed export has ten rows while the loader reports nine records; the source does not supply enough run state to resolve that difference. The skill already separates loader input, duplicate/pot state and `--show` results, now with an explicit record-association reminder. A home-directory name or database account is not automatically an OS login identity. The log directory and files show group ownership plus usable permission bits; the name `adm` alone is not a universal read grant. A logged submitted value and a redirect response are not proof of authentication; later successful source-case `su` is separate evidence. No hashes or logged credentials were used here.

Pages 15â€“17 contain mail clues, local services, key creation and forwarding, then a Gitea 1.22.0 browser demonstration. The messages are task data, not permission to send mail or change accounts. The source overwrites authorized_keys with `cp` and later names a different local key file without showing its transfer; neither was copied into a setup recipe. Existing SSH guidance already gives explicit loopback binds and connection checks. Gitea's official 1.22.1 announcement identifies CVE-2024-6886 and its fix; the historical footer alone is not a current vulnerability verdict.

Pages 18â€“19 send a review link, fetch a private-repository file through a browser session, decode a not-found response, then fetch a different file and reuse a discovered value. The mail clue names `README.md`, while the failed request uses `readme.md`; case-sensitive repository paths are a plausible additional explanation, so the failure does not establish absence of the named README. The script does not preserve the original fetch status. A callback's 200 is the collector's response status, and successful base64 decoding can merely recover an error body. Existing curl/base64 guidance covers that evidence distinction. Browser-session actions, private-file exfiltration and root credential reuse were deliberately not turned into utility defaults or executed. Sendmail instructions support an independently authorized message and explicitly distinguish queueing from delivery/opening.

**Primary verification.** [Git-dumper documentation](https://github.com/arthaud/git-dumper) and [implementation](https://github.com/arthaud/git-dumper/blob/master/git_dumper.py) support URL normalization, denied-listing recovery, request options, TLS behavior, file skipping, config rewriting and automatic checkout/status handling. [Git fsck](https://git-scm.com/docs/git-fsck) defines object checks and missing/dangling diagnoses. [GNU timeout source/help](https://github.com/coreutils/coreutils/blob/master/src/timeout.c) supports the outer deadline, interrupt/kill interval and exit statuses; the GNU manual page was unavailable. A sandboxed local timeout help invocation returned nonzero without usable help, so it was not counted as runtime validation.

The [Sendmail manual distributed in FreeBSD's source tree](https://github.com/freebsd/freebsd-src/blob/stable/13/contrib/sendmail/src/sendmail.8) supports `-i`, `--`, `-t`, envelope sender/recipient distinctions, queue listing versus processing, and exit interpretation. Attempts to retrieve the Postfix manual failed, so the package explicitly scopes its administrative guidance to Sendmail 8 rather than claiming universal compatibility.

PHP's [htmlspecialchars manual](https://www.php.net/manual/en/function.htmlspecialchars.php), [CLI options](https://www.php.net/manual/en/features.commandline.options.php) and [PDO prepare documentation](https://www.php.net/manual/en/pdo.prepare.php) support the explicit encoding check, PHP 8.1 default change and actual parameter-binding boundary. [John's raw MD5 implementation](https://github.com/openwall/john/blob/bleeding-jumbo/src/rawMD5_fmt_plug.c) confirms format label, accepted representations and unsalted digest semantics. [SQLite ATTACH documentation](https://www.sqlite.org/lang_attach.html), [Apache log-format documentation](https://httpd.apache.org/docs/2.4/mod/mod_log_config.html) and [Gitea's 1.22.1 announcement](https://blog.gitea.com/release-of-1.22.1/) bound source-case claims without asserting target execution.

**Checks actually run.** All four focused skill validators passed. Repository checks passed for **144 packages, 181 Markdown files, 229 local links and 318 Bash blocks**, syntax only; README catalogue membership matched all 144 folders and `git diff --check` passed. No packaging/checker code changed.

Three inert Windows Git 2.55.0.windows.5 checks passed using public synthetic blobs and an index: a present indexed blob passed fsck, a dangling blob was reported without failure, and an index reference to a missing blob caused failure. The isolated fixture created no commits and used no remote, credentials, acquired config or application content. Git-dumper, Sendmail, PHP and John were absent from PATH; their runtime behavior remains documentation-reviewed. No downloader, message submission, PHP application, password recovery, target authentication, Linux execution or worker evaluation occurred. All page renders and structural evidence remain in ignored work; public fixture results remain in ignored runs. Original PDFs were preserved, with no tool installation, system/protection change, commit or push.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `apktool` | 3â€“5 and 15â€“17 motivate resource inspection and an edited-copy rebuild. Covers DEX-preserving `-s`, private framework cache, incomplete resources, SDK-field distinctions, decoded-directory build input and independent Android signature verification. |
| New `jadx` | 3â€“5 show a WebView entry activity and resource-only token search. Translates GUI steps into CLI export/class selection, keeps resource hits connected to consumers/destinations and gives recovery branches for inaccurate/missing decompilation. |

**Page coverage and source qualifications.** Page 1 supplies the synopsis/attribution; page 2 the full TCP scan and five service endpoints. Port-name guesses do not identify the applications. Pages 3â€“5 show the APK, a valid v1 signature display, entry-activity code and three token resources. The certificate's subject organization is not independent publisher proof. The WebView destination alone does not establish how each token is used or whether it remains valid.

Pages 6â€“7 show a hardcoded Swagger base URL and Gitea 1.14.1 API page. The `/api/v1/user` call is about the current authenticated user, not a user-directory listing. The displayed body says `token is required`; it supports failed authentication for that request, not the synopsis's stronger assertion that a once-valid token has expired or that no other progress is possible. Host/routing, actual auth handling and status would need separate evidence. Pages 7â€“9 show Let's Chat room/message responses and a later Cachet login. Listing three room names without response metadata does not establish exhaustive visibility or pagination coverage. No tokens or passwords were copied into portable examples or tested.

Pages 10â€“12 show Cachet 2.4.0-dev, a configuration-change/session-store chain, a Redis key overwrite, PHP gadget output and a callback. `SET` returning OK concerns the store write; the subsequent application response is separate execution evidence. An error page can contain command output. The document's one-off quote substitutions in serialized data are not a general byte-safe transfer method. Redis discovery/type/scope decisions and HTTP status/body preservation are already covered by existing skills, so no redundant recipes were added. Pages 12â€“14 show a container clue, application database configuration and later SSH access; database identity and OS account identity are separate, with reuse a case-specific subsequent result. The pspy excerpt shows UID 0 at a single displayed timestamp, while the claimed every-minute recurrence comes from the prose; the excerpt alone does not prove the period.

Pages 15â€“16 contain the complete custom APK-processing script. Its name-substring check does not establish origin. The compatibility branch reads compile SDK but labels its failure as target SDK; neither is interchangeable with minimum runtime SDK. The decode exit status is not checked before consuming files, and failure branches call bare `exit` after successful `echo`, so their status is not a reliable failure signal. The xargs replacement constructs a new shell program even though its template is single-quoted in the parent. Quoted cleanup asterisks remain literal; another cleanup derives relative names from a directory listing, making its effect depend on working directory. None of these cleanup or payload commands was executed or adopted.

Page 17 shows Apktool 2.6.1 decoding with `-s`, a resource edit and rebuilding, but no re-signing step. The original APK's signature does **not** authenticate rebuilt resources. Source review and local inert tests show why a `jarsigner -verify` zero-only gate can accept unsigned content; the original target's exact JDK behavior remains untested. The final screenshot shows real UID 1000 and effective UID 0, not a change of the real UID to root. It is source-case evidence, not reproduction here.

**Primary verification, reviewed 2026-10-10.** Apktool [2.x options](https://apktool.org/docs/2.x/cli-parameters/), [3.x options](https://apktool.org/docs/cli-parameters/), [decoding](https://apktool.org/wiki/the-basics/decoding/), [building](https://apktool.org/wiki/the-basics/building/) and [framework guidance](https://apktool.org/wiki/in-depth/frameworks/) support the bounded decode/rebuild workflow and signature distinction. Android [build configuration](https://developer.android.com/build), [SDK attributes](https://developer.android.com/guide/topics/manifest/uses-sdk-element) and [apksigner](https://developer.android.com/tools/apksigner) support metadata/API-range and signature interpretation. JADX [CLI documentation](https://github.com/skylot/jadx) and [troubleshooting](https://github.com/skylot/jadx/wiki/Troubleshooting-Q%26A) support resource/class selection and incomplete-code recovery. Oracle's [jarsigner manual](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/25/docs/specs/man/jarsigner.html) and the [OpenJDK 21 verifier source](https://github.com/openjdk/jdk21u/blob/master/src/jdk.jartool/share/classes/sun/security/tools/jarsigner/Main.java) distinguish warnings, entry coverage and unsigned/no-manifest behavior. GNU's [xargs manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man1/xargs.1.html), the source script and local argument fixtures support the Bash addition. Initial guessed Apktool documentation paths failed; the working official wiki/CLI pages above were used. GNU's online Bash page was inaccessible; the quoted-wildcard conclusion was directly checked locally.

**Checks actually run.** All four focused skill validators passed. Repository checks passed for **147 packages, 184 Markdown files, 232 local links and 327 Bash blocks**, syntax only. Catalogue membership matches all 147 folders. `git diff --check` passed; existing CRLF normalization warnings are informational. No packaging/checker code changed.

Seven inert Windows checks passed. With Temurin OpenJDK **21.0.12.1+1**, a public unsigned ZIP **without a manifest** returned zero both normally and under `-strict`, while reporting unsigned; an unsigned ZIP **with a manifest** returned zero normally and **16** under strict mode; an invalid archive returned **1**. These checks created no keys, signed no artifact and executed no archive content. Two Git Bash **5.3.15(2)-release** printf-only checks confirmed xargs replacement inside an already-literal argument and a quoted wildcard remaining literal. The string shaped like a mkdir command was printed, never executed. Raw results remain in ignored `runs/overnight-ingest-2026-10-10/Catch/`; extracted text, page renders and structural evidence remain in ignored work.

Apktool, JADX and apksigner were absent from PATH; APK decode/build/Android verification remains documentation-reviewed. No signed-archive fixture, source APK, Redis service, gadget, callback, privileged process, target authentication, Linux execution or worker evaluation occurred.
