| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `ldbsearch` | 13â€“14 motivate structured inspection of an SSSD cache after a strings-only excerpt loses user association. This is an independently documented improvement to that workflow, not a command shown in the PDF. Covers copied-cache selection, explicit local database, DN/attribute context, selected-record queries, missing/stale data and LDIF representation. |
| Enriched `strings` | 14 uses sorting and truncation on cache text. Adds why a password-like string and nearby username cannot establish a logical record; directs the worker to a format-aware reader. |
| Enriched `hashcat` | 14 supplies a complete `$6$` verifier and mode 1800. Adds sha512crypt representation, optional rounds preservation and the limits of cached-verifier recovery. |
| Enriched `openssl` | 6â€“9 compare key generation, PEM acceptance and saved content. Adds PKCS#1/PKCS#8/OpenSSH distinctions, non-secret consistency/public inspection and parser acceptance versus whole-file validation. |
| Enriched `python3` | 19â€“20 and 25 motivate offline SAML Redirect decoding and separating request ID, response endpoint and issuer identity. A skill-local conditional reference contains a bounded standard-library decoder, recovery decisions and authentication-evidence limits. |

**Page coverage and source qualifications.** Page 1 supplies the synopsis and attribution. Page 2's screenshot lists both TCP 8080 and 60288, despite prose saying only 8080 is open. The scan is dated July 2023, after the cover date. Apache/Ubuntu output from a container and a Windows service guess do not conflict in a way that identifies one unambiguous host OS. Pages 3â€“5 show a Python file-read requester, Linux hosts data and Icinga database/authentication configuration. The requester is not a general execution shell; text conversion, missing timeout/status handling and response interpretation limit its evidential use. Database credentials, application administration and OS identities are separate, with any reuse requiring its own evidence.

Pages 6â€“7 show RSA key generation and an SSH identity resource. The UI presents a traditional RSA label while the retrieved file is shown with a PKCS#8 label; the source does not establish byte preservation or why serialization changed. Compatibility depends on the consumed format rather than the generator's name. The failed null-byte attempt alone does not establish that a particular OpenSSL fix was installed. Pages 8â€“10 show acceptance of surrounding text, PHP content, a written resource and module configuration. The prose/file names and displayed payloads differ in places; later module loading is a separate execution step. No payload was adopted or run. The portable addition addresses how to inspect a key and interpret parser success, rather than reproducing the application chain.

Pages 11â€“13 show a callback, SUID enumeration, Firejail 0.9.68rc1, a join exploit and subsequent root identity inside the container. The displayed executable is `/usr/bin/firejail`; the prose has `/user/bin/firejail`. The join command alone does not prove privilege gain: the screenshot also uses `su -`. A version string by itself omits vendor patches, SUID state, namespace conditions and exploit prerequisites. The process listing shows SSSD and a configured domain backend, which is not independent proof of healthy AD membership. The absence of one secrets-store file says nothing universal about the separate identity/authentication cache.

Page 14 shows `strings` output from an LDB cache after `sort -u | head`, then a successful source-case Hashcat run. The excerpt does not structurally associate the verifier with the named user. Preserving a DN/record and attribute is the accepted improvement. A sha512crypt cache verifier is neither an NT hash nor a Kerberos ticket; testing a candidate against it does not establish current online access. Pages 14â€“17 then show a container address, payload/handler setup, a SOCKS service, a route to one host using a /32 mask, and later WinRM identity evidence. Route creation and transport connection are not destination reachability tests; different callback addresses appear across the source. The route covers the selected host, not automatically the whole subnet.

Pages 17â€“19 show local listeners, PID mapping and reverse SOCKS. The screenshot maps PID 5672 to Java and PID 2072 to Microsoft.ActiveDirectory.WebServices, rather than making both Java as the prose suggests. The Chisel command's reverse remote is `R:8888:socks`; a prose occurrence omits its second colon. Reverse capability does not transparently proxy all traffic, and consumer/DNS configuration determines what traverses the listener. Existing `chisel` already covers the transport/listener/destination distinction and reverse mode, so it was not expanded again.

Pages 19â€“20 contain two different encoded SAMLRequest values; they must not be silently treated as one capture. The displayed XML is an AuthnRequest, not an assertion. Its request ID differs from the application-specific identifier embedded in its AssertionConsumerServiceURL. The request issuer identifies its sender; it does not supply the separate IdP issuer used later. Page 21 shows module inputs, while pages 22â€“24 show acquisition and extraction of an `.ezip` backup. The 7-Zip screenshot identifies a 7z container with LZMA2/7zAES; the extension is not the format. A password prompt alone does not establish failure. The described filename-derived password is historical application behavior, not a general archive rule. A working-directory change also leaves an archive-path/copy step unstated. Existing `7z` already covers actual-format inspection, prompts, fresh extraction destinations and incomplete evidence.

Page 25 locates an issuer value in a backup configuration table. That identifies the stored configuration at backup time; it does not establish the current live setting or make a URI-shaped entity identifier a URL that should be fetched or rewritten. Page 26 reports an inconclusive automatic service check and later shows a SYSTEM identity. These are distinct evidence stages, and neither was reproduced here.

**Primary verification, reviewed 2026-10-10.** The [upstream ldbsearch manual](https://github.com/samba-team/samba/blob/master/lib/ldb/man/ldbsearch.1.xml), [search implementation](https://github.com/samba-team/samba/blob/master/lib/ldb/tools/ldbsearch.c) and [shared command-line implementation](https://github.com/samba-team/samba/blob/master/lib/ldb/tools/cmdline.c) support explicit database selection, scopes, attributes and LDIF output. Search mode prevents database creation, but it was not described as a guaranteed immutable-storage mode; the skill uses a disposable copy. SSSD [cache troubleshooting](https://sssd.io/troubleshooting/backend.html), its [local LDB query example](https://sssd.io/troubleshooting/sudo.html), [attribute definitions](https://github.com/SSSD/sssd/blob/master/src/db/sysdb.h) and [cached-authentication design](https://sssd.io/design-pages/cached_authentication.html) support cache/identity context. [RFC 2849](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2849.html) verifies LDIF folding and base64 representation. Initial guessed legacy SSSD/Samba documentation URLs were inaccessible; current upstream sources above were used.

The [GNU strings manual](https://sourceware.org/binutils/docs/binutils/strings.html) and the source pipeline support the extraction-versus-record distinction. Hashcat's [mode 1800 implementation](https://github.com/hashcat/hashcat/blob/master/src/modules/module_01800.c) verifies sha512crypt input. OpenSSL's [pkey](https://docs.openssl.org/3.5/man1/openssl-pkey/) and [genrsa](https://docs.openssl.org/3.5/man1/openssl-genrsa/) manuals, [RFC 7468](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7468.html) and the [OpenSSH private-key format](https://github.com/openssh/openssh-portable/blob/master/PROTOCOL.key) support the key-inspection distinctions. OASIS [SAML bindings](https://docs.oasis-open.org/security/saml/v2.0/saml-bindings-2.0-os.pdf), [SAML core](https://docs.oasis-open.org/security/saml/v2.0/saml-core-2.0-os.pdf), and Python [urllib.parse](https://docs.python.org/3.12/library/urllib.parse.html), [base64](https://docs.python.org/3.12/library/base64.html) and [zlib](https://docs.python.org/3.12/library/zlib.html) verify the offline decoder and field interpretation. These references support the accepted bounded tasks; no new exploit applicability was claimed.

**Relocated earlier history.** The `strings` footer previously recorded documentation review on 2026-10-09, Linux execution untested, Appsanity p. 22 adapted from a Windows strings workflow, and the GNU manual above. That source trail is retained here; its operational documentation link remains in the skill.

**Checks actually run.** All five focused skill validators passed. Repository checks passed for **148 packages, 186 Markdown files, 234 local links and 331 Bash blocks**, syntax only. Catalogue membership matches all 148 folders. `git diff --check` passed in the explicit repository context; existing CRLF normalization warnings are informational. No checker or packaging implementation changed.

Sixteen inert Windows fixture checks passed. Python **3.12.10** ran the exact embedded decoder against synthetic local inputs: one valid Redirect with escaped plus characters, duplicate request, unsupported encoding, invalid base64, already-decoded plus corruption, truncated stream, trailing data, zlib-wrapped input, output-expansion limit, input-size limit, and existing-destination preservation. The successful case retained exact XML bytes; the failures produced no new decoded file and the existing output remained unchanged. This checked decoding, not SAML signature validation or authentication.

Windows OpenSSL **3.5.7** generated a fresh 2048-bit fixture key and confirmed five outcomes: default PKCS#8 labeling; successful consistency/public-only inspection; traditional PKCS#1 conversion retaining the same public DER bytes; acceptance of harmless explanatory text outside PEM boundaries; and rejection of malformed key input. No source private key was copied, no artifact was signed, and no authentication or target connection occurred. Raw fixtures remain in ignored `runs/overnight-ingest-2026-10-10/Cerberus/`; PDF text, page images and structural evidence remain in ignored work.

`ldbsearch`, Hashcat and GNU strings were absent from PATH. No original cache, SSSD instance, Linux command execution, Hashcat recovery, database repair or agent evaluation was performed. The LDB workflow remains documentation-reviewed. Deliberately deferred material: Icinga arbitrary-file/module execution, the Firejail privilege exploit, payload/handler setup, credential reuse, password guessing for backups and ADSelfService Plus SAML exploitation. These source chains do not need additional portable execution recipes to support the accepted artifact-inspection tasks. Original PDFs and unrelated methodologies/edits were preserved; no installation, system/configuration change, deletion, commit or push occurred.


### 33. Cereal.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **21 physical PDF pages** were text-read, rendered and individually inspected, including source code, browser storage, GraphQL schema, build and identity screenshots. Extraction yielded 26,245 characters with recovered object-offset warnings for objects 7 and 33; visual inspection covered every page. Source: Hack The Box, *Cereal*, prepared by MinatoTW, machine author Micah, cover dated 26 May 2021, document D21.100.120. SHA-256: `b69d3e650c034d7bc53ccfaea2d12eaeb798930cf6d5aa191352e5184a434d7a`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| Enriched `curl` | 16â€“17 and 20 motivate a skill-local GraphQL reference: inspect root/type names, typed variables and result shapes; distinguish transport status, request errors, partial data and Boolean results. The portable introspection requests are independently documented adaptations, not copied source commands. |
| Enriched `git` | 4â€“6 use `git log -p` to recover an earlier literal argument. Adds a bounded displayed-patch workflow using `-G`, explaining why `-S` can miss replacement of a value when the marker occurrence count stays constant. |
| Enriched `jwt-tool` | 4â€“8 distinguish HMAC signing, framework claim mapping, returned application JSON, browser storage and actual request authentication. Adds localStorage-versus-cookie/header and UI-field-versus-wire-claim decisions. |
| Enriched `dotnet` | 18â€“19 show a .NET Framework 4.5 retarget prompt and a Windows build. Adds Framework-versus-modern-.NET target, retargeting, platform and build-success distinctions. |

No new package was needed. README catalogue entries were updated; all four skills remain independently installable, with the conditional GraphQL material inside the curl folder.

**Page coverage and source qualifications.** Page 1 supplies synopsis and attribution. Its description of an earlier JWT key as an encryption key is imprecise: the subsequent source uses an HMAC signing key. Page 2 shows SSH, IIS HTTP/HTTPS and certificate hostnames. Certificate names are routing leads, not proof that each virtual host is currently served. Page 3 shows a compilation error disclosing a Windows source path, plus directory-scan redirects. A 301 for `/.git` is not proof of complete repository recovery; differently cased upload paths need not be distinct resources. Existing `git-dumper` already covers acquisition and repository completeness.

Pages 4â€“7 show authentication source, a historical signing-key literal, a later masked value and a token stored in a browser application object. Replacing the literal while retaining the same `Encoding.ASCII.GetBytes` marker motivates changed-line search instead of occurrence-count search. A historical value is not proof of a current valid key. The source calls the JWT a cookie, but the pictured workflow returns it in JSON and stores a `currentUser` object in localStorage; later requests attach an Authorization bearer header. The .NET `ClaimTypes.Name` source and `unique_name` wire claim can be related through configured claim mapping, not guessed from model/UI properties. Pages 7â€“8 show browser storage and a visible interface; those observations alone do not establish authorization for a protected server operation. No source token, signing key or credential was copied into a skill or used in a fixture.

Pages 8â€“10 show an IP-restriction policy name, a configured loopback whitelist, request-row deserialization with TypeNameHandling.Auto, a blacklist and DownloadHelper property setters. Configuration alone does not demonstrate the policy implementation or effective enforcement. A substring blacklist is not a general type-binding boundary. The helper acts after both properties are set; its filename-prefix check is not general filesystem containment validation. No deserialization payload was adopted or executed.

Pages 11â€“13 show Markdown rendering, a browser callback and a multi-stage application chain. Package age alone does not establish exploitability. A local Python HTTP-server GET returning 404 proves receipt of that request, not successful download, caller identity or execution. The printed Python authentication line on page 13 is visibly truncated, and one earlier GET example omits the request ID that appears in the automated version. Multiple quoting layers, disabled TLS verification and missing response-status checks further limit direct reuse. Existing curl, jq and python-http-server guidance already covers those general transport/construction distinctions. Page 14 shows returned row IDs, a web-shell identity and a SQLite file. Treating raw binary database output as text does not establish the relationship between a credential-like string and an account; existing SQLite/strings guidance is more suitable.

Page 15 shows a later SSH session, SeImpersonatePrivilege, Windows Server 2019 build information and an absent Spooler service on that specific host. Privilege presence, OS name or a version string alone is not a complete exploit prerequisite check. Page 16 shows a local SSH forward and an HTTP GraphQL endpoint. The existing SSH skill already explains that the forward connects to the remote loopback listener; it supplies neither application credentials nor proof of operation success. Introspection is a schema feature: its availability is not inherently a vulnerability.

Page 17 exposes query fields and an `updatePlant` mutation with required Int, Float and String arguments and a Boolean result. Those types determine value construction and selection-set use. The shown omitted comma between arguments is valid because GraphQL ignores commas. A callback in the source supports that particular fetch observation, not arbitrary reachability or downstream process identity. The new reference queries schema metadata only. Pages 18â€“19 show a Visual Studio .NET Framework 4.5 retarget prompt recommending 4.8, a Release/AnyCPU context and a successful Windows build. Build success does not establish runtime compatibility, and that retarget operation is not a way to make the program run on Linux. The Windows `wget` context is PowerShell, not evidence about GNU Wget semantics.

Page 20 shows a curl GraphQL mutation and GenericPotato output; page 21 shows a later GodPotato variant. The source changes a version argument between examples and shows no useful curl response body/status beside the mutation. A JSON envelope key named `query` can carry a mutation, and a Boolean response needs application-specific interpretation. Process logs and a callback shell are separate evidence stages; a `C:\Windows\system32` working directory alone does not identify the shell account. Neither Windows privilege chain was reproduced or generalized into a new skill.

**Primary verification, reviewed 2026-10-10.** Curl's [manual](https://curl.se/docs/manpage.html) verifies body/header files and response capture. GraphQL's [HTTP guidance](https://graphql.org/learn/serving-over-http/), [introspection](https://graphql.org/learn/introspection/), [query/variable rules](https://graphql.org/learn/queries/), [response semantics](https://graphql.org/learn/response/) and [October 2021 specification](https://spec.graphql.org/October2021/#sec-The-__Type-Type) verify the request envelope, selected operation, root/type fields, wrappers, default values, deprecated-field omission and data/error distinctions. Status behavior depends on implementation and negotiated response media type. The [jq manual](https://jqlang.org/manual/) confirms that `//` substitutes for false as well as null; jq itself was not changed or executed.

Git's [log manual](https://git-scm.com/docs/git-log) verifies changed-line `-G` versus occurrence-count `-S` filtering. Microsoft's [.NET runtime/platform guidance](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/standard/choosing-core-framework-server) and [target framework reference](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/standard/frameworks) verify the Framework/modern-.NET distinction. MDN's [localStorage reference](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Window/localStorage) and IdentityModel's [claim-mapping implementation](https://github.com/AzureAD/azure-activedirectory-identitymodel-extensions-for-dotnet/blob/dev/src/Microsoft.IdentityModel.JsonWebTokens/ClaimTypeMapping.cs) support storage and claim-name distinctions; actual mappings remain configuration-dependent. Python's [HTTP-server documentation](https://docs.python.org/3.12/library/http.server.html) supports the callback interpretation. These sources support the accepted CLI tasks, not new claims of exploit applicability.

**Relocated earlier history.** The dotnet footer previously recorded documentation review on 2026-10-09, Linux execution untested, Bagel pp. 7â€“8 and 23â€“26, and Backfire pp. 9â€“10. That trail is retained here. Its operational links to dotnet, dotnet-build, dotnet-run, global.json, MSBuild security and deployment documentation remain in the skill.

**Checks actually run.** All four focused skill validators passed. Repository checks passed for **148 packages, 187 Markdown files, 235 local links and 333 Bash blocks**, syntax only. Catalogue membership matches all 148 folders. A final docs/link check and `git diff --check` passed in the explicit repository context; existing CRLF normalization warnings are informational. No checker or packaging implementation changed.

Eight inert Windows fixture checks passed. Curl **8.21.0** sent the saved JSON requests to a synthetic loopback HTTP server and preserved method, request bytes, headers, body bytes and status in five cases: schema roots, an error-only HTTP 200, partial data/errors, an HTTP 400 error, and a harmless Boolean-false query. The fixture disabled curlrc, bypassed proxies only for loopback and used a ten-second cap. This tested HTTP transport and capture, **not a GraphQL engine, schema validation or resolver behavior**. Git **2.55.0.windows.5** ran three `diff --no-index` cases on public synthetic C# text: equal marker counts gave no `-S` patch, a changed matching line gave a `-G` patch, and an added marker occurrence gave a `-S` patch. The patch cases returned the expected diff status 1. This exercised the shared filtering mechanism; no repository/commit was created and no historical `git log` workflow was executed.

Raw fixtures remain in ignored `runs/overnight-ingest-2026-10-10/Cereal/`; PDF text, renders, snapshots and structural results remain in ignored work. No .NET build, JWT-tool execution, jq run, target request, authentication, Linux fixture or agent evaluation occurred. Deliberately deferred material: JWT forgery, stored browser injection, unsafe deserialization, web-shell deployment, credential reuse and the Windows impersonation chains. Original PDFs and unrelated methodologies/edits were preserved; no installation, system/configuration change, deletion, commit or push occurred.

| Enriched `tshark` | 14â€“16 supply a saved packet capture and GUI Kerberos dissection. Adds a CLI summary and selected-frame detail workflow that keeps the encrypted timestamp's fields separate from request-body encryption preferences and other nested values. |
| Enriched `hashcat` | 15â€“16 motivate mode 19900 for verified AES256 encrypted-timestamp pre-authentication records, with complete-record, identity and salt checks. No case secret or guessed conversion was retained. |
| Enriched `certipy` | 17â€“23 distinguish agent-issuing and destination templates, effective rights, missing directory email, failed request IDs and actual issuance/authentication stages. Earlier review history was moved here. |

README catalogue entries were updated. The new package has only its own SKILL.md and no shared runtime dependency. Existing unzip, LDAP, database, bcrypt, BloodHound and offline NTDS coverage already addresses related tasks and was not expanded with duplicate examples.

**Page coverage and source qualifications.** Pages 1â€“3 give the synopsis and AD/web-service scan. The output reports roughly eight hours of clock skew; it does not justify changing a worker's system clock. Hostnames and certificate names are evidence to reconcile with DNS/service identity. Pages 4â€“7 show registration, enrollment and the upload form, including its explicit allowance for ZIP-contained assignments. A browser's displayed allowed types is not alone proof of effective server validation.

Pages 8â€“10 compare a rejected ordinary PHP-containing ZIP with a modified archive, show edits to local/central filename metadata, an UnZip listing, a generated hyperlink and a later shell identity. The 400 response includes an invalid-extension explanation; it is stronger evidence than a bare status but does not fully describe every validator. The visible filename in `unzip -l` is not a raw-byte dump or proof of how another extractor handles the archive. The explanation that a NUL became a URL space is a source inference, not established parser behavior from the supplied server implementation. Existing unzip guidance already separates displayed names, exact bytes, extraction and application acceptance. No malformed archive or upload payload was created, uploaded or executed.

Pages 10â€“13 show database configuration, schema inspection, user rows, bcrypt recovery, domain enumeration and separate SMB/WinRM results. An application username, email suffix and role are not themselves AD identity or authorization. The database hash and domain authentication are distinct evidence stages. Existing mysql/hashcat/netexec/evil-winrm skills cover these decisions. Source passwords and hashes were not adopted or tested.

Pages 14â€“16 show a workstation problem description and a saved capture. The screenshot includes an initial AS-REQ, PREAUTH_REQUIRED response, another AS-REQ, an AS-REP, later TGS exchanges and an SMB error. That sequence does not support treating the initial pre-authentication error as a wrong password or a TGT response as successful SMB access. The selected request's type-2 encrypted timestamp uses encryption type 18, distinct from its list of desired encryption types. The screenshot shows a realm labelled `CERTIFICATE`, while the constructed recovery record uses a DNS-style domain; the supplied PDF contains no raw capture or complete salt evidence to independently validate that transformation. The source reports a successful recovery, but the new skill preserves observed fields and requires a version-appropriate converter rather than reproducing an unverified salt/realm assumption. The historical capture is not proof that a credential remains valid now.

Pages 16â€“18 show group membership, a group description, published templates and an enrollment-agent certificate. Descriptive text about issuing/revoking certificates is not an effective-rights check. An agent EKU plus enrollment permission is one part of the evidence; the destination template, subject rights and CA restrictions must also be considered. Pages 19â€“20 show error `CERTSRV_E_SUBJECT_EMAIL_REQUIRED`, required-email flags, a request ID despite failure and GetADUsers output. A populated table field establishes stored directory metadata, not a deliverable mailbox; a UPN does not substitute for `mail`. The printed requester domain is misspelled in both on-behalf-of commands, so those commands should not be copied as verified portable syntax. Upstream filtering also explains why `-all` matters when missing-email accounts are the question.

Pages 20â€“22 show SharpHound collection and BloodHound membership/description views. The group description suggests a management purpose, while the actual privilege observation appears later. A completed collector run is separate from graph import and current effective permissions. Windows SharpHound methods and server compatibility should not be assumed identical to the Linux BloodHound.py collector already covered in this repository.

Page 23 shows a successful certificate request, saved PFX, a later KDC exchange/cache and a credential-recovery result, followed by WinRM. The prose's phrase about converting a certificate to a ticket obscures the live authentication step. PFX filename capitalization also differs between prose and saved output; the portable task must use the actual Linux filename. Pages 24â€“26 show SeManageVolumePrivilege, permission changes, a DLL/reverse-shell implementation, a cross-compiler command and later NETWORK SERVICE identity. The root ACL excerpt and a tool's count of changed entries are not an inventory proving identical access to every file. Different callback/hosting addresses appear in code and output. The command uses a Windows-targeting cross-compiler, not an ordinary Linux executable build. No compiler skill was added from this payload-specific example.

Pages 27â€“28 include a protection-bypass snippet, a privilege exploit and a SYSTEM shell. The help output on page 27 alone is not successful exploitation; the later identity is separate evidence. The EFS explanation is not independently substantiated by an encryption metadata/read-error excerpt, and a privileged identity by itself does not prove possession of the required decryption key. Pages 28â€“30 create an IFM snapshot, copy hives/database through SMB and parse them offline. The proposed destination is under Temp while the displayed snapshot uses Public; preserve actual acquisition paths rather than assuming the transcripts are one exact command. Existing secretsdump guidance distinguishes LOCAL parsing, matching acquisitions, cache/LSA/NTDS record types and later authentication. No dump, share, original credential extraction or authentication was performed.

**Primary verification, reviewed 2026-10-10.** The [GetADUsers implementation](https://github.com/fortra/impacket/blob/master/examples/GetADUsers.py) and [connection/identity helpers](https://github.com/fortra/impacket/blob/master/impacket/examples/utils.py) verify the selector, cache flags, default/all filters, displayed attributes and exception handling. The parser uses `-all`; an old source comment says `-all-users` and was not followed. Microsoft's [lastLogon definition](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/adschema/a-lastlogon) establishes per-DC maintenance; [pwdLastSet](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/adschema/a-pwdlastset) documents its zero-value special case. The portable procedure explicitly requires installed support for its connection flags.

The [TShark manual](https://www.wireshark.org/docs/man-pages/tshark.html), [Kerberos field registry](https://www.wireshark.org/docs/dfref/k/kerberos.html) and [RFC 4120](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4120.html) verify offline display filtering, selected-frame detail, field names, pre-authentication negotiation and encrypted-timestamp structure. Hashcat's [mode 19900 module](https://github.com/hashcat/hashcat/blob/master/src/modules/module_19900.c) verifies record classification and distinguishes explicit-salt support in current source from older formats. No assertion was made that the historical Hashcat 6.2.6 accepts every current layout.

Certipy's [upstream ESC3 documentation](https://github.com/ly4k/Certipy/wiki/06-%E2%80%90-Privilege-Escalation) supports the two-template and issuance/authentication distinctions. Microsoft's [certificate name-flag rules](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/openspecs/windows_protocols/ms-wcce/a1f27ffb-7f74-4fa1-8841-7cde4ba0bcfe) and [subject-name flag definitions](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/desktop/api/CertEnroll/ne-certenroll-x509certificatetemplatesubjectnameflag) identify the directory email requirement and when supplied subject information is ignored. These references support evidence collection and interpretation, not new exploit execution.

**Relocated earlier history.** Certipy's previous footer recorded documentation review on 2026-10-09, Linux execution untested, and Authority pp. 9â€“13. Its find CLI, implementation and Microsoft mapping-enforcement links remain in the runtime skill. The former case-specific v4.8.2/2023 wording was replaced with operational version/configuration conditions.

**Checks actually run.** All four focused validators passed. Repository checks passed for **149 packages, 188 Markdown files, 236 local links and 336 Bash blocks**, syntax only. Catalogue membership matches all 149 folders. Final documentation/link checks and `git diff --check` passed in the explicit repository context; existing CRLF normalization warnings are informational. No checker or packaging implementation changed.

TShark, Hashcat, Certipy and impacket-GetADUsers were not found on PATH, and the usual Windows Wireshark executable path was absent. No CLI fixture, source capture analysis, hash conversion/recovery, LDAP/AD CS connection, Linux execution or worker evaluation occurred. All new command behavior remains documentation/source-reviewed and shell-syntax-checked. The PDF text, all renders, before snapshots and structural results remain in ignored work. Deliberately deferred material: archive-filter bypass and PHP shell, password spraying/reuse, on-behalf-of certificate requests and authentication, volume ACL modification, DLL placement/execution, protection bypass, Windows privilege exploitation and IFM acquisition. Extraction yielded 11,396 characters with no warnings. Source: Hack The Box, *Certified*, prepared by kavigihan, machine author ruycr4ft, cover dated 11 March 2025, document D25.100.327. SHA-256: `c385d7a00eff97cc64d94505cc45be621f5dfd59999f273254f9b2c13e8dee7f`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `impacket-dacledit` | 3â€“4 motivate selected-object DACL inspection, exact trustee/context interpretation, inheritance scope and backup limits. The read/local-backup commands are upstream-verified adaptations; the source demonstrates a DACL write. No directory-write recipe was added. |
| Enriched `bloodhound-python` | 3, 5â€“6 distinguish an owned annotation, graph rights, current object permissions and actual remote-session evidence. Adds ownership/DACL/membership distinctions and the meaning of CanPSRemote. Earlier footer history was relocated here. |
| Enriched `certipy` | 7â€“8 correct the source's claim that ESC9 permits UPN changes, separate template and directory rights, qualify current strong-mapping requirements and distinguish key trust from CA-issued certificates. |

README catalogue entries were updated. The new package contains only its own SKILL.md and is independently usable. Existing nmap, Kerberos cache, netexec and evil-winrm guidance already covers the related scan/authentication tasks; no duplicate examples were added.

**Page coverage and source qualifications.** Page 1 introduces an assumed-breach AD scenario with a supplied low-privilege credential; those case credentials were not reused. Page 2 uses an initial default-port Nmap scan followed by selected-port detail. It is not evidence that all TCP ports were covered. The BloodHound.py command requests All collection and contacts computers; the historical Neo4j setup is specific to the receiving generation, not a universal CE installation procedure.

Page 3 shows a graph from a user through a group to a service account and a computer. The owned marker is an operator annotation. The source misspells the username in prose and calls the graph relationship `CanPSRenote`; the meaningful edge is `CanPSRemote`, which is not an AD attribute. A WriteOwner edge, an owner change, DACL modification and group-membership modification are separate claims and operations. The enriched skill also qualifies implicit owner control against current owner-rights conditions instead of assuming ownership always grants unrestricted access.

Page 4 shows an owner change, an Impacket development-build DACL write with inheritance flags, group membership modification, a new key credential and a PKINIT exchange. The DACL backup is created after the owner change and before later membership/key-credential changes. It is therefore not a complete rollback record for the chain. Inheritance follows the directory tree, not the group's membership links. The tool's generic adminCount warning should not replace an inspection of actual inheritance protection and current object context. No owner, ACL, membership or key-credential write was executed here.

Page 5 uses a cache and the PKINIT AS reply key for a further Kerberos exchange, then shows a WinRM identity and another graph. A saved PFX, a TGT cache, PAC credential recovery and a remote session are distinct evidence stages. The AS reply key is not a general account-password key or proof of offline certificate conversion. The graph's remote-access hypothesis is separate from the later whoami result. Page 6 repeats key-credential registration/PKINIT for another account after a GenericAll graph finding; broad modeled rights still require the specific attribute, account policy and KDC prerequisites. The PDF does not show cleanup of the key credentials or group changes.

Page 7 enumerates AD CS through directory data and then inspects a certificate template with Certipy v4.8.2. An enrollment-server directory object does not establish live endpoint health or successful issuance; the abbreviated CA name in one excerpt differs from the complete name later. The source's explanation that ESC9 lets the operator modify a UPN conflates independent directory write access with a template's omission of the SID security extension. The runtime addition separates these facts and leaves missing CA policy unresolved after directory-only collection.

Page 8 shows explicit UPN modification, certificate issuance, UPN restoration and later authentication. This is a historical mapping outcome, not proof that the same UPN-only certificate path works against current patched DCs. Current strong mappings and combined CA conditions must be assessed separately; no blanket claim was made that ESC9 is universally exploitable or universally fixed. Restoring a UPN does not revoke the certificate or undo earlier owner, ACL, membership and key-credential changes. Page 9 shows an Administrator remote-session identity, which documents the source's final observation without proving present access. No certificate request, authentication or remote command was executed.

**Primary verification, reviewed 2026-10-10.** Impacket's [dacledit source](https://github.com/fortra/impacket/blob/master/examples/dacledit.py) verifies the target/principal selectors, cache and LDAPS flags, read/local-backup actions, DACL-only SD control, backup filename fallback and restore scope. Important edge cases include substring matching against rendered trustee text, unsupported ACE parsing and error paths that can leave a zero exit status. The direct upstream source snapshot contains 779 lines and SHA-256 `93cad2d3dfbdb6db9db161b44d817ee6485cd5c031136fcaa168d38d8bdb6faa`; it is saved in ignored work. The web index returned an older 723-line rendering; the relevant flags and behavior were also checked in the direct snapshot. The syntax is conditioned on installed help rather than claiming every packaged release supports it.

Microsoft's [AD inheritance rules](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/ad/access-control-inheritance) support the effective/inherit-only and container-tree distinctions. [Implicit-owner restrictions](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/openspecs/windows_protocols/ms-adts/fb7c101d-ec8b-4fbf-bca8-7d7c2d747d0c) and [OWNER RIGHTS SID definitions](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-server/identity/ad-ds/manage/understand-security-identifiers) qualify the broader ownership statement in SpecterOps' [WriteOwner explanation](https://bloodhound.specterops.io/resources/edges/write-owner). SpecterOps' [CanPSRemote definition](https://bloodhound.specterops.io/resources/edges/can-ps-remote) and Microsoft's [remoting requirements](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/powershell/module/microsoft.powershell.core/about/about_remote_requirements) support separating the graph edge from endpoint reachability, session permissions and successful access.

Certipy's [ESC9 documentation](https://github.com/ly4k/Certipy/wiki/06-%E2%80%90-Privilege-Escalation) distinguishes template flags, enrollment conditions and separate directory/CA prerequisites. Microsoft's [KB5014754](https://support.microsoft.com/en-us/servicing/os/windows-server/2022/05/kb5014754-certificate-based-authentication-changes-on-windows-domain-controllers) records the enforcement transition and retirement of the compatibility-mode switch with the September 2025 updates; runtime guidance requires the actual installed update/mapping state rather than a calendar assumption. The [pyWhisker README](https://github.com/ShutdownRepo/pywhisker) identifies the key-credential write and locally generated certificate/key, while [PKINITtools](https://github.com/dirkjanm/PKINITtools) documents the live TGT and subsequent U2U/PAC exchange. These sources support the distinctions; no additional credential-manipulation package was created.

**Relocated earlier history.** BloodHound.py's former footer recorded documentation review on 2026-10-09, Linux execution untested and Administrator pp. 3â€“4. Its upstream generation guidance, CE CLI and legacy CLI links remain in the runtime skill. The history was moved here without claiming a new runtime test.

**Checks actually run.** Three focused skill validators passed. Repository checks passed for **150 packages, 189 Markdown files, 237 local links and 338 Bash blocks**, syntax only. Catalogue membership matches all 150 folders. A final prose qualification about trustee substring matching was revalidated; it changed no command blocks. Final documentation/link checks and `git diff --check` passed in the explicit repository context. No packaging/checker implementation changed.

Impacket dacledit, Certipy and both BloodHound.py executable names were absent from PATH. No affected CLI fixture, LDAP/CA/KDC connection, credential recovery, target authentication, Linux execution or worker evaluation occurred. New commands are documentation/source-reviewed and shell-syntax-checked only. All PDF text/renders, source snapshot, before state, review diff and check results remain in ignored work. Deliberately deferred material: ownership/ACL/group writes, shadow-credential registration and recovery, UPN manipulation, certificate enrollment/authentication and privileged WinRM access.
